#3014. Stock Market Today, Nov. 10: AMD Rises on Plan to Invest $2.5 Billion in Anthropic

Chip designer AMD gained as investors weighed its AI deal push, with trading well above normal, today, Nov. 10, 2026.

Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +1.30%), a data-center and PC chip designer, closed at $515.21, up 1.30%. Tuesday's gains followed a premarket upgrade and fresh AI-chip deal chatter, and investors are watching data-center demand and AI spending next.

Trading volume reached 46.7 million shares, coming in about 102% above its three-month average of 23.2 million shares.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.38%) closed at 7,521, down 0.38%, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -0.74%) ended at 25,066, down 0.74%. Among semiconductors, x86 CPUs, GPUs, AI accelerators, and data-center/server chip stocks, Nvidia (NVDA -5.47%) fell 5.47% to $168.89 and Intel (INTC -2.09%) dropped 2.09% to $58.13, while AMD outperformed both chip rivals.

AMD stock rose today after artificial intelligence giant Anthropic disclosed in an amended IPO filing on Nov. 10 that AMD would be part of a group involved in a $26 billion private placement alongside some of the world's biggest technology companies. AMD plans to invest $2.5 billion worth of non-voting Class N common stock in Anthropic, which counts toward an earlier commitment of up to $5 billion linked to chip deliveries.

AMD shares have been on a run this week after its chief rival, Nvidia, disclosed an investigation into its CEO, Jensen Huang, over his relationship with an alleged Chinese spy. Wall Street's fears that Huang could be forced to step down caused investors to shift into AMD stock.

This added to analyst upgrades of the company's shares, as its business continues to boom. AMD's Nov. 3 earnings results showed record quarterly revenue of $13.4 billion and Q4 revenue guidance of about $17 billion, which kept AI-driven growth in focus.

I have long been an admirer of AMD's CEO, Lisa Su, which is one of the factors behind my buying shares. The company's performance demonstrates her skill at leading the semiconductor giant as it capitalizes on massive AI demand.

#3012. CoreWeave Delivers Another Blowout Quarter. Is It Enough To Save the AI Trade?

CoreWeave topped estimates, but balance sheet worries linger.

CoreWeave(CRWV -0.51%), the largest of the neoclouds, tumbled over the last month, dragging down the AI sector with it, after a social media post showed that its bond yields had reached as high as 15%. That observation set off another round of concerns that the AI build-out is unsustainable, and the issue has only been magnified since Democrats swept the midterm elections, in part by campaigning on increased regulation for data centers.

Against that backdrop, CoreWeave reported third-quarter results Tuesday afternoon, and the company made it clear that demand for its services, which essentially consist of renting AI compute, continues to skyrocket.

Revenue in the quarter jumped 168% to $3.66 billion, ahead of the analyst consensus at $3.54 billion. CoreWeave's revenue backlog improved from $104 billion at the end of the second quarter to $132.5 billion at the end of the third, another sign of strong demand.

Its generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP) operating margin also improved from 4% to 6% as GAAP operating income jumped from $52 million to $207 million.

However, CoreWeave has taken on tens of billions in debt to fund its rapid data center expansion and chip purchases, which have continued to weigh on the bottom line. Interest expense jumped from $310 million in the quarter a year ago to $846 million, and it finished the quarter with a GAAP net loss of $709 million, or $1.27 per share. That compared with expectations of a loss of $1.21 per share. On an adjusted basis, its net loss expanded from $41 million to $529 million, but improved slightly on a sequential basis.

On a free cash flow basis, CoreWeave lost $7.7 billion in the quarter, and its debt jumped to $47 billion.

Investors mostly shrugged off the earnings report, with the stock gaining modestly in after-hours trading, up 2% before its earnings call at 5 p.m. ET.

The report didn't seem to change many opinions on the stock, and that's understandable. CoreWeave reported another round of soaring revenue growth, but investors expected that, and it didn't show it was turning the corner on its balance sheet risk yet.

However, management said it would not use the unsecured debt market at current interest rates, which offers some encouragement to investors worried about its rising bond yields.

CoreWeave expects interest expense to continue to rise into the fourth quarter, reaching $1.05 billion-$1.15 billion on $4.7 billion-$5 billion in revenue as it raised its full-year revenue forecast to $13 billion-$13.3 billion. The fourth-quarter forecast implies revenue more than tripling.

On the earnings call, CEO Michael Intrator argued that prices for older GPUs had been resilient and were in fact rising, even for Nvidia chips that are three generations old.

That should offer some reassurance to investors, as should the company's plan to collect $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits in the fourth quarter.

Still, the Fed is expected to raise interest rates at its next meeting in December after a hot Consumer Price Index report. CoreWeave has a current ratio below 0.5, and its interest expense is more than 20% of its revenue.

Its revenue growth is impressive, and its prospects are bright, but investors are understandably wary given current interest rates.

Overall, CoreWeave is making prudent decisions, and operating leverage is improving. With time, the stock should recover its recent losses if it remains on this path.

For the AI sector more broadly, from CoreWeave’s perspective, demand looks set to outstrip supply for years to come, and the company believes it has several levers to pull to fund the build-out. That should reassure any fears about a string of defaults crushing the industry.

#3008. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3005. Mike Brown’s lineup experiments are paying dividends for Knicks

At this stage in the season, winning isn't enough for the Knicks.

The champions care more about how they win.

"Obviously, we want to play well, we want to win, but we also want to continue to grow as a team and try not to plateau," Jalen Brunson said after practice Monday. "I think the way we do that is to continue to try to see how we can win games differently."

New York Knicks head coach Mike Brown directs his team against the Cleveland Cavaliers in Cleveland, Saturday, Nov. 7, 2026. AP Photo/Phil Long

That sentiment trickles down from head coach Mike Brown. Constant experimentation is a core staple to his philosophy. It feels like there's a surprise in the box score every game.

His players believe that brings the best out of them.

"It's kind of a freeing thing, playing that way, knowing that you don't have to really be looking over your shoulder or thinking that one thing is gonna ruin whatever," Landry Shamet said Monday. "And I think that he knows that we have that makeup as a group, too, that we're all on the same page in wanting the best for each other — the next man up kind of mentality. It's from top to bottom, and it's a good environment to have."

Brown used an 11-man rotation for Saturday's 109-97 win in Cleveland. That wasn't the result of garbage-time minutes, as all 11 saw the court in the first half. It was clear Brown wanted to get guys involved who are usually out of the rotation, such as Mohamed Diawara and Dalton Knecht.

It represents the biggest difference between Brown and predecessor Tom Thibodeau, who established a rotation and stuck strictly to it unless forced to change. Thibodeau prioritized maximizing his chances of winning every single night. Brown looks at the regular season more like a canvas to try new lineups, rotations and systems so that, by the end of the year, he has more available in his toolkit.

That process might be ugly at times, but it's designed to deliver long-term results.

Brown credits it as a big reason for their championship last season. The likes of Shamet and Jordan Clarkson spent time in and out of the rotation all year but stepped up in the postseason when their number was called. The shift to an offense built around Karl-Anthony Towns as a hub was only possible because they toyed with it during the regular season.

Landry Shamet #44 of the New York Knicks dribbles against the Cleveland Cavaliers on Saturday, Nov. 7, 2026, at Rocket Arena in Cleveland. NBAE via Getty Images

"It builds trust," Brown said Monday, "because it's like, 'Hey, they trust me to go out there and play right now, even though I might not have played in two or three games.' It helps with connectivity because guys feel more part of it, because they're getting an opportunity. It helps with everybody's readiness because the reality of it is, if a guy is sitting on the bench and he's been there game after game after game, which happens sometimes — I'm not saying that will never happen because it's gonna happen sometimes — then he may lose a little interest and relax a little bit because it's like, 'Hey, I'm not going in.' But then, if you call their number every once in a while, throw them in a game, now they know, 'Oh, I gotta be ready.' "

Even Jaden Akins, signed to a two-way deal like Knecht, has seen a little action beyond garbage time.

"If he can go in the game during some real moments," Brown said, "anybody can."

It's different from Thibodeau's final season, when he inserted Shamet and Delon Wright into the rotation out of desperation in the Eastern Conference final, having hardly used them previously.

Under Brown, there is a track record on which to fall back for everyone on the team, even if they go long stretches out of the rotation.

"It builds a lot, when you're talking about building a team," Brown said. "And then at the end of the day, your guys get used to it, so come playoff time, if you try something new or different when it comes to the lineups, it's not like, 'Oh my God, what's going on? We've never done this.' Nah, we've done it."

There is the flip side, though. Playing time and roles constantly are changing. Jose Alvarado started the year as the backup point guard but now finds himself behind Tyler Kolek in the pecking order after missing time due to an ankle injury. There is constant fluctuation with the bench players in how much time each plays. That could cause problems.

"That's just kind of the makeup of our group, and that's special," Shamet said. "Typically, you go to a lot of NBA locker rooms, there's some ulterior motives and personal agendas — which is part of why we're all here, is some ego, of course. Everybody is a good player at this level and is striving to get to something or be somewhere. But very rarely does that take a backseat to just truly buying into one another, wanting the best because, ultimately, good things come from that for everyone. It's special. It's really special."

It can perhaps deliver more special results.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2999. Player Grades: Dallas gets third win in a row with 125-122 victory over the Pacers

The Dallas Mavericks (7-4) won a 125-122 thriller over the Indiana Pacers (3-8) on Monday night. Kyrie Irving and Cooper Flagg were dominant, combining for 78 points, while Caleb Martin made the defensive play of the game to seal the deal. On the second night of a back-to-back, the Mavericks found themselves trailing for much of the first half but worked their way back, determined to not have a letdown after a convincing win over the Brookly Nets just 24 hours prior. With the victory, the Mavericks extend their winning streak to three games.

We try to be discerning with a perfect grade, especially for a Hall of Famer who has plenty of exceptional games on his resume. Tonight Irving gets the bump despite no rebounds, steals or blocks. He was simply too dominant offensively not to. He connected on 11-of-18 from the floor and 7-of-10 from downtown. He hit 6-of-7 free throws while he was at it. Helping get him over the top was a game-high +29 plus/minus. Even with a few goose eggs in the stat line, Irving was playing on another level Monday night and did a lot in the fact of tough on-ball defense to keep the Mavs connected and ultimately able to pull away.

Little needs to be said when the box score reads as it does. Flagg hit 16-of-25 shots in a 40-point double-double and was absolutely huge down the stretch, hitting big shots in the fourth quarter in a refuse-to-lose performance. Just dominant while leaving a couple rough shooting nights as a distant memory.

The conundrum of Martin persists. A starter with limited minutes and little tangible production makes the defensive play of the night to seal the victory. We've yet to determine how best to grade him, but suffice it to say tipping the inbounds pass away from Paskal Siakam to prevent the Pacers from even attempting a shot on the final possession gives his grade a boost.

Washington gets the "5" based on some intangibles. His 4-of-12 shooting left a bit to be desired (though one of his two made three-pointers came at a critical time) and he was only 3-of-6 from the charity stripe. Of the three misses, two of them came in the waning moments where even one make would have effectively ended the game. Worse still, he probably could have gotten the ball to Irving before he was fouled, allowing the better free throw shooter to get the opportunity at the line. He rebounded well and had a few defensive efforts that don't hit the box score and the fact the outcome was a win finds us giving him a bit of a bump on his grade.

Gafford was foul-happy, playing himself off the court early on. He showed better control during the latter part of the game, but having him on the floor consistently to match up with Ivica Zubac would have helped along the way. He was solid in the limited minutes and managed to show some passing ability but it was a somewhat mundane night.

Aldama was 4-of-9 from the floor, but only 1-of-5 from downtown. Even with the longball not falling, he did a nice job maximizing his limited minutes. He had a soft touch around the rim and put himself in the right spots – especially impressive given he just returned from injury had had no prior reps with his teammates before just a couple games ago.

This was a hard-fought game that came down to the final possession. Dallas was on the second night of a back-to-back, yet got incredible offensive performances from both Flagg and Irving. The team plays together and fights hard on every possession. That isn't to say every possession is pretty, but Dallas continues to stay disciplined and tied together. On Monday night, that cohesion resulted in a great clutch win.

I invite you to follow me @_80MPH on X, and check back often at Mavs Moneyball for all the latest on the Dallas Mavericks.

#2993. Player grades: Warriors vs. Pelicans

Well that was fun. The Golden State Warriors made NBA history on Monday night, by becoming the first team to start 0-3 and follow it up with an eight-game winning streak. And they certainly did it in emphatic style, as they positively crushed the New Orleans Pelicans, ending their home-stand with a 138-87 victory that was every bit as lopsided as the score would suggest.

Needless to say, the grades are going to be good for this one. As always, my grades are based on my personal expectations for each player, with a "B" grade representing that player's average performance.

Note: True-shooting percentage (TS) is a scoring efficiency metric that accounts for threes and free throws. Entering Monday's games, league-average TS was 57.1%.

21 minutes, 3 points, 7 rebounds, 4 assists, 1 block, 3 fouls, 0-for-3 shooting, 0-for-1 threes, 3-for-4 free throws, 31.5% TS, +21

You gotta love Gui. Even in a game where the Warriors were pouring in points faster than a bartender pours shots, Santos was content to just help out doing the little things. He attacked the glass and did a swell job of keeping the ball moving. He repeatedly found the open man and ended up with four dimes and zero turnovers. He was a big part of the strong defensive performance.

He just does all the little things, and even though it doesn't always come with offense, you can happily live with that on this team.

27 minutes, 23 points, 7 rebounds, 4 assists, 1 steal, 2 turnovers, 3 fouls, 8-for-10 shooting, 4-for-4 threes, 3-for-4 free throws, 97.8% TS, +34

Yeah. I think we have a star on our hands, friends. I don't think Lendeborg is good, or even really good, or even has the potential to be a star. I think he is a star, point blank. It's only been 11 games but that's abundantly clear.

There was some Steph Curry in Yax's game on Monday, in that he seemed to relish the opportunity to rip a team's soul out. So often you see rookies get sloppy when playing in a blowout. At best they hog the ball and put on their own show; at worst, they check out because the outcome is determined.

But stars like Curry lock in. They want the win to be as lopsided as possible. They want to crush the other team's spirit and make it clear that they're not in the same league (except, you know … in the literal sense). And they want to make sure that they're the one who does the damage.

Lendeborg, it seems, has that gene. He didn't try to do to much, even when the Dubs led by 20, or 30, or 40, or 50. There were no reckless heat checks or audacious dunk attempts. Instead, he just locked in like it was a tied game, and went after the Pelicans in every part of the game.

21 minutes, 2 points, 3 rebounds, 6 assists, 1 steal, 2 turnovers, 1 foul, 1-for-1 shooting, 100.0% TS, +32

Shot check! Green is up to 27 field goal attempts in nine games. There was some offseason chatter that Steve Kerr wanted Green to shoot more (specifically threes), and then it turned out the Warriors offense was good enough that Dray could be super selective, and focus on the three things he does brilliantly on that end of the court: passing the ball, setting screens, and making sure that everything runs through Curry.

Green has really been an offensive asset this year, which is a testament to his patience and playmaking, but also to the players around him. Having four shooters on the court at almost all times certainly helps there as well.

Defensively, Dray played well, though we were robbed of getting to see him defend Zion Williamson, as the Pelicans' star got the night off. Oh well; they'll meet again in a week.

Grade: APost-game bonus: Tied for the team lead in assists.

26 minutes, 25 points, 2 rebounds, 4 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 10-for-19 shooting, 5-for-11 threes, 65.8% TS, +36

Lest you get too caught up in the Yaxel hype, Podziemski is here to remind you that there's more than one early-20s Warrior with a fun European name who just might be a star. This was Podz at his very best. He was all over the court in a blur of chaos, on both ends of the court. He attacked on offense, while also popping out time and time again, and hiding behind screens, to show off his jumper … which looks a lot better this year (he's 25-for-62 from deep so far this year).

In a fast and frantic game in which Podziemski seemed to have the ball in his hands a lot, he went a whole game with nary a turnover nor a foul.

It really feels like his offense is far more in control this year. He's always had creativity and unique explosiveness, but this year he's harnessed it far better than in year's past. Can you remember a single shot he's taken this season that has made you scratch your head or yell at the TV (except in a positive way)? I can't. He's just unbelievably in control, and fully coming into his own.

Grade: A+Post-game bonus: Tied for the team lead in points; best plus/minus on the team.

21 minutes, 25 points, 5 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 turnover, 6-for-10 shooting, 4-for-7 threes, 9-for-9 free throws, 89.5% TS, +32

Here's a fun question: who's enjoying the Lendeborg and Podziemski breakouts more, Curry or Kerr? Steph is clearly having the time of his life playing with these invigorating youngsters. And more importantly, you can see the relief in not having to carry the team on his shoulders night in and night out, and possession in and possession out.

The way that Curry is picking his spots is honestly a little reminiscent of when Klay Thompson and Kevin Durant were on the team. Curry knows he can step into his easier roles — playing the distributing point guard, or just using his gravity to space the defense out — and the offense will still function well. And then, when the time comes — if the team's in trouble, or he wants to put a nail in the coffin, or he just wants to have a little fun — he goes into supersduperduperstar mode.

He's averaging more than a point per minute with a true-shooting percentage well into the 70s. And it looks sustainable because he's not having to kill himself out there. I should give him an A+ but i can't justify a perfect grade for someone I know can — and will — pop off for 50.

Grade: APost-game bonus: Tied for the team lead in points.

26 minutes, 16 points, 4 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 1 turnover, 3 fouls, 5-for-11 shooting, 4-for-8 threes, 2-for-2 free throws, 67.3% TS, +20

Melton had a little bit of a slow start to the season, but new he is absolutely picking it up. He's firing on all cylinders right now, and just making everything work. He has so much veteran savvy and poise … he plays with a little bit of that old man game where everything is slow, but then he displays these little bursts when they're needed, to turn a rebound into a transition opportunity, or cut to the hoop, or strip a driving opponent. There's more than a little bit of Andre Iguodala in his game, that's for sure.

Like Curry, Melton has really been excelling at picking his spots this year, and that goes for both offense and defense. In this one, he came in with the bench unit and helped turn a solid lead into a blowout, and he never took his foot off the gas.

15 minutes, 5 points, 2 rebounds, 6 assists, 2 steals, 3 blocks, 2 fouls, 2-for-5 shooting, 1-for-3 threes, 50.0% TS, +29

He had five stocks in 15 minutes. He had six assists — as a center — in 15 minutes. It's just comical how frequently he makes the right play.

I said when the Warriors signed him before last year that he was the perfect player for Kerr, and I think that dramatically undersold it.

Grade: A+Post-game bonus: Tied for the team lead in assists.

Unfortunately, he aggravated his hamstring in the first quarter, and was ruled out for the rest of the game. Hopefully he won't be sidelined for too long.

#2991. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2988. Fantasy Basketball Trends: Dyson Daniels' pickpocketing ways paying off early

Three weeks into the 2026-27 NBA season, several veterans are defying expectations, and one new-look contender is finding its footing. Stephen Curry has turned back the clock, Dyson Daniels is doing stuff that hasn't been done in decades and the Heat have won four of six after a winless start. Klay Thompson has caught fire, Davion Mitchell ranks fourth in the league in assists and Giannis Antetokounmpo is back to his MVP ways, while Andrew Wiggins' slump has opened the door for Pelle Larsson.

This breakdown covers which hot starts are sustainable, which slumps are buy-low opportunities and who deserves a spot in your 2026-27 fantasy basketball lineup.

Curry is defying Father Time with an impressive start to his 18th season. The 38-year-old is averaging 30.6 points per game, which would lead the league if not for Luka Dončić's incredible run. Nonetheless, Curry is not only scoring with fluidity, but he is also doing so with incredible efficiency, shooting 50.3% from the field, including 45.7% from deep. Of course, he also continues to thrive from the foul line, hitting a magnificent 98.7% of his free throws. Moreover, he is taking care of the ball, boasting an assist-to-turnover ratio of 2.4, which would rank as the second-highest of his career. His current pace would have him on track for one of his best seasons yet, which would be an incredible feat at this stage.

He also recently passed LeBron James for the most 40-point games by a player age 35 or older, recording his 16th such game and second of the season. Additionally, in the same outing, he broke his own record as the oldest point guard to drop 40 points, five rebounds and five assists in a game.

Daniels has been an absolute star to start the season, becoming the first player since Scottie Pippen in 1994 to total over 100 points, 75 rebounds, 50 assists and 30 steals through his first 11 games of the season. He is averaging 9.8 points, 7.1 rebounds, 5.8 assists and 3.0 steals through 12 total games. He has posted three outings with at least five swipes and leads the league in steals per game. He also handles the ball with poise, recording 4.6 assists per turnover, an incredible ratio that ranks as his career best so far.

He has already developed a reputation as a pickpocket, but it's impressive to witness his increasing ability to contribute in other areas. He's averaging a career-best mark in rebounds per game and is on track to average over five assists a night for the second straight season. On the other hand, his offensive versatility remains a bit limited, as he has knocked down only two 3-pointers this season despite averaging 1.3 attempts per game. His free-throw shooting also continues to lag, as he's hitting just 57.1% from the line.

A four-game losing streak to start the season had even the most forgiving analysts questioning what was going on with the Heat. It was fair to expect some growing pains with such drastic changes to the lineup in the offseason, including having to integrate a high-usage player like Giannis Antetokounmpo and parting ways with a couple of good playmakers in Tyler Herro and Jaime Jaquez Jr. The rough start was surely tough to swallow for everyone involved, especially since it raised questions about whether the new dynamic between Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo would ever work.

However, the squad has since gone 4-2, providing a bit of hope for better things to come.

The Heat are attempting 39.7 3-pointers per game, which ranks 12th most in the league, but they are hitting just 34.0% from deep, which ranks 10th lowest. Despite having two of the more dominant interior players in the game, they do not rank inside the top 10 in terms of free throws (13th) or points in the paint (11th) per game. Additionally, they are struggling on the glass, with just 42.1 rebounds per game, which is the ninth-lowest mark in the league. Meanwhile, on the defensive end, they are giving up the league's fifth-most points (121.1) per game, including the most 3-pointers (16.4).

Now, for a look at some of the trends among the individual starters, beginning on a positive note with the improved play of Klay Thompson. Thompson averaged a measly 7.8 points on 31.7% shooting through the first five games of the season but has picked up his play drastically since then. The 14-year vet is averaging 21.6 points on 52.8% shooting, including a blistering 50.0% from deep over the last five games, and he knocked down a game-winner to help spark the squad's anticipated turnaround.

Davion Mitchell has been another bright spot, averaging 16.2 points on 50.4% shooting, along with 8.5 assists per game, which ranks fourth most in the league. He has already racked up double-digit assists on six occasions, a mark he reached just nine times last season. Add in his average of 1.2 steals per game and the sixth-year guard is looking like one of the most efficient and productive point guards through the first few weeks of the season.

Bam Adebayo drew a lot of criticism after two single-digit scoring performances through the first four games, but he has since found his groove, averaging 23.0 points, 9.2 rebounds, 3.0 assists and 2.2 steals over the last six outings. He is shooting 53.0% from the field through 10 games and is averaging just 3.3 3-point attempts per game, down from a career-high 5.5 last season.

Unfortunately, Andrew Wiggins is in the midst of one of the worst stretches of his career, averaging just 8.3 points on 40.2% shooting, 3.1 rebounds and 1.6 assists in 26.4 minutes per game. He is also struggling to get to the foul line, with just six free throws attempted through 10 games. He's having a hard time finding his place in the new offense, which could lead Erik Spoelstra to make Pelle Larsson a full-time starter, as Larsson is averaging 11.1 points on 45.7% shooting in 29.4 minutes per game.

Lastly, Antetokounmpo has been fulfilling his side of the bargain, averaging 27.5 points, 12.8 rebounds, 5.3 assists, 1.0 steals and 1.1 blocks through 10 games. He finished with fewer than 20 points just once in that stretch and already has eight double-doubles, including two triple-doubles. He is averaging 2.5 3-point attempts per game, which is his most since 2022-23, but he is hitting only 20.0% from deep. He also continues to show unimpressive free-throw efficiency, shooting 62.2% from the stripe.

#2985. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#2982. Yaxel Lendeborg among Warriors Hall of Famers in scoring through first 11 games

Yaxel Lendeborg among Warriors Hall of Famers in scoring through first 11 games originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg's hot start to his NBA career puts him in Hall-of-Fame-level company.

Lendeborg ranks third in scoring among rookies through their first 11 games in Warriors franchise history with 235 points. Hall of Famers Wilt Chamberlain and Rick Barry scored 395 and 237 points, respectively, through their first 11 career games with the franchise.

Of the other nine players on the top 10 list, seven of those former Warriors are in the Naismith Basketball Hall of Fame.

Golden State desperately needed a secondary scorer behind Steph Curry while Jimmy Butler recovers from injury, and Lendeborg has answered the call. The Warriors' lottery pick in the 2026 NBA Draft ranks second on the team in scoring with 21.4 points per game.

His scoring has been consistent, with the rookie recording at least 12 points in all but one game. Lendeborg has scored at least 23 points in six of his 11 career games, highlighted by a 37-point performance in a win against the Los Angeles Clippers.

Those points have come in big moments as well, particularly in Saturday's overtime win over the Orlando Magic, in which Lendeborg led all scorers in the extra period with seven points.

Warriors coach Steve Kerr has been impressed with the rookie's poise so early into his career.

"He's learning something new every game – probably multiple things a game," Kerr told reporters Saturday after the overtime win. "He's picking it up with such poise and grace. He gets frustrated, but he moves on and just plays.

"The way he closed the game in the fourth quarter and overtime looked like someone who has been around for a while – not a guy who has played 10 NBA games."

Lendeborg is a key reason for the Warriors' eight-game winning streak, the team's longest since the 2021-22 championship season. Golden State and its rookie scoring machine will look to extend its streak on the road Wednesday against the Houston Rockets.

#2978. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2976. Drama takes center stage in Celtics-Sixers, but bigger story is what comes next

Drama takes center stage in Celtics-Sixers, but bigger story is what comes next originally appeared on NBC Sports Boston

The NBA is fueled by drama the way New Englanders are fueled by Dunkin. So the hype surrounding Jaylen Brown's first game against his former team was always going to be largely over-caffeinated. His comments in the ramp up to Tuesday's showdown in Philadelphia only added a shot of espresso to the order.

But the last thing this drama needs is another food reference. Lamb chops, gravy, coffee or otherwise.

The speculation and intrigue surrounding Brown's relationship with former teammate Jayson Tatum isn't new but got a jolt in the fallout from the jaw-dropping summer swap. It's probably best the two have to share the same 94 feet of hardwood for a couple hours on Tuesday night in Philadelphia.

Celtics-Sixers will undeniably be must-see TV. But sure to be overshadowed by the breathless takes about Tatum and Brown's working relationship — nearly a full decade of winning, mind you, that included two Finals appearance and one championship banner — and all their interactions — of maybe lack thereof — on Tuesday night will cloud the bigger question here.

Did the Celtics make the right decision in moving on from Brown?

That answer could take years to be truly revealed. And in this social-media era of bite-sized content consumption, we don't have that sort of patience. The Celtics clearly believed that moving on from Brown offered the best pathway back to building a sustained title contender. Complicating matters, Brown's new team became overstocked with the sort of top-end talent that immediately made the Sixers legitimate title contenders if they could handle all the personalities.

Tuesday night delivers an early season matchup between, to this point, the two top teams in the Eastern Conference. And yet maybe it's merely a checkpoint in a bigger race between two rivals chasing the same lofty goal.

Tatum and Brown, of course, are inextricably intertwined, especially now playing for rival franchises. Their legacies are unlikely to be altered by a single Tuesday in November, though certainly those 48 minutes of basketball will provide an elevated entertainment experience.

Bragging rights will ultimately fall to the player who proves they can win without the other.

BOSTON, MASSACHUSETTS – NOVEMBER 04: Paul George of the Boston Celtics celebrates after scoring against the Milwaukee Bucks during the second half at TD Garden on Nov. 4, 2026 in Boston, Massachusetts.

We yearn to firmly declare winners and losers from the very moment a Shams alert reaches our phones. Back in July, the Celtics lost, decidedly, in the court of public opinion when the world learned that Boston traded Brown to the Sixers for a package highlighted by 36-year-old Paul George and a pair of future first-round draft picks.

It's still way too early to make any wholesale declarations, but both sides should feel pretty good about where they stand through 10 games of the 2026-27 season.

George has been better than most could have anticipated after two injury riddled seasons with the Sixers. He immediately declared this was Tatum's team and suggested that he was here to maximize his talents. George has thrived on both sides of the court, looking like an All-Defense candidate while lingering among the league-leaders in deflections and steals. He's even turned back the clock and showcased the offensive talents that made him a nine-time All-Star. His usage rate is lofty (27.4), but Tatum (35.6) is the clear-cut focal point when he's on the court.

The Celtics lead the league in net rating (+11.6), aided by a softer schedule out of the gates. Boston needs to prove it can hang with the best teams in the league — fumbling away a big lead on opening night in Detroit and running in mud on the second night of a back-to-back in a loss to Houston. The Celtics might not be quite the 65-win team their point differential suggests, but their depth gives them a chance to stack regular-season wins.

Brown's stat line with Philly looks very similar to Tatum's last fully healthy season in Boston. Brown is averaging 22.3 points, 6.7 rebounds and 4.0 assists per game in 10 appearances. His turnovers (2.1 per game) are at a career-best level since his All-Star ascension in 2021. He has topped the Sixers in scoring in each of their last three outings. Philadelphia has utilized its new collection of experienced talent to thrive in late-game situations, posting an 8-0 record in clutch games (score within 5 points, final 5 minutes).

Is success sustainable for both sides? If George stays healthy, he's been a rather ideal runningmate for Tatum. The Celtics have a +11.1 net rating in 162 minutes together this season, but the team's net rating spikes to +24.7 in George's 109 minutes without Tatum. The Sixers have stiff-armed any thoughts that they might endure growing pains with so much talent, though their season will ultimately be judged on postseason success given the amount of talent.

"Optionality" became a Boston buzzword after Stevens championed it in the aftermath of the team's decision to trade Brown. Fans stung by Brown's departure have gone so far as to wear jerseys with "Optionality" on the nameplate around TD Garden.

Stevens knows that even the goodwill built by delivering a title team in 2024 can erode quickly unless the Celtics use their newfound optionality to offer hope of securing Banner 19.

It's not an overstatement to suggest that Stevens must really hit with the two picks acquired in the Brown swap, including a 2028 first-round pick via the Clippers and a 2031 first-round pick via the Sixers. With Tatum and Brown, the Celtics were always in the contention conversation. Stevens must set up the next iteration of a contender with what was acquired for Brown. Because Boston's status right now is a bit murkier.

The most likely scenario is Boston using those picks as trade assets while increasing the talent around Tatum in what is essentially the prime of his career. If the Celtics stay below the tax this season, they will reset pesky repeater penalties, which would allow the team to spend ambitiously next summer. It might not be the full-throttle splurge like the summer of 2023 that delivered Jrue Holiday and Kristaps Porzingis and set into motion the quest for Banner 18 — but it could certainly increase Boston's talent pool and decrease the margin for error that currently exists.

George could be part of that future, maybe at a reduced rate if he were to opt out and extend this summer. Boston could also consider using his salary in any big swing. Again, the theme here is multiple options and pathways.

It seems fair to suggest that, as currently constituted this season, the Celtics are longshots to contend for a title this year. A healthy Tatum, closer to the peak of his powers and further removed from the Achilles injury that sapped much of last season, could elevate that ceiling but the talent and depth across the best teams in the NBA might demand a bit more tinkering around him.

Green may run deep, but patience in these parts does not, and Stevens is well aware of the accelerator that fans will demand he press in the quest for the next banner.

Tough decisions still exist. What does the core of this team look like in that next iteration? Is Derrick White able to overcome the poor shooting of last season and thrive at age 32 and beyond? Can Payton Pritchard, fresh off inking his new big extension, find even a bit more consistency against the best teams? Which of the younger players are surefire pieces of the future roster?

That's why the 2026-27 season still feels important in the information-gathering and development process. Stevens must evaluate his roster in the post-Brown era and decide how best to restock around Tatum.

Feb 28, 2025; Boston, Massachusetts, USA; Boston Celtics guard Jaylen Brown (7) high fives forward Jayson Tatum (0) after the Cleveland Cavaliers called a timeout during the first quarter at TD Garden.

From the very moment Jaylen Brown inked a 5-year, $304 million supermax extension, the biggest contract in NBA history to that point, and with Tatum set to sign his own 5-year, $314 million deal a year later, it felt inevitable the Celtics were heading towards a bit of a reckoning. The apron era ensured it.

Boston got ahead of things and produced Banner 18. They've paid what we'll call the parade tax with painful roster decisions the past two summers while getting their finances in order.

The decision to trade Brown thrust his relationship with Tatum under a harsher microscope. Surely, there must be more than meets the eye, most wondered. It's hardly a secret that they were two very different people but they undeniably motivated and pushed each other as teammates. No one can diminish the success they had, particularly after winning a title. You don't have to be best friends with your co-workers.

Two players making 35% of the cap is simply a tough situation to navigate, at least without sacrificing talent among depth pieces. Two players commanding 35% of possessions might be even more difficult. Tatum's injury added a layer to the decision-making, in part because Brown thrived in the solo role and might understandably yearn to be regarded as equals. Even the possession-maxing Celtics didn't have enough shots and touches for two mega stars.

The Celtics ultimately decided that the best pathway back to competing at the highest level was Tatum using the majority of possessions and figuring out the rest around him. It's fascinating to wonder, if Tatum never gets hurt and Brown never gets the season in the spotlight, would the Celtics have rode out the Jays era a bit longer, trying to squeeze every last drop from the pairing?

Everyone wants a smoking gun, a single moment that changed the relationship and can explain why the Celtics moved on from Brown when they did. The fact that it was Philly on the other end of the trade, and for a package that felt initially underwhelming, added even more layers of complexity for fans to digest.

The business of basketball often forces executives to make uncomfortable decisions. The best general managers are the ones that don't wait too long to rip the Band-Aid and keep their eye on the larger prize, without worrying about the court of public opinion.

You don't need to rewind too far in Celtics history to find the last reunion game that had a little extra drama to it. Kevin Garnett ignoring Ray Allen during Boston's visit to Miami in the 2012 season opener was a rather surreal moment. It took another decade for things to get fully patched up between that Big Three.

Maybe it'll take that long for the Jays to move past whatever feelings exist now. As Brown bluntly told reporters on Sunday: "He's on his side; I'm on my side … Just check the ball up."

Being under the same roof and forced to at least co-exist on the same 94 feet of hardwood might force the two to acknowledge each other. Maybe it starts the healing process, for fans and players alike.

But, ultimately, this is all going to take some time. Eventually everyone will sit back and remember the Jays era for all the winning, including Banner 18. Numbers 0 and 7 might eventually hang in the TD Garden rafters together. Forever.

Tuesday night will be fun. There's a long way to go to sort out the rest.

#2972. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#2969. Celtics vs. Sixers has everything a great NBA rivalry needs…and now we’re adding Tatum and Brown

Sports rivalries rule. I grew up during the early-2000s Red Sox-Yankees battles. I'll never forget swinging from the absolute heartbreak of Aaron Boone's home run in 2003 to complete euphoria the following October, when the Sox cowboyed up, erased a 3-0 deficit and finally broke the curse.

I also got to experience the Celtics-Lakers rivalry returning to the NBA Finals in 2008 and 2010, when the animosity between the teams and their fans felt almost as important as the basketball itself. Those games were appointment television, and I'm still fiending for another Celtics-Lakers Finals.

LOS ANGELES – DECEMBER 25: Kobe Bryant #24 of the Los Angeles Lakers gets into an argument with Paul Pierce #34 and Kevin Garnett #5 of the Boston Celtics during their game at Staples Center on December 25, 2008 in Los Angeles, California. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and/or using this Photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2008 NBAE (Photo by Noah Graham/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

The NBA has produced some incredible rivalries throughout its history, but it feels like fewer of them have much staying power anymore. Players are constantly switching teams, contenders fall apart and yesterday's bitter enemies become tomorrow's podcast guests. Sometimes we're told two teams are rivals simply because the league scheduled them on Christmas or they're playing during Rivals Week, presented by Amazon Web Services. Because nothing screams genuine hatred like a comprehensive cloud computing platform.

Anyway, the Boston Celtics play the Philadelphia 76ers tonight.

In the lead-up, I've been thinking about what makes a great NBA rivalry and how many of those ingredients are present in the current relationship between Boston and Philadelphia. The more I thought about it, the more convinced I became that we're watching one of the league's oldest rivalries return to something resembling its best form.

Joel Embiid famously declared back in 2018 that Celtics-Sixers wasn't much of a rivalry because Boston kept beating Philadelphia. Eight years and one particularly painful playoff series later, I think it's worth revisiting his assessment.

I've settled on four criteria for evaluating a proper NBA rivalry, and Boston and Philadelphia are about to receive their entirely unofficial rivalry report card.

(Original Caption) Bill Russell of the Celtics turns a deaf ear on Wilt Chamberlain of the 76ers and has to be restrained by referee John Vanak away from Chamberlain.

You can't manufacture decades of bad blood, and Boston and Philadelphia have enough to fill a small museum. Ideally one with separate entrances for fans of each team.

The Celtics and Sixers have met in 23 playoff series, more than any other pairing in NBA history. Boston holds a 15-8 advantage, with some of basketball's greatest players participating in the ongoing effort to make the other franchise miserable.

It started with Bill Russell and Wilt Chamberlain, when Boston spent much of the 1960s blocking Wilt's path to a championship. Philadelphia finally broke through in 1967, ending the Celtics' run of eight consecutive titles.

Then came Larry Bird and Julius Erving, and an entirely new generation of reasons for the cities to dislike each other. The teams met in four Eastern Conference finals during the 1980s, splitting them 2-2. The 1981 series was particularly ridiculous, with Boston overcoming a 3-1 deficit and winning the final three games by a combined five points. Five!

And, of course, there was the infamous fight between Bird and Dr. J on November 9, 1984, which just celebrated its 42nd anniversary. Bird had already scored 42 points when things got ugly in the third quarter, with Moses Malone restraining him as Erving landed several punches.

Erving later explained his attitude toward playing Boston rather succinctly: "I don't care if it's your mother, you're not supposed to get along." I appreciate that level of commitment to the rivalry. Erving family dinners must have been interesting.

At that time, Philadelphia even had a player nicknamed the Boston Strangler. Andrew Toney earned the name by repeatedly torching the Celtics, including a 34-point performance in Game 7 of the 1982 Eastern Conference finals that sent Boston home.

Imagine being so consistently good at ruining another team's season that people start calling you the Boston Strangler. I'd have that printed on my business cards.

The beauty of this history is that it never really goes away. The players change, coaches come and go, and special-edition uniforms occasionally make us nauseous, but the relationship survives.

For a younger Celtics fan, tonight might be their first real introduction to the rivalry. Meanwhile, somewhere in Philadelphia, there's probably a grandfather who still refuses to allow anyone named Larry into his house.

What a time to get involved. There's nearly eighty years of basketball history waiting for you, and both teams have recently supplied plenty of new material.

For a long time, Boston fans had a pretty comfortable relationship with Philadelphia.

Remember Embiid's assessment from 2018? Following another loss to Boston, he famously said, "This is not a rivalry…they always kick our ass."

He wasn't wrong. Boston eliminated Philadelphia in six consecutive playoff meetings between 1985 and 2023.

The most memorable recent example came in 2023, when Jayson Tatum scored 51 points in Game 7 of the Eastern Conference semifinals, setting the NBA's Game 7 scoring record at the time. Celtics fans celebrated while Sixers fans contemplated another year of trusting the process.

After Boston built a 3-1 series lead in the first round, Philadelphia won three straight games and completed the comeback with a 109-100 victory at TD Garden. Before that series, Boston had been 32-0 when leading a playoff series 3-1. Philadelphia had never overcome that deficit in 18 previous attempts.

For Celtics fans, it was a miserable way to end a season already defined by uncertainty surrounding Tatum's Achilles recovery. The Sixers, who'd spent years being reminded of their playoff failures against Boston, finally got to celebrate on the parquet.

And that's precisely what this rivalry needed, however gut-wrenching it is to admit. A rivalry becomes a lot less interesting when one team wins every meaningful matchup. Eventually, the winning side starts treating the opponent like a routine trip to the barber shop.

Philadelphia's comeback changed the dynamic.

Sixers fans now have a recent series in which their team delivered one of the most painful postseason defeats in Boston's history. On the flip side, Celtics fans can point to decades of superiority, but that doesn't make the most recent result any less irritating.

Both fan bases have something to bring up and something they'd desperately prefer the other forget.

I'm stopping short of an A because I'd like to see the teams trade a few more meaningful blows before declaring this an entirely balanced postseason feud. Preferably with Boston getting the next one.

We've spent an extraordinary amount of time discussing the relationship between Jayson Tatum and Jaylen Brown since Boston traded Brown to Philadelphia.

At this point, the alleged beef has been stirred, sautéed, broiled, slow-cooked and served with several unnecessary side dishes. I'm sure a script is in the works to turn the story into a limited series.

The latest round of questions came this week as the former teammates prepared to face each other for the first time. Brown told CelticsBlog's Bobby Manning that the two had nothing to discuss before tipoff, while Tatum has continued emphasizing that he'd rather focus on basketball.

Tatum and Brown spent nine seasons together in Boston. They became perennial All-Stars, reached two NBA Finals and won a championship together in 2024. Whatever their relationship looks like today, I'd rather celebrate what they accomplished than spend another afternoon interpreting Brown's cryptic comments.

PHILADELPHIA, PENNSYLVANIA – APRIL 24: Jayson Tatum #0 and Jaylen Brown #7 of the Boston Celtics look on against the Philadelphia 76ers during game three of the Eastern Conference first round playoffs at Xfinity Mobile Arena on April 24, 2026 in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. The Celtics defeated the 76ers 108-100. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Mitchell Leff/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Besides, the actual basketball matchup is much more enjoyable to dream about.

Will they guard each other? If they do, how much does their familiarity help? They've spent almost a decade practicing together, studying film together and learning how to attack defenses alongside one another. They know each other's habits, preferred spots and tendencies better than most opponents ever could.

Does that make defending each other easier, or do two players with that much shared experience become even more difficult to predict?

And somehow LeBron James has managed to find his way into this episode, too. At this point, we're approaching Avengers-level crossover territory.

LeBron spent years tormenting Boston during his Cleveland and Miami tenures. Now he's wearing a Sixers jersey alongside Brown, Embiid and Maxey, as though Philadelphia deliberately assembled a collection of Boston's greatest supervillains.

Meanwhile, Paul George is enjoying an excellent start in Boston after two difficult seasons with Philadelphia. His ability to operate alongside Tatum has been one of the Celtics' most encouraging early developments, and he'll have his own familiar faces waiting on the other side tonight.

There are enough connections here to keep the NBA's broadcast crew occupied for several hours. I'm just hoping we spend most of that time discussing how these players might actually compete against each other.

This might be the most important ingredient of all.

You can have decades of history and a roster full of former teammates, but the rivalry loses some appeal when one team is spending March counting down the days until its season ends.

The best NBA rivalries have stakes. Both teams need to be good enough that beating the other feels meaningful, preferably with a legitimate chance they'll meet again when the games matter most.

Through the first few weeks of the 2026-27 season, Boston and Philadelphia have certainly held up their ends of that arrangement.

The Sixers enter tonight at 9-2, sitting atop the Eastern Conference. Boston is right behind them at 8-2, having won three straight games, including Sunday's 117-97 victory over Milwaukee.

Philadelphia has quickly found success with its shiny new roster. Brown is averaging more than 22 points per game, while Maxey continues to be a nightmare for opposing defenses. Embiid remains an enormous problem whenever he's healthy, and LeBron gives the Sixers an ageless playmaker who has spent a good chunk of his life learning how to beat Boston.

The Celtics have started impressively despite undergoing significant changes themselves.

Tatum has returned to playing at an All-NBA level following his Achilles injury. George has fit beautifully alongside him, and Boston's supporting cast is beginning to settle into its new responsibilities. Joe Mazzulla has even started hugging people during games, a sign that either things are going well or the end times are near.

Tonight will be our first regular-season opportunity to see how these particular rosters match up.

Boston has to contend with Maxey's speed and Embiid's presence inside. Brown and George could find themselves defending one another, while Tatum's developing partnership with George faces one of its toughest tests yet.

There's plenty to examine beyond the inevitable pregame questions about who plans to shake whose hand.

And while I don't want to get ahead of myself ten games into the season, it's difficult to look at these rosters without imagining another playoff meeting in the near future.

If that happens, tonight could be the beginning of another particularly memorable chapter in a rivalry that already has a lot of unforgettable ones.

The history is there, Philadelphia has finally given Boston a reason to seek revenge, and the player storylines have somehow become more complicated than the plot of an HBO drama.

What we haven't seen is how all of that translates onto the court with these particular rosters.

Maybe tonight ends up being an ordinary basketball game. It's certainly possible Tatum and Brown exchange a quick handshake, everybody behaves like responsible adults, and the teams play 48 minutes without providing a single controversial quote.

At this point, that would probably be healthy for everyone involved.

Personally, I'm hoping for an excellent game with a little competitive nastiness. I wouldn't mind seeing Pritchard or Scheierman give Embiid a hard time after an inevitable flop. Maybe Anfernee Simons will have words for the Celtics coaching staff after burying a three in front of their bench.

After years of Boston getting the better of this rivalry, Philadelphia finally has something to brag about. I can't think of a better way to welcome the rivalry back than having the two teams actually play each other.

Just preferably with the Celtics winning.

#2968. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2966. Federal Reserve Board issues enforcement action with Ontario Bancorporation, Inc.

#2962. NFL trade deadline moves: Should the Browns have made any of these?

Over the last couple of weeks, NFL trade deadline rumors ran hot and heavy, just not for the Cleveland Browns. The Browns were linked to a number of defensive ends as possible trade options, and trading WR Jerry Jeudy and QB Shedeur Sanders were suggested along the way. In the end, Cleveland GM Andrew Berry held on to premium picks and salary cap space for future options instead of present problems.

Over the last two weeks, a wide array of trades went down across the league. Some of them were of the monumental variety, pulled off by the Chicago Bears and Jacksonville Jaguars, some were mostly shuffling around lesser draft capital, and a few fell somewhere in the middle.

A look at all the different trades that went down over the last few weeks: are there any that the Browns should have made by matching the offer or increasing it just slightly?

Chiefs receive: WR Josh Downs and a 2027 seventh-round pick

Colts receive: A 2027 fourth-round pick and a 2027 fifth-round pick

Chargers receive: A 2027 fourth-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick

Raiders receive: A 2027 third-round pick.

Bills receive: S Amani Hooker and a 2027 fifth-round pick

Eagles receive: A 2027 first-round pick and a 2029 second-round pick

Jets receive: A 2028 sixth-round selection

Saints receive: 2028 sixth-round pick (which could elevate to a 2027 sixth-round pick if Jacksonville acquires one via trade or the compensatory selection formula)

Falcons receive: A 2027 second-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick

Ravens receive: QB Jacoby Brissett and a 2027 fifth-round pick

Cardinals receive: A 2027 fourth-round pick and a 2028 seventh-round pick

Bears receive: DL Jeffery Simmons and a 2028 fifth-round pick

Titans receive: A 2027 first-round pick, a 2028 first-round pick, and a 2027 fourth-round pick

Colts receive: A 2027 third-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick
