#2961. Jordan Poole leaves door open to return ‘home' to Warriors in offseason

Jordan Poole leaves door open to return 'home' to Warriors in offseason originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

The Warriors extended their win streak to eight games with a 138-87 blowout victory over the New Orleans Pelicans on Monday night at Chase Center, the team's largest margin of victory in a home game since 1991.

On the other side of the beatdown was guard Jordan Poole, Golden State's first-round pick in the 2019 NBA Draft.

When asked after the game if he would be open to reuniting with the Warriors, Poole appeared enthusiastic about the possibility.

"Yes. It just feels like home, though, you know?" Poole told the San Francisco Standard's Danny Emerman. "Driving into the f–king arena, crossing the Bay Bridge or wherever you're coming from, it definitely feels natural. And I think it's pretty evident, the way that I play is very fitting to how I used to play here. Nothing's changed, other than the systems that I'm in. We won a championship, you know, so it works. And it did work.

"So to answer your question: Definitely. This is my last year on the deal. I'm a free agent at the end of this season. So you never really know what's going to happen…But if there's any place that I'm comfortable, it's probably where I started."

Poole was part of the 2022 championship-winning team before being traded to the Washington Wizards after the 2022-23 season as part of a package for veteran guard Chris Paul.

After four seasons away from the Warriors, Poole also said that he missed the way his former team played basketball.

"Hell yeah. Obviously. They play the right way, bro," Poole said. "They play good basketball. They play team basketball. Obviously, the environment is pretty high-level. Like I said, once you put a lot of work, and you've got hardware with an organization, with a team, with a city, you're kind of cemented in the history. So, it's good. But do I miss it? Hell yeah."

The Michigan alum broke out in the 2021-22 season for Golden State, averaging 18.5 points per game on 59.1-percent true shooting in the regular season and scoring 18 points per game on 65.4-percent true shooting in the playoffs.

After helping Golden State win the title, Poole received a four-year, $128 million extension and looked to be a bridge between the team's veteran trio and the organization's future.

However, a preseason dispute with teammate Draymond Green seemingly stymied the young guard's momentum.

Poole averaged just 10.3 points per game and shot 34.1 percent from the field in the 2022-23 playoffs, as the Warriors were eliminated in the second round.

The 27-year-old has struggled to get back on track, averaging 18.8 points for a bottom-tier Wizards team, and his numbers have only declined since landing in New Orleans.

Poole is playing only 19.6 minutes per game, scoring a career-low 6.6 points per game on 19.2-percent shooting from the field and 23.4-percent shooting from the 3-point line.

After the game, Poole called his current situation "pretty f–king difficult" and mentioned the moving pieces around him, including having five coaches in four seasons.

But if the Warriors are open to it, it sounds like Poole would welcome a homecoming after this season.

#2956. Bills acquire playmaking safety Amani Hooker from Titans, send second-round pick in return

With their sights set on a Super Bowl title, the Buffalo Bills are adding a dynamic defensive playmaker in trading for Tennessee Titans safety Amani Hooker.

The Bills announced at Tuesday's NFL trade deadline that they are sending the Titans a 2027 second-round draft pick and will also receive a 2027 fifth-round selection in return to acquire Hooker. The Bills also inquired about eight-time Pro Bowl Cardinals safety Budda Baker before trading for Hooker, NFL Insider Jordan Schultz reports.

The move bolsters a defense that ranks 29th in the NFL in passing yards allowed per game. The Bills have one of the league's best offenses helmed by an MVP quarterback in Josh Allen, but their pass defense has been a liability throughout the season.

Trading a second-round pick to acquire Hooker is a steep price, but the move addresses a clear area of need for a Super Bowl contender.

Hooker, 28, is a difference-maker in the secondary. He joins the Bills tied for the league lead with five interceptions alongside six passes defended. The interception tally also ties a career high through just seven games. Hooker has 17 interceptions and 45 passes defended in his career.

Hooker is also an accomplished defender against the run. He ranks second on the Titans this season with 52 total tackles. He projects to plug in as an immediate starter for the Bills.

An eighth-year veteran, Hooker has played his entire NFL career for the Titans since they selected him in the fourth round of the 2019 draft.

For the Titans, they part with a long-term veteran leader and one of their best players. But Hooker is best suited to contribute to a team ready to win now, and at 1-7, the Titans are not that. A second-round pick is a valuable asset as they seek to continue to rebuild around second-year quarterback Cam Ward.

#2955. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2953. First Jaylen Brown, Jayson Tatum showdown could provide early trade scorecard

For the first time since they spent nine prolific seasons together, while rising to the top of the NBA, Jayson Tatum and Jaylen Brown will face off as opponents in the regular season.

The Boston Celtics (8-2) head to Philadelphia Tuesday, Nov. 10 to battle the 76ers (9-2), the team that acquired Brown in a July blockbuster trade. And with that comes the next chapter in a relationship that has at times became tense and uncomfortable.

Despite that, as the game approaches, both sides have been sure to down play any drama.

"It's nothing to talk about," Brown told reporters Sunday, Nov. 8. "I mean, he's on his side, I'm on my side. Just playing basketball. Both of us are trying to win, so it's nothing to talk about. Just check the ball up."

'WHY CAN'T WE BE FRIENDS': Sixers tease Celtics amid Tatum-Brown feud

Yet, now that we're three weeks into the 2026-27 season, it's a fair time to pose a couple questions.

One: is there a clear winner in the trade? And, two: will either team come to regret their choice?

At first glance, it's like a rare case where the move benefitted both sides; entering Monday night, these were the top two teams in the Eastern Conference. The Celtics were leading the NBA in net rating (+11.6), and the Sixers have been seamlessly sharing the ball and rank third in assist-to-turnover ratio (2.01).

It's necessary, however, to point out a few caveats here. The sample size remains rather small and, entering Monday night, the Celtics had played the NBA's easiest strength of schedule (.396), according to ESPN; Philadelphia's, by comparison, ranked 20th (.476).

To be clear, neither of these points are disqualifying, but both teams – Boston in particular – need to demonstrate that they can sustain these levels over time and against the league's better competition.

The most positive sign for both teams is that the issues that originally forced the divorce in the first place don't appear to have carried over.

Dating back to their first seasons as stars in Boston, the narrative surrounding Tatum and Brown was that they were not optimal fits. They both excel with the ball in their hands, and both can create their own shot with ease. That often led to a disjointed offense, where they often took turns scoring in isolation.

In Boston, Paul George, whom Philadelphia sent to Boston in the Brown trade, has been nothing but steady. He has acknowledged that his sole role is to be complementary to Tatum, and his 20.4 points, 5.2 rebounds and 4.1 assists per game are more than sufficient.

In Philadelphia, Brown's numbers are a little better (22.3/6.7/4.0), but he has directly addressed the dynamics of his new team, saying point guard Tyrese Maxey "has been leading the way for sure" and that the Sixers are "his team," referring to Maxey.

Brown has been more provocative in his public comments about any feud between he and Tatum. He recently posted an emoji of a blue hat – which is another way to say cap, or slang that someone is being dishonest – to a video on social media in which Tatum said he was caught off guard by the July trade. Even in a recent comment that began with praise for Tatum, Brown snuck in a sly jab.

"I think he's right," Brown said Oct. 24. "There's no beef. Jayson comes from a wonderful family. We've accomplished some great things, he's a hell of a basketball player, he got a great (public relations) team. There's no beef, but it might be a little lamb chops and some gravy, and he should stay ready at all times."

In their first meeting as opponents, a preseason game Oct. 16 in which Brown did not play, both players warmed up on opposite sides of the floor and did not acknowledge each other, at least not publicly.

"All of y'all kind of saw us grow up and everything that we accomplished," Tatum said recently. "... Like, how many people play in this league and never went to the Finals? We went twice and won a championship. So it should be more about that. This ain't no (expletive) Shaq and Kobe. It's not about that.

"It should be more praise, and I understand that don't necessarily sell. Drama gets clicks and views, but that's not my thing."

This all raises one last question, about whether it's fair to criticize players on the same team for not being friends off the floor. Just because their work is televised and just because they're paid handsomely does not mandate that they be forced into any sort of deeper bond we fans and media seemingly expect out of athletes. At the end of the day, they are – at least in professional terms – merely employees just like you and I. And just like you and I may have colleagues with whom we might not necessarily align, these players are entitled just the same.

This trade was not executed because of any perceived fractured friendship. The Celtics clearly prioritized one player's skill set over another's, and they made a difficult choice, one that does not come without some risk. They wanted to divest away from having two max contracts.

Tuesday night's game is just the next logical step, nothing more.

The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2947. Sixers return home for tough test against Celtics

It wasn't the smoothest win on Sunday, but the Sixers still held things together in the fourth quarter to beat the Bulls 127-121.

It takes Philadelphia to 9-2 on the season, and they're in for more of a challenge on Tuesday. Next, as they return from their road trip, they're taking on the team fighting them for the first spot in the East: the Boston Celtics.

As per usual, the Celtics are continuing their trend of being a top team on both ends of the floor. After ranking in the top four in both offensive and defensive rating last season, they're top four in both areas yet again this season and first in net rating at +11.6.

Jayson Tatum, in his first full season back from his Achilles injury, has started strong. As the clear leader of the offense, he's averaging 27.1 points, 6.0 rebounds and a big jump in assists to a career-high 7.8 per game. His usage percentage is higher than ever at 33.3, he's effectively creating more for others, and the Celtics are using him well by running him off screens and getting him attacking downhill.

Tatum's shot selection will be something else to be mindful of for the Sixers' defense. So far, he's cut down his three-pointers from at least 49 percent of his total field goal attempts in the last two seasons to 38.1, while using more attacks at the rim and mid-rangers.

If the Sixers' forwards can guard Tatum physically and Joel Embiid can stay spry with his interior rotations and rim protection, that will be key to building an advantage on Tuesday.

Of course, the personal storyline we have to focus on in this one is Jaylen Brown's first regular season contest against his former team. He's already had some choice words for his old team and running mate Tatum to keep things entertaining lately.

Brown is entering the game in a great rhythm as well. He was stellar yet again in the Sixers' win on Sunday, finishing with 29 points on 11-of-22 shooting, seven rebounds, four assists and a steal. Not to mention some important plays down the stretch to help lead the offense on a night the Sixers' defense looked tired.

On the flip side, this is also going to be Paul George's first game in Philadelphia since being dealt to the Celtics. And to his credit, he's playing great at both ends of the floor to start his tenure in Boston. Through nine games, George has averaged 20.4 points while shooting 42.5 percent from three on 8.1 attempts, 5.2 rebounds, 4.1 assists and 2.3 steals per game, while playing excellent, versatile defense.

George will be another good matchup for Brown, LeBron James and Dean Wade as the Sixers' forwards switch across him and Tatum, and contesting George's three-point threat in particular will be key to complementing defense of Tatum inside.

With Adem Bona remaining out due to his back injury, we should continue seeing more of Jabari Walker as a smaller backup center. He played a season-high of 19 minutes and 13 seconds against the Bulls on Sunday, and played well again — just as he has since preseason. Walker finished with nine points on 4-of-8 shooting and 11 rebounds, including an impressive five offensive boards. He hustled hard, was an integral factor in the Sixers out rebounding the Bulls, and even hit a three-pointer for good measure.

The Celtics do have more size with two 7-footers in Neemias Queta and Mitchell Robinson as their top centers, though, so Walker is going to be stretched. It'll be interesting to see how he fares and competes physically to try and keep them in check on dives to the rim and go to work as a rebounder.

This matchup should be a great test for the Sixers' 20th-ranked offense overall. From Derrick White's elite defense in the backcourt, to Tatum and George disrupting on the wings and Queta and Robinson's rim protection. We've seen many flashes of just how dangerous the Sixers' new offense can be and lots of individual brilliance, whether it's LeBron's passing or Tyrese Maxey's electric scoring. But after a rough defeat against the Cavaliers on Friday and a shaky win against the Bulls, the Sixers are likely going to need a more consistent, balanced effort to beat the Celtics.

If the Sixers can handle Boston on Tuesday and the Raptors on Thursday, they'll be in good shape to strengthen their hold on the No. 1 seed this month. Because after that, the Sixers' schedule starts easing up with plenty of matchups against teams under .500 through the rest of November.

We've seen these Sixers take down several difficult opponents already. Let's see if they can do it again the Celtics.

#2945. Seattle Seahawks 2026 trade deadline tracker

We've reached NFL and therefore Seattle Seahawks trade deadline week, which often promises to have some interesting deals and rarely actually materializes. Minor or mid-level deals have happened more recently on deadline day itself, so it's not like we live in a "nothing happens" world. There have admittedly been prominent in-season trades already this year, including the Quenton Nelson and Jeffery Simmons trades just a couple of weeks ago, as well as Joey Porter Jr going to the Dallas Cowboys, and Marvin Harrison Jr heading for the Houston Texans.

For the second year in a row, the trade deadline is during Week 10. The Seattle Seahawks are usually active during the deadline, and this year they've pulled off one major trade. Will John Schneider cook up another midseason masterclass like he did for Leonard Williams, Quandre Diggs, Carlos Dunlap, and other names from previous deadline deals? Follow Field Gulls' trade tracker coverage throughout Monday and Tuesday, and we'll see what the Seahawks will do as they look to get back on track after a sputtering 5-4 start.

Yes. They acquired longtime Indianapolis Colts guard Quenton Nelson for a 2027 third-round pick and a 2028 fifth-rounder.

No one immediately springs to mind. The likes of Dante Fowler Jr have rarely played this year but likely wouldn't fetch much on the trade market. Emanuel Wilson being an unofficial healthy scratch is worth monitoring, although similarly he's not much of a valuable trade piece, and Seattle may not want to risk moving any capable RB given the injury concerns on the depth chart.

Edge rusher and wide receiver might be two positions of need for Seattle. Rashid Shaheed's injury and generally underwhelming receiving stats could impact what Seattle does at receiver. The edge rushing room is essentially three deep, and with only Derick Hall and Connor O'Toole under contract beyond 2027, there might be some forward thinking required. The New York Jets' Will McDonald IV has reportedly requested a trade and could be one to watch.

The Seahawks made zero trade deadline day moves.

Josh Downs is going from the Indianapolis Colts to the Kansas City Chiefs. The Chiefs will give up a fourth- and fifth-rounder and also get a seventh-rounder in return.

The Denver Broncos land Los Angeles Chargers linebacker Daiyan Henley for a 2027 fourth- and a 2028 fifth-round pick.

Kirk Cousins is going to the Minnesota Vikings for a 2027 third-rounder, which means the Seahawks will not face Cousins this Sunday. Fernando Mendoza figures to make his first NFL start.

The Buffalo Bills are acquiring Tennessee Titans safety Amani Hooker and a fifth-round pick in exchange for a second-round pick.

The 49ers are trading a 2027 sixth-rounder to the Colts for veteran wide receiver Keenan Allen.

The Eagles are trading pass rusher Arnold Ebiketie to the Cincinnati Bengals for a 2027 sixth-round pick. UPDATE: No they're not.

WHOA! The Jaguars just traded for Philadelphia Eagles defensive tackle Jordan Davis, giving up a 2027 first-rounder and a 2029 second.

The Atlanta Falcons have traded safety Jessie Bates to the Jacksonville Jaguars for a 2027 second-rounder and a 2028 fifth-rounder.

The New York Jets have dealt safety Andre Cisco to the Detroit Lions for a 2028 sixth-rounder.

The New Orleans Saints have agreed to trade defensive tackle Davon Godchaux to the Jacksonville Jaguars for a 2028 sixth-rounder, which could become a 2027 sixth-rounder.

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2940. Charge want to be a pipeline for the Cavs: ‘We want the development to be in-house’

CLEVELAND — The G League matters more for team building than ever. Look no further than the Cleveland Cavaliers roster for proof.

Seven of the 14 players on standard deals for the Cavs have spent time in the G League. That number should jump to eight, with Khalifa Diop expecting to play with the Cleveland Charge some this year as well. Diop's inclusion would mean that over half the roster has spent time in the G League.

This is a change from where things were over a decade ago. The reasons for that are layered. Investment in the league, along with changes to the salary cap and the style of play, has all contributed to this.

"In the world of aprons and salary cap and all this, what better way to cultivate the next wave of people that can help fit into [an NBA] environment than this?" Charge head coach Eli Kell-Abrams said.

The Cavs have invested in the Charge over the last several seasons. The move from Canton to Cleveland has allowed them to use the Cavs' training facility. More recently, switching their home games to Cleveland Public Auditorium, along with refitting the building for basketball, speaks to the organization's commitment to the G League.

"We want the development to be in-house," Charge general manager Liron Fanan said. "The type of players that you now take on your G League team, you really take into consideration what Kenny [Atkinson] and his staff are looking for. What kind of player are they on and off the court? How can he fit our culture of the Cavs and what we're looking for? It's mainly guys that are willing to accept a role, willing to compete every day, work hard, put their head down, and be ready."

The Charge are looking to identify and develop role players who can specifically fit alongside the Cavs' core group. There are two skills that the Charge are looking to cultivate, knowing that they will translate to the next level.

"They're going to have to be defensively minded, and also able to make shots when you stand in the corner because you're not going to have the ball in your hands," Fanan said. "You're going to be that guy that comes off the bench for five minutes and tries to impact the game in those minutes. So, how ready are you? What's your mentality? The toughness and the willingness to just take a role. Those are the skills that we look at."

The Charge are trying to refine those skills with their two-way players. Riley Minix has proven to be a good shooter — Summer League notwithstanding — but shooting alone isn't going to allow him to become a rotation player. "He needs to get better [defensively]," Fanan said. "Can he really defend and switch at the NBA level? We saw a little bit of that, and we're seeing improvement. I think there needs to be a big jump this year."

Rookie two-way center Ernest Udeh Jr. has the potential to develop into a useful backup center. The Cavs like the motor, but there's a learning curve for bigs transitioning from college to the pros.

"He always plays hard, now for us, it's to get him better at understanding spacing," Fanan said. "Understanding guarding outside the perimeter, working with his hands better."

Success in the G League is different than the NBA. The Charge want to win games, but their goal is to ensure they're developing talent that can be used by the Cavs and other NBA teams. For them to continue doing that, they want to continue cultivating a culture that makes that possible.

"We're in a league that is sometimes not about the right stuff," Kell-Abrams said. "We want to be the group that relies on each other, that is about everybody else's success. That passed the ball in a league that doesn't."

The Charge open their season at home on Tuesday night against the Motor City Cruise.

#2939. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2937. Dolphins quiet through NFL trade deadline 2026

The Miami Dolphins were projected to be sellers at the NFL's 2026 trade deadline, but with the 4 p.m. Eastern deadline now come and gone, Miami stood pat with their roster. While most teams that start a season 1-7 would be considered a disappointment and in need of a rebuild, Miami has already embraced the restart the franchise has needed and shed most of their veteran players before the season. Those that are on the roster were retained for a reason and, with the deadline now passed, they will hold those roles throughout the remainder of the year.

Speculation had focused on Jordyn Brooks. The All-Pro linebacker could have provided a team with a much-needed upgrade, especially if they need an injury replacement. Teams like the Detroit Lions and Dallas Cowboys were believed to be interested in Brooks.

Defensive tackle Zach Sieler was another potential trade asset, but both the Dolphins and Sieler seemed to want to keep the veteran in South Florida. News on Tuesday that Sieler sustained a torn biceps muscle during the Dolphins' Week 9 loss to the Detroit Lions ruled out any possible trade. Miami will now have to decide if they are going to keep Sieler beyond this year, especially with a salary cap number expected to balloon to $20.8 million next year compared to this season's $11.0 million.

The Dolphins could be stingy with any offer they received this year, putting a premium on all of their players and expecting high draft picks in return. The veterans that could be targets for other teams seem to be players Miami plans to keep beyond this year. There was no pressure to move on from a player, agreeing to every trade offer sent, and, ultimately, Miami chose not to make any deal.

The day was not completely silent in Miami, however, as a groin injury for quarterback Malik Willis and a torn biceps muscle for defensive tackle Zach Sieler landed both veterans on injured reserve.

#2930. Davis puts on a show, Dybantsa flashes potential in loss to Pistons

The Washington Wizards got 44 points and 13 rebounds from Anthony Davis, and very little from anyone else, and lost to the Detroit Pistons, 122-111.

It was Washington's fourth straight loss as the team has struggled to generate effective offense or maintain stout defense.

Davis put on a show, especially in the first half, dominating the center-less Detroit lineup. He scored 30 points in the first half on 10-11 shooting from the floor. The Wizards headed to the locker room down 10 because the rest of the team shot 10-30.

AJ Dybantsa shows big time potential in Wizards loss to the Detroit Pistons. | Getty Images

For the game, Davis shot 16-21. The rest of the team: 25-68. Davis used 25 offensive possessions to generate 41 points. His teammates used 78 possessions to produce 70 points. As the great Fred Katz used to say, "Yikes guys."

The other major redeeming feature of the game was the play of No. 1 overall pick AJ Dybantsa, who had the most spectacular replacement level game I've ever seen. I can't decide which plays impressed me the most:

The lightning Euro-step drive in transition that froze John Collins and ended with a two-handed dunk

The drive that included getting fouled by Cade Cunningham out top, and ended with a cuffed dunk on Collins

The fake drive to attack a closeout that led to a relocation stepback three from the corner

That doesn't include his relentless defensive effort and the constant pressure he put on the Detroit defense. Or his duel with Cunningham that was massively entertaining.

Or when he battled Ron Holland for a loose ball, inadvertently slapped him in the face, and then spent a chunk of the break while refs watched the replay reviewing the play on the arena screen with Detroit's Daniss Jenkins.

The five turnovers and below average turnovers undermined his overall value for the game, but the highlights were big time plays, and the defensive impact was great from a 19-year-old rookie.

The Peacock broadcast format is great, and I hope it catches on around the league. Embedding the analysts on the benches is a genius innovation — especially when they share what the coaches are telling their players.

Bilal Coulibaly missed the game with a left hand injury that I'm pretty sure happened a couple games ago.

Will Riley entered the game in the first half, and almost immediately gave up one of the easiest drives in Cunningham's career. Zero resistance. Not even turnstile level defense.

Around 8:41 in the second quarter, Davis got an open three because Trae Young sprinted off screens, turned the corner and got into the paint. His effort on the play forced the help defender to commit (not coming over would have conceded a layup), which left Davis wide open at the three-point line. Young made the pass, and Davis converted.

When I looked at my notes, I discovered one more Dybantsa play that I'd forgotten — in the second quarter, he threw a fake so fast and convincing that Kevin Huerter stepped in the wrong direction. Dybantsa went past him for a layup.

Detroit opened the second half with Duncan Robinson getting a four-point play on what I thought was a bad call on Young. The Wizards started the period with a turnover, and then followed it up with an atrocious possession that ended with Dybantsa being forced to take a deep three with the shot clock running out. The team had tried — and failed — to feed Kyshawn George in the post against the diminutive Jenkins. They then ran multiple screens that finally got a switch that gave Dybantsa a mismatch…and not enough time to attack it.

Peaccock's Austin Rivers reported that during a third quarter timeout, Wizards coach Brian Keefe urged the team to improve their effort. His critique included Davis, who'd just given up a backcut dunk to Cunningham, which included an and-one free throw. "Let's not wait until the next game," Keefe said, according to Rivers. "Let's do it now."

George needs to figure out to play on offense without turning it over so much. He had five more turnovers last night. On one in the fourth quarter, he drove into the paint, couldn't get to the rim, and picked up his dribble. Stuck, and with the three-second count ticking, he attempted a ridiculous pass out with Ausar Thompson in the vicinity. The pass was stolen, of course.

I wrote this in the fourth quarter, but I could have written it in any period: The Wizards cannot figure out how to initiate their offense. Dybantsa set a screen for George. George came up to set a ball screen for Davis. But, Davis went nowhere. George drifted a little and the possession just stalled. Davis finally tossed up a three, which missed. The offensive design was fine, though perhaps not with Davis as the ball handler.

A few minutes later, the Wizards ran a similar set but with Davis screening for George. Davis rolled hard, George made a crisp pass, and Davis converted the layup.

Thompson had an amazing block on Alex Sarr with around four minutes left in the game. He met Sarr at the rim, contested with with verticality, went up higher despite being several inches smaller, and swatted the dunk attempt.

Below are the four factors that decide wins and losses in basketball — shooting (efg), rebounding (offensive rebounds), ball handling (turnovers), fouling (free throws made).

eFG% (effective field goal percentage, which accounts for the three-point shot)

TOV% (turnover percentage — turnovers divided by possessions)

FTM/FGA (free throws made divided by field goal attempts)

PPA is my overall production metric, which credits players for things they do that help a team win (scoring, rebounding, playmaking, defending) and dings them for things that hurt (missed shots, turnovers, bad defense, fouls).

PPA is a per possession metric designed for larger data sets. In small sample sizes, the numbers can get weird. In PPA, 100 is average, higher is better and replacement level is 45. For a single game, replacement level isn't much use, and I reiterate the caution about small samples sometimes producing weird results.

POSS is the number of possessions each player was on the floor in this game.

ORTG = offensive rating, which is points produced per individual possessions x 100. League average so far this season is listed in the Four Factors table above. Points produced is not the same as points scored. It includes the value of assists and offensive rebounds, as well as sharing credit when receiving an assist.

USG = offensive usage rate. Average is 20%. Median last season was 17.7%.

ORTG and USG are versions of stats created by former Wizards assistant coach Dean Oliver and modified by me. ORTG is an efficiency measure that accounts for the value of shooting, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers. USG includes shooting from the floor and free throw line, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers.

+PTS = "Plus Points" is a measure of the points gained or lost by each player based on their efficiency in this game compared to league average efficiency on the same number of possessions. A player with an offensive rating (points produced per possession x 100) of 100 who uses 20 possessions would produce 20 points. If the league average efficiency is 115, the league — on average — would produced 23.0 points in the same 20 possessions. So, the player in this hypothetical would have a +PTS score of -3.0.

Players are sorted by total production in the game.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2929. NFL Injury Report, Week 10: Latest news, updates for Puka Nacua, Patrick Mahomes, more players for fantasy football

It's the halfway point of the 2026 NFL season and by now you likely have weathered plenty of injuries on your fantasy football rosters. We have a handful of big names we're tracking going into Week 10 while four teams are on bye — the Bears, Broncos, Buccaneers and Eagles. Below we'll update you on the latest injury news, plus provide some useful fantasy football analysis.

Kyler Murray, MIN (hamstring) — Week-to-week

Sam Darnold, SEA (quad/ribs) — Out for Week 10

Drew Lock, SEA (oblique) — Should be good to start

Gardner Minshew, ARI (elbow) — Feared to have torn UCL

Mahomes: At the time of Mahomes' injury in Week 9, it looked as though we were watching a QB who may be done for the season. But after exiting due to an elbow injury, Mahomes was able to return in the Chiefs' 35-12 win over the Jets on Sunday. Mahomes completed 23-of-31 passes for 242 yards and two scores, posting fewer than 20 fantasy points for the fifth time in the past six games.

Kenneth Walker III continues to be the focal point of the offense as the passing game lacks depth and a vertical threat. The Chiefs face the Falcons in Week 10 and Mahomes is considered "day to day" at this point. Should he sit, Justin Fields would draw the start, a downgrade for receivers like Rashee Rice and Travis Kelce. Walker would be relied on more heavily but could suffer with defenses keying in on the run game.

Seahawks QBs: Head coach Mike Macdonald already went on the radio Monday and said Darnold will be out Week 10 against the Raiders. Lock underwent tests on an oblique injury he suffered Sunday and those came back "good," so he's in line to start again. The Raiders have been a great fantasy matchup for QBs this season, allowing the fifth-most points to the position. However, the Seattle offense has struggled a bit with lack of depth behind Jaxon Smith-Njigba. Lock is back in the QB2 streaming convo for Sunday and isn't a bad spot start if you have Caleb Williams or Bo Nix on bye.

McCarthy: New York was able to sneak out of Philly on Sunday with a win. But McCarthy was forced to exit for a period of time to a hip injury. Head coach John Harbaugh has already told reporters McCarthy looks "like he's going to play" in Week 10 against Washington on TNF. The quick turnaround isn't ideal and we could see Jameis Winston back as the starter if McCarthy can't play.

The offense didn't look great against the Eagles, though they weren't much of a threat as the Giants' defense put the clamps on Joe Milton. We've seen both McCarthy and Winston elevate the passing game, so there isn't much change there in terms of how we approach Malik Nabers and Isaiah Likely. Cam Skattebo should also be back to help the run game in Week 10 after missing Sunday's game. You were likely only starting McCarthy in deep superflex formats anyway.

Minshew: It looks like the Cardinals QB will be done for the season with a torn UCL. That means rookie Carson Beck is in line to start. If you remember back to the preseason, Beck was actually pretty good. There's a chance this is a good move overall for the offense, particularly TE Trey McBride. But Arizona is down WR Michael Wilson (neck) and Beck won't have many other options in the passing game. In superflex, Beck is worth an speculative add to see how he does in Week 10 against the Rams. In regular 10-12 team formats, Beck is a risky streaming option until we see how his debut goes.

Willis: The Dolphins surprised us before the trade deadline by placing Willis on IR, which could mean he's done for the season this late in the year. He'll miss at least the next four games, which would mean the soonest he could be back is Week 14 against Chicago. 2025 sixth-round pick Kyle McCord is the backup and is in line to start in Week 10 versus the Colts. Brady Cook is on the practice squad and will likely be elevated as the backup.

Miami's passing offense enters Week 10 ranked 30th in the NFL. Chances are it doesn't get much better with McCord under center, but we'll need to see. It's a downgrade to receivers like Malik Washington, Greg Dulcich and Caleb Douglas in the short-term until we assess things. Ollie Gordon II could be leaned on a bit more, and McCord may use the RB and Dulcich as safety valves.

Cam Skattebo, NYG (hamstring) — In line to return

Josh Jacobs, GB (finger) — Report: Out a max of three weeks

Patriots RBs: Stevenson exited Sunday's blowout loss to the Packers early and did not return. Henderson has missed the past two games after being hospitalized due to "unexplained neurological symptoms." Stevenson is set to undergo an MRI on Monday and we'll see what the deal is. If Stevenson and Henderson are sidelined in Week 10 against the Lions in Germany, Corey Kiner would take over as the lead back. He finished with a solid 8-55-1 rushing line against Green Bay, though most of that production was on a fourth-quarter drive when the game was essentially over. Lan Larison would be the RB2 for New England, but the Pats could explore adding a back.

Jacobs: On the other side of that game, Jacobs suffered a fractured finger in the win over the Patriots and didn't return. Rushing duties were then split between Pierre Strong Jr. and Kaleb Johnson with MarShawn Lloyd out. Johnson rushed for 68 yards on 14 carries with a score. He looked pretty good running the ball for a team that has struggled to do so all season. But it was also in a blowout. Even so, Johnson is a proactive add off the waiver wire with Jacobs expected to be out until at least Week 13. Green Bay faces the Vikings in Week 10 before the bye and it would be hard to trust any Packers RB against one of the best defenses in the NFL. Johnson will be a risky flex play against Minnesota in Week 10. Strong isn't a bad add in deeper PPR formats while keep an eye on Lloyd, who could be back soon.

Puka Nacua, LAR (knee/turf toe) — Likely headed to IR

Michael Wilson, ARI (neck) — Out for season

Luther Burden III, CHI (concussion) — on bye

Nacua: The Rams sent Nacua for an MRI and Sean McVay told reporters Monday evening that the wideout is likely landing on injured reserve with knee sprains and turf toe. The Rams are already without Davante Adams and there isn't much depth behind those two. Konata Mumpfield, Tutu Atwell and the trio of tight ends — Tyler Higbee, Colby Parkinson and Davis Allen — would need to step up. We'd throw Terrance Ferguson into that group, but he remains on IR and isn't expected back in Week 10. L.A. should lean more on its run game with Kyren Williams and Blake Corum.

With Nacua out, it's a downgrade to the passing game and Matthew Stafford's fantasy outlook. It's a slight bump up for Williams and Corum, both of whom are involved in the passing game plenty. The Rams take on the Cardinals before their bye in Week 11, which is an easy draw for the run game. Mumpfield isn't a bad waiver wire add along with Atwell in deeper formats. But don't expect any receiver to get the type of volume like Nacua.

Michael Wilson: In a surprising development on Monday afternoon, Wilson will miss the rest of the season due to a neck injury. It's a tough blow for managers who were hoping for more following the Marvin Harrison Jr. trade. Instead, Wilson struggled, failing to reach double-digit fantasy points in four of the past five games. With Jalen Brooks and Kendrick Bourne on injured reserve, Arizona is very, very thin at WR.

Simi Fehoko, Reggie Virgil and Devin Duvernay all played significant snaps in Week 9 against Seattle. None of them did much, while Trey McBride was obviously the main target for Gardner Minshew. Though, Minshew is also expected to miss the rest of the season due to a torn UCL, per NFL Network.

This team is falling apart as we approach the deadline on Tuesday. We could see rookie Carson Beck starting at QB soon. You can take a shot at one of those WRs mentioned above off the wire but none seem particularly reliable/exciting. If you need replacements at WR off the wire, refer to Justin Boone's column for Week 10.

Pickens: The Cowboys held Pickens out of their win over the Colts in Week 9. As a result, CeeDee Lamb went off for 10-195-1 while third-string WR Ryan Flournoy was quiet with two catches for 53 yards. Dallas leaned on the run game, leading most of the first half. It's clear the offense missed Pickens and will need him ready for a key NFC matchup with the Niners in Week 10. If Pickens is out again, Flournoy remains an a flex option while Lamb would remain a target hog and elite WR1.

#2922. What to expect from the Boston Celtics and Philadelphia 76ers

NBA fans have been waiting for November 10 since the NBA schedule was released in August. From the moment Brad Stevens opted to trade Jaylen Brown away from the Boston Celtics to a conference rival, the Philadelphia 76ers, you knew the two sides were on a collision course. Now, Brown is set to face off against his old team on his new home court for the first time since that trade went down.

On paper, the Sixers have the stronger roster. Nick Nurse is coaching a starting five comprised of multiple future Hall of Famers, including two former MVPs, LeBron James and Joel Embiid. On the opposite side of the floor, Joe Mazzulla is working with a retooling roster comprised of veteran talent and young, high-upside developmental pieces such as Hugo Gonzalez and Jordan Walsh.

There are a lot of differences between where each team is in terms of its development and timeline. However, once the game gets underway, all of that will go out of the window, and all eyes will be on the matchup between Jayson Tatum and Brown. The former teammates will undoubtedly take turns guarding each other at points during the game, and will likely feel they need to prove something, both to the fans in the arena and those watching from home.

In a recent discussion that has been posted to the "NBA on the NBC" YouTube channel, Ahmed Fareed, Brian Scalabrine, and Frank Isola discussed the upcoming matchup between Boston and Philadelphia and what we can expect to see on the court. Tatum and Brown were undoubtedly at the forefront of that discussion, but they weren't the only factors the trio pinpointed, with both teams having things to focus on and areas to try to avoid.

#2920. Rams earn poor trade deadline grade after taking big risk at WR

Despite a decimated wide receiver room, the Los Angeles Rams are staying put and have let the NFL trade deadline pass without making any moves to bolster their passing game.

Puka Nacua's placement on injured reserve after spraining his ACL and MCL on Monday fueled speculation that general manager Les Snead's hand could be forced to act, considering both of Matthew Stafford's top receivers are now out for the foreseeable future, with Davante Adams still weeks away from returning from his ruptured hamstring.

When addressing the media on Monday, head coach Sean McVay told reporters they would be looking at "all avenues" to improve the position group.

"Putting our heads together and figuring out just the semantics of, what is the 48/53 (man roster)? What does our practice squad look like?" he said. "How does that shape the different areas of our football team, defensively, offensively and in the kicking game?"

Ultimately, it appears Snead and McVay have decided to go in a different direction.

NFL trade grades: NFL trade deadline grades reveal which team won every major deal

Despite the confidence the front office shares in the position group, they're taking a big risk in not adding at least some help externally. Nacua alone accounted for over half of Stafford's passing yards in Week 9 against the Commanders, and he was the only Rams receiver to have more than 20 receiving yards on the day.

Bringing in another guy won't automatically recreate the trust and chemistry that Nacua has built with Stafford, but it could've at the very least added depth to their most depleted unit.

Maybe the asking price was too high; maybe Snead and McVay didn't like the fit of any of the available receivers on the market. We won't know that until later, but for now it can't help but feel like the Rams let an opportunity slip by.

Here's what LA's receiver room currently looks like with Nacua and Adams out:

It's worth noting that McVay told reporters Whitting is dealing with back soreness, "so we'll see what that looks like moving forward."

McVay has voiced confidence in the depth of his receivers before, but that was with Nacua on the field. Without him, Konata Mumpfield assumes WR1 duties with Jordan Whittington (who is dealing with back soreness), CJ Daniels, Tutu Atwell and Alex Bachman rounding out the group. Tight end Terrance Ferguson returned to practice after missing five games with an ankle injury last week but tweaked his hamstring on Friday and is expected to miss more time.

"Guys will be asked to step up," McVay said on Monday ahead of the deadline. "Puka has always had a pretty large target share based on his production and the different things that he does and because of the way that you're able to get him the football because of some of the unselfish stuff he does in the run game. (He was) 14-of-19 (on Sunday), he was outstanding. There are other guys that are very capable of doing some good stuff for us.

"We'll look to see those guys step up and it'll be great opportunity for them in spite of it being a bummer for those guys that are out."

#2917. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2914. Columbia coach Megan Griffith returns from suspension for big game vs. Michigan

Without head coach Megan Griffith on the sidelines for its first three games of the season, the Columbia women's basketball team didn't just survive — it thrived.

The Lions went 3-0 against Davidson, Rhode Island and Belmont, a trio of squads that ranked in the top 110 of NET last year, a metric the NCAA uses to sort teams. Columbia won those games by an average margin of 13.3 points with associate coach Tyler Cordell making the in-game decisions and adjustments.

"Yeah, she should just take my job," Griffith said with a laugh during a Zoom call with reporters. "What I loved about it was how she just jumped right into it. I think Ty really understood the game planning aspect from my standpoint and how I think about it, and she put her spin on it. Which for her, that is so easy because she built the program with me."

Griffith is now back on the sidelines for Columbia following a three-game suspension, returning to the lead chair on the bench for perhaps the toughest stretch of the Lions' schedule. On Thursday, Nov. 12, they'll host No. 2 Michigan at Levien Gymnasium in New York City, travel to No. 18 Maryland on Sunday, Nov. 15, then host No. 5 Duke on Saturday, Nov. 21.

Columbia announced it was suspending Griffith — an alum of the Ivy League institution who is in her 10th season leading the Lions — in October following a review of the program by an outside firm after the university received a complaint about her behavior during practices from past seasons.

In a statement, Columbia said the review did not require notice to the NCAA of any rules violations and did not involve any allegations of violations of the university's anti-discrimination policies, Title IX or related policies. According to the statement, "Griffith expressed regret for the times in which her interactions with the team did not align with the university's expectations."

USA TODAY Sports was provided with a statement from current Columbia players, too. They said they "fully stand with and support" Griffith and that the complaint was "not indicative of our experience as part of this program."

During a call with reporters on Tuesday, Nov. 10, Griffith didn't discuss the reasoning behind her suspension, but talked about the positives that she took away from it.

"What a unique experience this has been. The biggest benefit was, I think, bringing my staff into how I game plan and think about the game, bringing our players more deeply into it, and then I got to watch a lot and coach the details of the game in a very different way," Griffith said. "I got to observe from a very different vantage point of a game. And obviously, that's not what I wanted. You want to be with your team, right?

"But it was a great opportunity to learn in a different way, and I think our team and my staff did a phenomenal job of stepping up and battling together."

Griffith, 41, played at Columbia from 2003-2007 and was a two-time All-Ivy League selection. After playing professionally in Europe, she joined the coaching ranks by becoming an assistant for Courtney Banghart at Princeton during a stretch where the Tigers made three NCAA Tournaments.

In 2016, Griffith took the reins at her alma mater and has turned Columbia into a consistent mid-major power. In the last five seasons, the Lions were 125-35 with three regular season Ivy League titles, two at-large NCAA Tournament appearances, a runner-up finish in the WNIT in 2023 and a WBIT championship last season. Columbia also won an NCAA Tournament game in 2025, defeating Washington in the First Four.

The March Madness bids and victory — and the WBIT title — were the first in Columbia's history. The program that Griffith has turned the Lions into is one that Power 4 teams and national title contenders are willing to play in the non-conference slate now, because they know Columbia will provide a competitive game and a sufficient measuring stick.

And for Columbia, pulling off an upset in one of these games can be the bullet point on the resume it needs to secure a berth in March Madness.

"It's a gauntlet. I love a challenge," Griffith said. "We have to prove a resume for March. Not that March hinges on (these games), but it gives you another chance, right? So, very intentionally, we always look for a couple of these Quad 1 games.

"Teams like Michigan, teams like Duke, they're not afraid to play anywhere. Those are teams trying to prepare themselves for hostile environments and playing against strong teams. I think that's just a good sign of a program that's really trying to test themselves for March and make a deep run."

Led by reigning Ivy League Player of the Year Riley Weiss, Columbia also brought back key contributors in Mia Broom, Fliss Henderson and 6-foot-3 German forward Hilke Feldrappe. Through the transfer portal, former Big Ten Rookie of the Year Mary Ashley Stevenson has also joined Columbia this season after spending one season apiece at Purdue and Stanford.

"They can turn it on when they want. I think we need to realize they can turn it on more. I think we're sometimes assessing the game and I'm like, 'Impose yourself. Be tone-setters,'" Griffith said. "I just want to see what we're made of a little bit more, right? Let's really test ourselves. We're throwing ourselves fully into the fire here."

To have a chance against a pair of teams in Michigan and Duke that made the Elite Eight last season and a consistent winner in Brenda Frese's Maryland, Columbia will need to push itself to play at its very best.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: Columbia coach Megan Griffith returns from suspension for big game vs. Michigan

#2913. Wemby is an alien; Phoenix is painfully human

There were no illusions of grandeur entering the Suns' matchup against the San Antonio Spurs on Monday night. The game would serve as a litmus test, an opportunity to see where Phoenix stood against one of the best teams in the NBA, and it played out pretty much as you might have expected. The Spurs are one of those unique teams that have the advantage of youth on their side, and when you add the fact that they have an alien in Victor Wembanyama patrolling both ends of the floor, defeating them is going to be a difficult task for every team across the Association throughout the season.

Victor Wembanyama is a demigod, an unstoppable force who, at this point, is toying with the sport of basketball. His impact on both ends of the floor is unlike anything we've seen. He possesses the size of Shawn Bradley, whom many of us grew up watching, but his game extends far beyond simply being big. The way he defends, the way he deters anyone who ventures inside the three-point arc, and the constant threat of his mere existence manipulate the game in a manner that leaves you shrugging your shoulders and sighing.

As for the Suns? They did a good job taking care of the basketball, committing only seven turnovers. But they were outscored 62-48 in the paint, 20-10 in second-chance points, and 19-10 in fast-break points. They were also outrebounded by 20, with San Antonio holding a commanding 55-35 advantage on the glass.

The Suns have made progress over the past week, but there's still a considerable gap between where they are and where the Western Conference's elite reside. San Antonio exposed the same deficiencies we've been discussing all season, particularly Phoenix's inability to control the glass and protect the paint. There's no shame in losing to a team featuring a generational talent like Wembanyama. But the manner in which you lose can tell you plenty about how far you still have to go.

It's not often the guy who scores under 10 points gets the Bright Side Baller, but the community here knows ball. The impact Ryan Dunn had in the victory over the Rockets was stellar, as he was a disruptor in every sense of the word.
