#2911. From Kirk Cousins saving Vikings' defense to Chargers ignoring reality, NFL trade deadline winners and losers

The NFL schedule makers scored a win with their pre-trade deadline "Monday Night Football" matchup.

Pitting two of the league's top teams against each other meant two teams likely to be active at the deadline would meet on national television to expose each other's vulnerabilities.

The Buffalo Bills' 23-6 takedown of the Minnesota Vikings did just that. And while Carson Wentz was starting in relief of an injured Kyler Murray (hamstring), the latest iteration of a toothless Minnesota offense alongside a menacing but asked-to-be-on-the-field-40-minutes Vikings defense signaled that change was necessary.

With less than 90 minutes remaining before the trade deadline, the Vikings dealt their 2027 third-round pick to the Las Vegas Raiders in exchange for reuniting with quarterback Kirk Cousins, a person with knowledge of the deal confirmed to Yahoo Sports.

What does this mean for the state of the NFC North and the NFC's Super Bowl race generally? And what has the trade landscape leading up to the deadline done to change teams' fortunes?

Here are Yahoo Sports' winners and losers of the NFL trade deadline:

The Vikings' defense: Many in Minnesota win with Cousins' arrival. Receiver Justin Jefferson no doubt is breathing a little more easily after the news. But the Vikings' defense coordinated by Brian Flores has to be the biggest winner. Flores' unit has allowed the fewest points and second-fewest yards across the entire NFL this season. Minnesota's offense has rewarded the blitz-happy dominance with … the 27th-ranked scoring offense and 31st-ranked (that's second-worst, for those counting at home) total offense. Cousins arrives with 2,340 passing yards (fifth) and a 97.1 passer rating (11th) this season. He led the Raiders to an offense averaging 8.1 more points and 64.2 more yards per game this season. Cousins knows O'Connell's offense from their 2022 and 2023 partnership in Minnesota, giving him a "low start-up cost," in the words of one high-ranking AFC executive. At 6-2, the Vikings' chances of winning a crowded NFC North live.

Vikings head coach Kevin O'Connell and QB Kirk Cousins, pictured in 2023, are running it back.

The Rams' 'F them picks' strategy staying relevant: The Los Angeles Rams famously sacrificed the future for their present en route to the 2021 NFL season's Super Bowl. They earned an "F them picks" reputation for bucking traditional wisdom, and they continued to prioritize proven stars over picks this summer when they sent the Browns Jared Verse alongside a 2027 first-, 2028 second- and 2029 third-round pick to acquire reigning Defensive Player of the Year Myles Garrett in June. The relevance now? Jacksonville Jaguars general manager James Gladstone came up in this league with the Rams and Los Angeles GM Les Snead. He has leaned into the ethos dating back to his first draft as GM, when he traded up for Travis Hunter, and he leaned into it again on Monday when he collected three new defensive players in one day — including two at steep prices.

The Jaguars aren't afraid to hamper their future to chase their window this year. So they sent the Eagles a 2027 first- and 2029 second-round pick for defensive tackle Jordan Davis; the Falcons a 2027 second- and 2028 fifth-round pick for safety Jessie Bates III; and the Saints a sixth-round pick (either 2027 or 2028, based on conditions) for defensive tackle Davon Godchaux. In Southern California, Snead was smiling.

Defensive tackles in vogue: Jeffery Simmons is not your average defensive tackle. But when the Chicago Bears became the first team in the modern draft era (since 1967) to pay two first-round picks for an interior defensive lineman, per ESPN research, the position received a well-deserved facelift. The Jaguars have since acquired two defensive tackles of their own, including a first-round pick in the deal for Davis. All of this comes in the shadows of the Cowboys paying big for Quinnen Williams at last year's deadline and the Cincinnati Bengals dealing the 10th overall pick of the 2026 NFL Draft for Dexter Lawrence II. Just as Christian McCaffrey and Saquon Barkley once reasserted running backs' place relative to receivers in a pass-happy NFL era, consider this the defensive tackle reassertion relative to edge rushers. Offenses can't stand when interior linemen blow up the pocket, the protection challenges far dicier than blocking for a talented edge rusher. Defensive tackles are also hard to find — and often better once seasoned by a few years in the NFL.

"Everybody wants a quarterback and if you don't have a quarterback, what's the best way you can impact a quarterback? By that middle pressure and interior line movement …" an AFC executive said Monday by phone. "When you get a good one — man, just like when you get a good quarterback, you try to keep them as long as you can or they don't come to the market."

The Chiefs' passing game: For three straight games now, no Chiefs receiver has hit 55 receiving yards. Injuries play a role, but the ceiling of Patrick Mahomes and Co.'s offense nonetheless needed more than 37-year-old Travis Kelce and superhuman running back Kenneth Walker III manufacturing a passing game. Acquiring Colts receiver Josh Downs at the deadline, the Chiefs give themselves more depth even if not a drastically higher ceiling. Trading 2027 fourth- and fifth-round picks for Downs and a 2027 seventh-round pick wasn't cheap relative to Downs' recent play. But consider that the Pittsburgh Steelers and San Francisco 49ers (and arguably the Los Angeles Rams) also needed a receiver, and the Chiefs paying a fourth and change to close the deal makes sense. Can Kansas City now catch the Denver Broncos in the AFC West?

The Titans' negotiating chops: Tennessee already impressed with its ability to net two first-round picks for Simmons. Then, on Tuesday afternoon before the deadline, the Titans traded safety Amani Hooker and a fifth-round pick to the Buffalo Bills for a second-round pick, per multiple reports. The Jessie Bates III deal likely outlined the parameters for the deal, and Hooker's league-best five interceptions for 114 yards this year helped. But the price was still rich for a player who hasn't been at the top of his position most of his career. Tennessee leveraged well the Bills' desire to keep pace in the AFC arms race.

Veteran Colts players wanting to win: A year ago, after nine games, the Colts were 7-2. This year: 2-7. They've already fired general manager Chris Ballard and are going nowhere this season. So godspeed to the two veterans on expiring contracts that were dealt with contenders. Defensive tackle DeForest Buckner reunited with the San Francisco 49ers club that drafted him in exchange for a 2027 fourth-round pick. Three-time All-Pro left guard Quenton Nelson joined the reigning Super Bowl champion Seattle Seahawks for a 2027 third- and 2028 fifth-round pick. The 49ers are a near lock to make the playoffs, at a 96% chance, per Next Gen Stats. The Seahawks face a more uphill battle in a tight division and conference, but Nelson's chance at competing is far greater than with Indianapolis. Each player earned more meaningful football with the trade; and the Colts, who were unlikely to re-sign either player when their contract expired in the spring, recouped draft capital and salary cap space.

The Eagles' quiet rebuild: Philadelphia had little choice but to trade receiver A.J. Brown in the offseason after he publicly made clear he wanted out and wasn't going to fall in line with quarterback Jalen Hurts. That haul — a 2028 first-round pick and 2027 fifth — began the Eagles' reset of cap space and draft capital as they pivot off an aging Super Bowl roster. The Eagles could have been more competitive this year if a rash of injuries, most notably blood clots in Hurts' lungs, didn't strike. But the injury-squandered year timed well with a retooling of resources for a roster that had been bursting at the seams. The Eagles have also since collected a 2028 second-round pick (with a fifth/seventh swap) for center Cam Jurgens as well as a 2027 first- and 2029 second-round pick for defensive tackle Jordan Davis. Adding two firsts and two seconds since June gives Philadelphia flexibility to build.

AFC South quarterbacks not named Trevor Lawrence: Monday was a rough day for C.J. Stroud, Daniel Jones and Cam Ward. The 6-2 Jaguars already hold double the number of wins as their nearest division foe (the Houston Texans are 3-5), and then they stockpiled Davis, Godchaux and Bates. Expect offenses to face more pressure at the line of scrimmage and more risk of turnovers against the Jaguars now: In his nine games with the Atlanta Falcons this season, Bates secured one interception, deflected four passes, forced three fumbles and returned one fumble for a touchdown. The Jaguars' 2027 second- and 2028 fifth-round picks were rich for a ninth-year player with half a year left on his contract. But Bates' play, and his veteran leadership in the secondary, will elevate the Jaguars' defensive ceiling into at least January.

Kevin O'Connell's QB whisperer reputation: Winning 64.5% of games in five years, and engineering multiple top-10 offenses, the Minnesota Vikings' Kevin O'Connell has proven he's a valuable head coach and play-caller. But two years ago, after Sam Darnold's resurgence in Minnesota, O'Connell was riding a reputation as a quarterback whisperer who would elevate and maximize any quarterback in his system. That luster has dimmed as the Vikings come off their third game this season without a touchdown. Minnesota thought it would spark Kyler Murray after acquiring the former No. 1 overall draft pick this spring, and he's instead currently 20th in passer rating as well as in and out of the lineup because of injury. Former second overall pick Carson Wentz played well last season in relief for Minnesota, but he has fallen off a cliff this year — most recently completing 32% of his pass attempts for 81 yards, two interceptions and a lost fumble in a 23-6 loss to the Buffalo Bills on Monday. And while it's disingenuous to say J.J. McCarthy would have solved the Vikings' QB woes in-house — some players simply need a change of scenery to find their footing, as McCarthy has with the Giants — Minnesota trading away a former first-round pick on his rookie deal for a fifth-round pick doesn't help perception as the club struggles to find an answer at the position. Perhaps a deep playoff run with Cousins will boost O'Connell's reputation in other areas.

The Colts' belief in Jonathan Taylor's market: Trading Buckner and Nelson never guaranteed the Colts would trade Jonathan Taylor. The prior two were veterans on expiring contracts; Taylor signed an extension in August and offers possible value for the 2027 Colts and beyond no matter who's coaching. So this loser column placement isn't to suggest the Colts should have dealt Taylor. But the Colts seemingly broadcasting their desired market for him appears to have backfired. League sources were well aware that Indianapolis wanted a Christian McCaffrey-like haul for Taylor: the equivalent of a 2027 second-, third- and fourth-round pick as well as a 2028 fifth-round pick. That was richer than any team could justify, so Taylor stays home. Was there a quieter way to go about this exploration, that put Taylor in less of an awkward position? "You play every game like it's your last …" Taylor said from his locker Sunday. "This place helped shape me into the man I am today, so I'm very grateful. So if it is, I'm very grateful for the city of Indianapolis." Narrator: It was not.

Brian Thomas Jr. fantasy managers: The Jaguars kept Thomas even as he averages 2.75 targets per game while seeing the field on just 43% of offensive snaps. The move means Thomas doesn't get the fresh start many believe would benefit him and likely doesn't get the on-field opportunities a team like the Kansas City Chiefs might have given him. A loss for Thomas and his fantasy managers, but a logical move according to league sources. Why? 1) Thomas' field-stretching ability opens up opportunities for his teammates in ways that could be hard to replicate without a player of his height, weight and especially speed. 2) If the Jaguars suffer an injury at the position, was a future draft pick worth a sudden weakness on the road to what the Jaguars believe can be the Super Bowl? And 3) The teams whose need was most likely to translate to strong pay for Thomas were the Chiefs and Pittsburgh Steelers. Would Jacksonville regret that during the AFC playoff bracket?

Jerry Jones' penchant for optimism: When the Cowboys were 2-4, team owner and general manager Jerry Jones was still optimistic enough to trumpet that "I would be active if we could get a chance to help the team this year." He still believed then in his offense's strength and his defense's capacity to improve. By this week, that optimism had faltered — not only because the Cowboys still sit beneath .500 at 4-5 but also because their last two performances against the Cardinals (an overtime loss) and the Colts (a narrow win) showed the Cowboys are playing more like the worst teams in the league than the best. "Overall," Jones told Dallas radio station 105.3 The Fan on Tuesday morning, "we're not playing very good." Could Dallas still make a run in a poor division? It's possible, with Next Gen Stats pitting their playoff chances at 31%. But paying handsomely for a player to help a year when the Cowboys look far behind the 49ers and entire NFC North wasn't wise. Not to mention the Cowboys already dealt a 2027 sixth- and 2028 second-round pick for cornerback Joey Porter Jr. in a contract year. Consider this week's patience a win for Jones' judgment, even if a loss for his unfailing optimism.

Fernando Mendoza's starting stretch: The 2026 first overall draft pick, Fernando Mendoza has already received more time to learn in the NFL than many rookie quarterbacks thrust into talent-shallow systems from the first week of their pro careers. In an ideal world, the Raiders would have given Mendoza still another month before starting. But receiving a third-round pick for Cousins was worth expediting Mendoza's timeline. Why a month? The Raiders face three tough defenses up next in the Seahawks, Denver Broncos and Cleveland Browns. After their bye, the schedule lightens to the Los Angeles Chargers, a second Broncos matchup, the Titans, Arizona Cardinals and a likely-resting-their-starters Chiefs team. Mendoza should benefit from head coach Klint Kubiak's sharp play-calling and talented pieces including center Tyler Linderbaum and tight end Brock Bowers. The Raiders' defense keeping Maxx Crosby at the deadline also should lower the threshold the offense needs to hit to win games. A blueprint Mendoza and the Raiders can hope for: Drake Maye's rookie season with the New England Patriots. Maye entered midseason with a struggling team (and particularly, a struggling offensive line) that won few games but created the foundation for him and the team to thrive in Year 2.

The Chargers' sense of reality: The 1-7 Titans, 2-7 Eagles and 2-7 Colts all gathered draft capital in recent days and weeks to bolster their future given this season's playoff hopes are lost. Why didn't the 0-8 Chargers, the league's only winless team for three weeks now, follow suit? Give the Chargers a morsel of credit for dealing linebacker Daiyan Henley just before the deadline. The Chargers sent their contract-year linebacker to the in-division Broncos in exchange for a 2027 fourth- and 2028 fifth-round pick, a person with knowledge of the deal confirmed to Yahoo Sports. But otherwise, there were head-scratching decisions. Why not offload veteran Khalil Mack's salary? Did the Chargers listen to offers on talented young edge Tuli Tuipulotu, even if his future value might not be worth shopping away? How about interior defensive lineman Dalvin Tomlinson as teams go all-in on defensive tackles? Much is wrong in Los Angeles right now, and the club has seemed unwilling to make changes at the staff or roster levels to fix it. The Chargers' trade-deadline passivity is just the latest reason to question Jim Harbaugh's grand plan.

#2907. Terms of trade for Andre Cisco changed; here's what Lions sent Jets

In their original announcement of the trade, the Detroit Lions said they had sent a sixth-round draft pick in 2028 to the New York Jets to acquire safety Andre Cisco.

Those terms have since been updated, according to the NFL's transaction wire Tuesday, Nov. 10. The Lions instead sent the Jets their sixth-round draft pick in 2029.

"All other conditions of the trade remain unchanged," the transaction wire read.

More: Lions quiet at trade deadline after earlier move for Andre Cisco

Detroit's move for Cisco, 26, was its only in-season trade in 2026. Lions coach Dan Campbell said Monday, "We've liked Cisco from afar. ... He's athletic, he's versatile, he knows our system, he knows the verbiage, so we feel like this is a guy that can help us here a little bit. We're excited."

Cisco has been playing under Jets head coach Aaron Glenn since 2025, allowing him to be familiar with Detroit's defense. Glenn was the defensive coordinator with the Lions from 2021-24, and his influence remains through current defensive coordinator Kelvin Sheppard, who Glenn mentored.

#2905. Patriots make no moves at the NFL trade deadline

A total of six trades took place on Tuesday before the NFL trade deadline, but none involving the New England Patriots.

Even though the team of head coach Mike Vrabel had been the subject of rumors for multiple weeks, no moves materialized at One Patriot Place. The trading window closed at 4 p.m. ET, and the Patriots let it do so quietly.

This means that the 5-4 team did neither add any outside talent to its roster nor remove any players through trade. The most recent change to their roster came on Monday, when they released rookie tight end Tanner Arkin.

This means that any outside reinforcements will have to come either from the free agency market, the waiver wire, or another team's practice squad. Of course, the Patriots are also counting on some internal changes to what is currently a 52-man roster following Arkin's release.

They have several players on injured reserve expected to come back at some point, including offensive lineman Greg Van Roten, whose 21-day return window was opened last week. In addition, the expectation is that wide receiver Romeo Doubs and left guard Alijah Vera-Tucker will also return in the near future; both are eligible to come back after the Patriots' game against the Detroit Lions this week.

On top of those players re-joining the team, it also is dealing with multiple injuries to players not on IR — including wide receiver A.J. Brown and running backs Rhamondre Stevenson and TreVeyon Henderson. Their return to full strength, whenever it will come, will also serve as a boost to their respective position groups.

While the Patriots did not join the action this time around, there were some notable moves across the NFL. The most impactful might have come from the Minnesota Vikings, who acquired quarterback Kirk Cousins from the Las Vegas Raiders — a move that means Cousins will face New England for a second time this season: after already squaring off against the team in a 45-17 defeat in Week 5, he will again travel to Foxborough in Week 14.

The NFL closing its trading window until the start of the 2027 league year next March also means that all players who are released will be subject to waivers. Before the deadline, only those with less than four seasons had to pass through the waiver wire to enter free agency.

#2903. Warriors have won eight straight games after blowing out Pelicans

SAN FRANCISCO – It might be too premature, but the Golden State Warriors look like one of the best teams in the Western Conference, let alone the NBA.

The early results indicate that at the very least, they are one of the hottest in the association. The Warriors have won eight straight games after blowing out the New Orleans Pelicans, 138-87, at Chase Center in San Francisco on Monday, Nov. 9. Stephen Curry and Brandin Podziemski each scored 25 points.

Golden State extended their win streak to eight games and advanced to 8-3 after an 0-3 start to the season which caused many to panic. Guard De'Anthony Melton told USA TODAY Sports for a split moment the team was in a "dark place" after the slow start.

"We knew we couldn't win games and stuff like that," Melton said after scoring 16 points in the win. "Nobody wants to see 0-3, but at the same time I think after we lost our third one, the next day the vibes were still high. Like we were 0-3 but we're still just enjoying being around, having fun, playing cards, stuff like that."

There was additional outside noise, chatter of where the Warriors might finish. They were already counted out to begin the year, projected to finish 41-41. When they got off to the 0-3 start, that's all they heard.

"It's hard not to hear it, but you don't ever play into it," Curry told USA TODAY Sports. "I know I've seen some memes with the towel over my head, staring into the distance, trying to figure out what, you know, we needed to do.  People put an existential crisis on those looks. We hate losing. So you want to respond to all of that.  You hear it, but, we've been around long enough to know, you gotta be able to block all that stuff out, just be who you are."

Everyone outside of the Bay Area might be surprised at the Warriors hot start. But within the team, they remained calm and poised. There was a needed regroup that contributed to their hot streak.

"We're doing all the things we need to to convince ourselves that we are the team we think we can (be)," Curry said. "You know, starting 0-3, like, nobody's panicking, but it's still a feeling that you don't like where you haven't been rewarded for the effort that you're putting in."

The jury is still out on whether the Warriors are turning the page to becoming a great team, but Curry likes the direction they're heading in.

"My take is that we're a very resilient team and that matters to become part of a good, maybe a great team down the stretch of the year," Curry said. "If you can withstand some rough nights, whether shooting the ball, turnovers, or whatever, still find a way to win... I'm proud of everything we're doing. We just gotta keep it going."

More: Warriors backcourt depth takes hit losing Brandon Williams, Will Richard

There have been enough games to have a sample size of the Warriors' potential. Their first three games weren't enough to write them off, but they did display some fixable bad habits. One of their main concerns was taking care of the basketball. The Warriors turned the ball over 45 times in their first three games of the season.

Forward Draymond Green was asked to assess where the team is at through 10 games. Green told USA TODAY Sports he'd give them a "B" grade.

"I think we have a lot of things we can get better at. We're still not quite where we want to be, as far as turning the ball over," Green said. "We're executing better, but the execution isn't where we want it to be. Defensively, we've gotten a lot better since game one. That's been kind of our calling card right now and allowing us to win these games."

"A lot of room for growth, but yeah, no one's complaining, either," he added. "We're pretty… excited about where we are as a team, but it's equally as exciting (that) we have so much room to grow."

The Warriors don't look like the same team that began 0-3. They've since cleaned up their game and put attention into the small details of the game.

Head coach Steve Kerr discussed the goal of being a top-10 team in both offense and defense. It's one of the traits that makes a championship team.

Golden State currently ranks 3rd in defensive rating (103.5) in the early 2026–27 NBA season, according to NBA Advanced Leaders.

"The whole thing is about our process," Kerr told USA TODAY Sports. "Can we take care of the ball?  Can we execute offensively, get good shots for each other? And then defend like we have been in recent games, taking care of transition defense, taking away the easy stuff. So, it's not magic. We have a formula, and we're trying to get the guys to play to that formula, that standard night in and night out. We're on a good path, but this will be a great test for us on this road trip, so we'll see how we can do it."

Golden State has to lean into a team identity where there's a reliance upon every player, 1 through 15, to be ready when their name is called. The Warriors suffered losses to Brandon Williams and Will Richard, who will both seemingly be out for some time.

"We got guys that have been around the league, we always want to encourage them, if somebody's down, be ready to play," Melton said. "No matter what, it's the NBA, and we're trying to win games as a team because at the end of the day when we hold up the trophy, that's for everybody. So I think it's always going to be next man up."

Melton added that Curry, Green and Al Horford have breathed confidence into whoever is coming in the game to make an impact.

"(Curry) spoke to the team after the game (against the Magic) about the contributions of the bench," Kerr said. "He mentioned how much trust that he and the vets have in the young guys, and so we've got a good thing going. We've got a good vibe. Guys are all competing for each other. Playing really hard."

Offensively, the Warriors rank 5th in the NBA in offensive rating at 116.7 points per 100 possessions early in the season. They are led by Curry, their longtime superstar, but rookie Yaxel Lendeborg is making his imprint on the team as well, averaging 21.4 points and 7.2 rebounds.

"Our second best player right now is a rookie," Green said about Lendeborg. "He's gonna continue to get better. And as you can see, how good he's already made us, as he gets better, we'll continue to get better. But I think we got a lot of room for growth, and that's exciting."

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: Warriors have won eight straight games after blowing out Pelicans

#2899. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2898. There's more to the Celtics vs. Sixers than Tatum and Brown

Heading into the Boston Celtics matchup against the Philadelphia 76ers on Tuesday, November 10, all eyes will be on the potential matchup between Jayson Tatum and Jaylen Brown. After all, the two All-Stars spent nine years establishing themselves as one of the best duos in the NBA, winning a championship in 2024, only for Brown to be traded earlier this summer.

Brown wasn't the only player to change teams during that trade, though. Paul George was shipped to Boston to make the salaries work, and is already looking like an ideal fit next to Tatum and within Joe Mazzulla's system. In fact, both George and Brown have enjoyed strong starts to their life with their respective new teams, and on Tuesday, both will likely be motivated to prove the opposing team wrong for parting ways with them.

Once you factor in the deep rivalry between Philadelphia and Boston, and the presence of long-time Celtics enemy LeBron James, you have some can't-miss television that will appeal to die-hards and casual fans alike.

In a recent video uploaded to YouTube by 98.5 The Sports Hub, Chris Gasper joined Mike Felger and Tony Massarotti to discuss the upcoming Celtics and Sixers game, looking at multiple angles that will draw attention, both leading into the game and throughout the night itself. The trio obviously spent some time talking about the relationship between Tatum and Brown, along with the impact George has made to begin the season, before turning their attention to the other matchups we will see throughout the night.

#2896. Steelers make no moves at NFL trade deadline

The Pittsburgh Steelers are 6-2 and sit atop the AFC North, making them a team that would be a prime candidate to make some moves and improve their roster for a playoff push ahead of Tuesday's 4:00 PM NFL trade deadline. However, the Steelers opted to sit on their hands and decide their current roster will have to be enough to defend their lead in the AFC North.

In what feels like a wide-open AFC, teams near the top of the standings all made major moves. The Jacksonville Jaguars, Denver Broncos, Buffalo Bills, and Kansas City Chiefs all made significant moves to improve their roster as playoff contenders.

Jacksonville added three players: Safety Jessie Bates and defensive tackles Davon Godchaux and Jordan Davis. The Broncos added linebacker Daiyan Henley in a trade with their division rivals, the Los Angeles Chargers. The Bills acquired the NFL's leader in interceptions, Amani Hooker, from the Tennessee Titans. And finally, the Chiefs landed wide receiver Josh Downs from the Indianapolis Colts.

To be fair to the Steelers, it was a quiet deadline for much of the AFC North. The only notable deal to come out of the division within the last week was when the Baltimore ravens acquired Jacoby Brissett from the Arizona Cardinals after the injury to Lamar Jackson. The Steelers were one of just two teams in the AFC to have six or more wins at this moment and make no moves at the trade deadline, as the Cincinnati Bengals made no moves ahead of the deadline.

The Steelers have a tendency to remain quiet at the deadline under general manager Omar Khan, and this time around was no different. Despite major needs at wide receiver and corner, Pittsburgh has opted to trust those in house to carry them through the rest of the season.

#2891. Colts grant Josh Downs’ request in trade to Chiefs for 2027 4th, 5th-round picks

The Indianapolis Colts continued to dismantle their roster until the final hour of Tuesday's NFL trade deadline, sending slot receiver Josh Downs, along with a seventh-round pick to the Kansas City Chiefs in exchange for a fourth and fifth-round pick in the 2027 NFL Draft. The aggressive fire sale marks the fourth trade the Colts have made to begin the rebuild during this disastrous campaign.

Indianapolis granted Downs' request and traded away a second starting receiver, along with Keenan Allen to the San Francisco 49ers, with intentions to stack as much draft capital as possible. The 25-year-old receiver made his request public in October, signaling that he was ready to leave Indy. The Colts did all four former players a solid by sending them to active contenders and it's fair compensation rather than losing Downs for nothing in free agency.

Downs' trade request came after weeks of frustration, while Indianapolis continued to sell off cornerstone pieces of its roster. In fact, each of the last four losses have prompted the organization to make a move. From the firing of GM Chris Ballard after the Titans loss, to the trades of two cornerstone captains on the front line after the Vikings loss, to the pair of starting receivers who were traded in the final hour before the deadline.

In Sunday's 27-23 loss to the Dallas Cowboys, Downs looked disengaged on the field and had two noticeable drops. It was a tough performance for a receiver on a contract year, knowing his final curtain call with the Colts was three catches for 24 receiving yards. Downs has collected just 33 catches for 354 receiving yards and one touchdown in nine games this season.

Indianapolis selected Downs in the third round (79th overall) of the 2023 NFL Draft. The North Carolina product instantly established himself as one of Indianapolis' most dependable targets, fetching 68 receptions for 771 yards and two touchdowns as a rookie. He made clear improvements in his second season and notably had the best footwork among the receiver corps throughout training camp. Downs improved with 72 catches for 803 yards and five touchdowns in his sophomore campaign.

However, his production has steadily declined since Daniel Jones became the Colts starting quarterback. Downs endured a slight increase in drops during the 2025 season and recorded 58 receptions for 566 yards and four touchdowns. It looked bright out the gate as Downs started off the opening drive of the 2026 season with nearly 40 receiving yards, but has been held in check in essentially every game since October.

Kansas City was desperate to add another weapon for franchise quarterback Patrick Mahomes II. The Chiefs aimed to acquire a young, talented receiver with a proven ability to create separation out of the slot. With injuries to Xavier Worthy (hamstring), and Tyquan Thorton (ankle) out on IR, Downs joins a receiver corps that features Rashee Rice, Jalen Royals and Nikko Remigio.

Two weeks ago, the Colts traded All-Pro left guard Quenton Nelson to Seattle, then sent All-Pro defensive tackle DeForest Buckner back to San Francisco. On Tuesday, Indianapolis sent veteran receiver Keenan Allen to San Francisco for a 2027 sixth-rounder, then Downs became the fourth player shipped out of Indianapolis to stock more draft capital.

Firing the GM of nearly a decade and trading away four starters in the span of three weeks is another reminder that the focus has entirely shifted toward the future. Downs wanted no part of what that future looked like in Indianapolis, so it was best to find a willing trade partner in the Chiefs.

#2889. Milwaukee Bucks vs. New York Knicks Preview & Game Thread: Rumble in the Bronx

The Milwaukee Bucks play the New York Knicks tonight in the first game of a back-to-back, still seeking their first road W of the season. Milwaukee's rookies continue to look solid, with Brayden Burries stringing together some impressive performances as a starter, and Nate Ament finding the floor more and making the most of his minutes. The same cannot be said for Milwaukee's bigs, however, as Myles Turner and Kel'el Ware have struggled lately and could use a bounce-back performance. The Knicks, meanwhile, are 5-4 and, despite their relative success, don't look close to what they did a few months ago on the biggest stage.

While it may not look like it at first, the Knicks are currently playing a more efficient, better version of the game the Bucks are playing. Both teams are heavily prioritizing rebounding and second-chance points, but over the last few games the two teams have swapped rankings: the Bucks were previously in the top five, and the Knicks have played to their strengths and now sit where the Bucks were. Even more striking is that, beyond Karl-Anthony Towns, who is off to an All-NBA-caliber start to the season, the Knicks' frontcourt rotation is breaking down and has struggled overall. Landry Shamet is their current backup PF, and Andre Drummond hasn't looked up to the level, struggling to finish at the rim and shooting just 42% from the field.

If it is a mirror, though, it may be a mirror into the future; one where a fully formed team can still make its lack of depth work. Despite these front-court limitations, the Knicks can still find their spots and shoot at a high percentage and volume. When you look at their shot charts on 3Step, it is clear that even when you know where they want to go, it doesn't matter: they get to their spots and hit their shots. This is largely because of Captain Clutch himself, Jalen Brunson, who is leading the Knicks' offense at 115.7 points per 100 possessions. Meanwhile, OG Anunoby is anchoring a stellar defense, holding opposing teams to 107.9 points per 100 possessions. There is no way around it: the Bucks will have their hands full trying to both contain New York's offense and break through its defense. That said, should one of the Bucks' guards be able to set the table better than we've seen in the last couple of games for the offense, and they win or keep close the rebound and second-chance points battles, there is a real chance Milwaukee can pull out the W. While it may sound lofty, the Bucks did both of those things against Minnesota and Toronto to carry leads late in the game.

Going for a two-for-one here: both Kel'el Ware and Myles Turner need a get-right game. Turner arguably needs it more as a veteran, but both bigs have left a lot to be desired. There are probably many ways to dissect how bad it has been, but the eye test is enough; against Boston, Ware got tossed around by Robinson, and Queta had his way against Turner. If either big man bounces back tonight, it would go a long way toward the Bucks making it close late.

Who: Milwaukee Bucks (1-9) vs. New York Knicks (5-4).

Where: Madison Square Garden—New York City, NY.

Brayden Burries has been a major bright spot in an otherwise dreary stretch of basketball, and I expect him to be ready for the bright lights of the Big Apple with a 20/10/5 game!

#2888. Does anyone feel safe from layoffs right now?

In late October, Bashel Lubarsky had a feeling something was off when she got a DM from her co-worker on the way to work. Once she opened her email and saw a note announcing layoffs, Lubarsky suspected she'd been cut.

Minutes later, Lubarsky stepped off the subway to join an impromptu Zoom meeting with HR and got laid off in the middle of downtown Brooklyn, New York.

Up until that point, Lubarsky, 28, says she held two ideas in her head: On the one hand, she had a feeling the company would be going through another round of October layoffs considering it had done the same in 2025. On the other, she felt pretty secure in her job as a graphic designer and illustrator. She'd gotten a promotion earlier in the year, had essential skills used across multiple projects and jokes that she was likely the "cheapest" member of her team. "I never thought my job wasn't safe," Lubarsky tells CNBC Make It.

Lubarsky's experience hits home for many Americans these days. Despite official numbers saying the job market is relatively stable, with new jobs opening up, increased hiring and a steady unemployment rate, an increasing number of workers can't shake the feeling that layoffs are just around the corner and that nothing — not an artificial intelligence-related job, stellar performance or company success — is enough to keep them safe.

According to the American Psychological Association's spring survey of 2,009 employed adults, some 32%, or 1 in 3, U.S. workers say they're concerned about losing their job in the next few weeks or months, up from 26% in 2025.

U.S. employee engagement fell to 29% in the third quarter of 2026, its lowest level since 2011, according to Gallup. And employee confidence fell to its lowest point in September since Glassdoor began measuring it in 2016; confidence rebounded slightly in October, per Glassdoor's latest analysis, but remains near record lows.

Despite the sour outlook, layoffs are less severe today than they were a year ago. Through October, employers announced 617,189 job cuts, down 44% from the number of layoff notices in the first 10 months of 2025, according to data from outplacement firm Challenger, Gray and Christmas.

The biggest cuts this year are happening in tech, transportation and food, per the report, with companies citing AI as a leading reason for layoffs.

It's not that companies are outright replacing employees with AI, but rather they're "shifting investment and headcount from one part of the business to another" focused on AI, says Daniel Zhao, chief economist at Glassdoor.

"Unfortunately for many workers, that resource reallocation story is a scarier one because it doesn't really matter whether your job can be done by AI or can't be done by AI," Zhao says. "It only matters whether the projects that you're working on are part of the company's strategy moving forward in an AI world."

Working on AI-related efforts still may not provide security if a company shifts priorities in how it wants to use the new tech. Briana Taylor says she felt secure in her product marketing manager job at Microsoft since it was related to the company's AI products. She says she received "exceeded expectations" remarks on her performance review, scoring her a bonus and level promotion, months before being laid off in June 2025.

"I feel like right now companies need something to blame, so they're using AI as a scapegoat," says Taylor, 35, of Chicago. "All the layoffs seem to be happening out of left field."

The surprise exit influenced her next career moves. Taylor says she took a pay cut in her next job, and she also launched her own AI consultancy for small businesses, dubbed Rethink to Rise AI, as a safety net.

Much of today's stock market growth is being propped up by expectations of what AI can do as an industry and how that will shape the rest of the economy, says Jo-Ellen Pozner, associate professor of management at Santa Clara University's Leavey School of Business.

That's putting pressure on tech companies in particular: "You've got to put up results," she says. "And in the absence of a blockbuster new product introduction, lowering costs is probably the only way to do that."

The introduction of AI and new forms of automation "make it that much more tempting for companies to just get rid of extra headcount," Pozner says.

That sometimes means companies are reducing headcount even if they're showing other signs of doing well. Microsoft offered voluntary buyouts to 60,000 employees in October days before reporting 19% revenue growth year over year in the quarter ending Sept. 30. American Express and Barclays announced 5% cuts to their respective workforces even as the financial services sector sees profits from growing consumer spending and resilient credit.

Looming layoffs create an "intensely destabilizing" work environment, where employees lacking psychological safety divert their energy from creative and innovative work into their anxiety, Pozner says. "It's very counterproductive, and I think quite short-sighted."

In some cases, layoff signals could lead workers to go into overdrive to keep themselves safe. When layoffs are a moving target, it's not always a guarantee.

Weeks ago, Sam Epperson, 33, in Colorado Springs, Colo., was told his tech sales team was behind their goals for the year and took it as guidance to pick up the pace. By early October, Epperson says he was able to close a number of deals amounting to $300,000 to bridge the gap.

It wasn't enough, and he was laid off before the end of the month. "I very much so felt safe in my position until I was let go," he says.

While economists say there are signs of a slowly improving job market, such as jobs being added to the economy and people feeling confident enough to quit their jobs, "we really are going to need to see consistent improvement in the job market to really make workers feel like things are looking up again," says Zhao of Glassdoor.

For her part, Lubarsky in New York City is scaling her own graphic design and illustration client book while looking for a new job. Her outlook isn't great.

Lubarsky says she's been applying to jobs for over a year, even while employed. "I am hardworking. I have so many recommendations, referrals [and] clients. I have everything you could need. I can't even get a response saying, 'No, thank you,'" she says.

It feels worse than the Covid-19-era job market she entered after college, she says: "I graduated in 2020 into the pandemic, and I still got a job within a year."

#2887. Win Now, Draft Later: Examining the Jaguars’ 2027 Draft Board

The Jacksonville Jaguars made their intentions clear on Monday. In a single day, they traded for safety Jessie Bates III, defensive tackle Davon Godchaux, and defensive tackle Jordan Davis – three moves clearly aimed at winning now. The cost shows up in the future draft columns, which are now considerably thinner than normal.

Here is exactly what Jacksonville has left, where the rest went, and why the blueprint looks so familiar to anyone who watched general manager James Gladstone's former team operate.

First round: Sent to the Philadelphia Eagles, along with a 2029 second-round pick, for defensive tackle Jordan Davis

Second round: Sent to the Atlanta Falcons, along with a 2028 fifth-round pick, for safety Jessie Bates III

Sixth round: Previously sent to the New York Giants in August for offensive lineman Daniel Faalele.

Assuming no offseason trades, the 2027 Jaguars draft will result in consecutive years without a first-round selection for Jacksonville. Jacksonville's 2026 first-round pick went to Cleveland as part of the 2025 package that moved the Jaguars up to No. 2 to select Travis Hunter.

Round 5 (projected compensatory pick: LB Devin Lloyd)

Round 6 (projected compensatory pick: RB Travis Etienne)

Round 6* (projected compensatory pick: CB Greg Newsome)

Compensatory picks are not awarded until the offseason, so those selections remain projections until the weeks before the draft.

The Jaguars sent the Saints a conditional 2028 sixth-round pick for Godchaux. However, that pick can become a 2027 sixth-rounder if certain performance conditions are met. Since Jacksonville no longer owns its own 2027 sixth, any 2027 sixth it eventually acquires would go to New Orleans, per reports. In practice, that means one of the projected compensatory sixth-round picks could be gone. At worst, the Jaguars would still have one left over if projections hold.

Jaguars GM James Gladstone spent nine seasons with the Los Angeles Rams, beginning in 2016 as a senior assistant to general manager Les Snead and working his way up to the team's director of scouting strategy. Head coach Liam Coen also worked for the Rams. The Rams' approach under Snead is famous for their "F— them picks" philosophy. Los Angeles went so far as to go seven straight years, 2017 through 2023, without a first-round pick, and two of those seasons without a first or second-round selection (2018 and 2022).

This season the Rams traded a 2027 first-round pick, plus Jared Verse and later-round picks, for linebacker Myles Garrett, and previously sent a 2026 first for cornerback Trent McDuffie. Jacksonville has now done the same with Hunter and Davis.

Overall, Los Angeles traded a first-round pick away nine times in 10 drafts, and the Jaguars are clearly believers in this process. The preference for locking in bona fide talent now, over the prospect of a first-round selection in the 20-32 range, seems to be a core tenet in both front offices.

It's quite possible that the Rams and Jaguars both view this 2027 draft class similarly; a class that was once thought to be extremely deep on top-end talent one year ago now has shown some concern about proving that depth on the field. Multiple top prospects, primarily the quarterback class, have somewhat struggled, which could push up talent in other position groups in this class. The Rams are down to four 2027 picks:

The Jaguars' list is clearly similar, though not exactly the same, with no first or second round pick for Jacksonville.

When the Rams had no first-round pick in 2023, they still landed Kobie Turner and Byron Young in the third round and Puka Nacua in the fifth. That kind of Day 3 value is the bet Gladstone is clearly making, with five to six Day 3 selections still in the bank for 2027. Slight differences also exist in how each team landed on their 2027 draft inventory. The Rams kept a second-round pick in 2027, while Jacksonville has none. Jacksonville, though, holds more total selections once compensatory picks are counted.

Jacksonville 2025 Day 3/UDFA Contributors

Jacksonville 2026 Day 3/UDFA Contributors

Jacksonville, operating at the deadline, as a year-in and year-out playoff team, is betting that proven talent beats draft uncertainty. With a defense that already ranks fifth against the run and second in scoring entering trade deadline week, the Jaguars are clearly All-In on 2026. If the Rams' draft-and-develop model holds, Gladstone's late-round picks (and undrafted free agency) will have to do the heavy lifting in 2027 and beyond. The Jaguars will find out whether that works soon enough, but the early results are promising.

Former Yankees pitcher Anthony Misiewicz has been released by the Cincinnati Reds.

GettyNEW YORK, NEW YORK – JUNE 19: Anthony Misiewicz #58 of the New York Yankees reacts after an out against the Baltimore Orioles during the ninth inning at Yankee Stadium on June 19, 2024 in the Bronx borough of New York City. (Photo by Luke Hales/Getty Images)

Misiewicz's time with New York officially ended following the 2024 season when he elected free agency.

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2884. Random Raptor Review: Remember when Voshon Lenard outscored Michael Jordan?

More than 300 people have worn Raptors' jerseys in the NBA. Not all of them are well known. So we randomly picked a former Raptor to profile in the year 2026. Here is the story of Voshon Lenard.

I would assume the answer to be, "not much." Which is a shame, because the more you dig into what Lenard's career on the Raptors looks like, the more fascinating things get.

TORONTO, CANADA: Washington Wizards' forward Michael Jordan (R) gets tangled with teammate Christian Laettner and Raptor guard Voshon Lenard (L) during second half NBA action against the Raptors in Toronto on 15 December, 2002. Washington defeated Toronto 95-82, despite Jordan's career-tying game low of two points. AFP PHOTO/J.P. Moczulski (Photo credit should read J.P. MOCZULSKI/AFP via Getty Images) | AFP via Getty Images

Lenard was a 6'4" shooting guard who played 11 seasons in the NBA, and suited up for Toronto in the 2002-2003 season. He played 63 games and started 24 of them, playing more than 30 minutes a night. He had his highest career scoring average this year, with 14.3 points per game. By that point, Lenard's sharpshooting abilities cooled off after a blistering start on the Miami Heat, but he could still be called upon to knock down a three.

And the Raptors needed every one of them they could get in this injury riddled season, where Vince Carter missed significant time due to injury, and for the first time in three years, Toronto did not make the playoffs. All together, while Lenard was a solid piece for the Raps, he joined the franchise at the beginning of the "bad times," and left before things improved.

That year, however, Voshon Lenard did what most NBA players of the day could only dream to do, and that was to literally beat Michael Jordan at his own game. On March 5th, 2003, Lenard was the Raptors' second leading scorer in a victory against the Wizards, where he netted 15 points, making two three-pointers.

That same game, Michael Jordan only scored four. And sure, that might have been because of back spasms, but as any Raptors fan knows, he never could have stood against the Lenard onslaught either way.

In 2020, Rob Williams of the Daily Hive ranked Voshon Lenard as the fifth best of the 'one-hit wonders' that the Raptors ever had, owing to his scoring production in 2003.

Bleacher Report interviewed him post-retirement in 2011. There, he talked about his charity work, and the state of the Miami Heat at the time. When asked about a possible return to professional basketball, he answered simply, saying that "I thought my time was up. Now I'm just trying to sit back and enjoy life with my family."

In some ways, Lenard is living the dream. The man carved out a niche for himself as a professional athlete, retired after making more than $25 million dollars, and kept his family close while still remaining active in meaningful work. This former Raptor made it out of the NBA alright, and should be remembered for his contributions to Toronto sports history, and whipping MJ and the Wizards really good that one time.

Thanks Voshon Lenard, wherever you are. Raptors fans haven't forgotten you.

#2883. Ex-Google recruiter: You're answering 'Tell me about yourself?' all wrong, and other job interview mistakes—do this instead

In a job market that can seem impossible to break into, relationships still matter and are often what will get your foot in the door. But if your coffee chats and informational interviews keep ending with "Let's stay in touch," and then nothing happens, you might be wondering where you went wrong.

I've spent more than a decade recruiting for companies like Google, TikTok, Uber and The New York Times. I've found that when people try to network, one of the biggest things that trips them up is the seemingly simple question: "Tell me about yourself?" It can unlock referrals, more introductions and interviews — but only if you know how to answer it.

When someone recommends you for a role at their company, they are often asked to fill out a form where they write how they know you and why you'd be good at the job. That note is where your coffee chat either turns into an interview or goes nowhere.

So the next time you are meeting with someone who does the work you want to do, or is employed at your dream company, remember that you're not there to impress them. You want to help them answer the question they will be asked afterward, which is "Why should we talk to this person?"

Here's my best advice for how to navigate three of the most common job interview mistakes.

When they hear "So, tell me about yourself," most people answer with a list of their accomplishments. The problem with that is that it gives your potential referrer nothing to hold on to. That's the wrong approach. Instead, break down the answer into three parts.

This is how it should sound when you combine them: "I'm a supply chain analyst. I reached out because you moved from operations into strategy and that's exactly what I would like to do."

That's under 30 words and it gives your informational interviewer a clear place to start when they recommend you. "Supply chain analyst moving from operations into strategy" is the first line of your referral note, and you just wrote it for them.

You've cleared that first hurdle, but the next mistake people often make is that they will ask: "What is a typical day for you?"

This will only get you a vague, unhelpful description of their responsibilities. A stronger question is: "What part of your job do you find to be the most rewarding?" When you ask this, they have to think about a specific moment, instead of a general list. This way you will find out what the job feels like on a good day, which the job description will never tell you.

Follow that up with "How is your performance measured at the end of the year?" When a hiring manager opens a new role, in my experience, one of the first things the recruiter asks them is, "What does success look like in the first 30 days and in the first 90 days?" But almost none of that makes it into the job description either.

With this approach, you get specific and quantified details that will help you better frame your expertise in an interview setting.

You're almost there, but now another trap is waiting for you. You're talking with someone you admire and whose expertise you value, so an understandable question is: "What advice would you give to someone who is just starting out?"

Please don't do this. It will generally only yield a response like "work hard" or "be curious." To get the information that will actually help you in the next phase of your job hunt, ask these questions instead:

Before your meeting ends, make sure to ask "Based on what we discussed today, is there anyone else you think I should talk to?" It's important to highlight the discussion part first. Without it, you're asking them to go through everyone they know and pick someone at random.

They'll probably say "Let me think about it." But with the first part, you've narrowed it down for them. The worst they can say is "Sorry, I can't think of anyone," and that's fine. But ask every time. That's how one coffee chat can lead to introductions, referrals and interviews.

Farah Sharghi is a technical recruiter, career coach and public speaker. She has worked as a talent acquisition specialist for Google, Lyft, Uber, TikTok and The New York Times. In addition to her work as a recruiter and career advisor, she is also a content creator whose posts on job hunting, interview prep and salary negotiations can be viewed on YouTube, Instagram, LinkedIn and TikTok.

#2881. Trump's 2028 successors are already fighting over who to blame for GOP midterm wipeout

Republicans are still reckoning with a historic midterm wipeout, but the fight to assign blame and inherit President Donald Trump's political movement is already underway.

Vice President JD Vance faults the war in Iran. Secretary of State Marco Rubio defends it. Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis says Republicans fought the wrong battles. Texas Sen. Ted Cruz, R-Texas, argues the party lost touch with voters' economic struggles.

The competing visions from potential presidential contenders offer an early glimpse of the Republican infighting ahead of 2028. But all face the same dilemma: How to convince voters the party can change course without alienating Trump or the MAGA base they need to win the nomination.

The biggest obstacle may be Trump himself, who has shown little appetite for a political retreat.

On Monday, Trump made a surprise appearance in the White House briefing room, declaring the Iran war "militarily is effectively over" while maintaining that the U.S. remains prepared to resume strikes. Earlier that day, he questioned the legitimacy of Republicans' midterm defeat, suggesting without evidence that voting machines were responsible, days after saying that after years of election denial, he would accept the 2026 results.

Since the election, Trump has also replaced Chief of Staff Susie Wiles with immigration hard-liner Stephen Miller and backed Republican National Committee Chairman Joe Gruters against a challenger demanding an accounting of the party's losses.

Trump is also keeping his would-be successors guessing. In a CBS interview Sunday, he praised Rubio alongside Vance but suggested he might stay out of the 2028 Republican primary.

"Maybe I'm better off not getting involved," he said, while emphasizing the weight his endorsement could carry.

That dynamic leaves Vance in a precarious position. Entering the midterms as the clear political heir, Vance now faces a critical question: Can he inherit MAGA without inheriting the blame for Trump's presidency?

In a lengthy post on X Wednesday as election results were still trickling in, Vance challenged Trump's initial explanation for the Republican defeat.

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance wrote, arguing voters who backed the Trump-Vance ticket in 2024 abandoned Republicans in 2026 over Iran.

Pointing to foreign policy allows Vance to criticize a defining administration decision without repudiating the broader MAGA movement, while echoing his longstanding opposition to foreign interventions. Vance also has national name recognition and donor ties to help his prospective candidacy.

But the election results complicate Vance's argument.

Just 34% of House voters called the war crucial to their vote, versus 49% citing the cost of living and 55% government corruption, according to the CNBC Voter Poll – suggesting a broader backlash against the Trump administration that Vance has spent nearly two years defending.

"You can't campaign against a war from within an administration you serve," conservative commentator Joel Pollak wrote on X, questioning how Vance could distance himself from a conflict he had publicly defended.

In his CBS interview, Trump said he did not disagree with Vance's assessment and acknowledged the vice president had discussed the post with him beforehand. But he insisted the decision to attack Iran was right and suggested Vance also has losses of his own to explain.

After Vance spent months fundraising and campaigning as Republican National Committee finance chairman, Democrats won both marquee races in his home state of Ohio. Amy Acton defeated Vance ally Vivek Ramaswamy for governor, after the vice president defended a controversial ad featuring Acton's estranged mother. Former Sen. Sherrod Brown claimed Vance's former Senate seat, defeating incumbent Republican Jon Husted.

Rep. Randy Fine, R-Fla., stated Sunday he would not support Vance in 2028 due to his war criticism, calling it a betrayal of Trump. "You have to be loyal to those people who brought you to the dance," Fine said in an interview with LiveNOW from FOX on Sunday, naming Rubio and Cruz as alternatives.

Rubio is making a different bet: Defend the Iran war while distancing himself from the Republican defeat.

"Sometimes, when you do the right thing, you don't know whether it's popular or not, but it's still the right thing to do," Rubio told reporters Sunday before departing for France.

Rubio, who has denied he plans to run in 2028, largely avoided the campaign trail and has stayed out of the immediate blame game. But his dual role as secretary of state and national security advisor ties him directly to Trump's foreign policy record, which could face renewed scrutiny when Democrats take control of the House. The House may vote this week on articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth in a rebuke of the Trump administration's foreign policy.

DeSantis, who is not in the Trump administration, is questioning the party's entire playbook.

The outgoing Florida governor called the midterms "close to an extinction-level event" for Republicans at a news conference Wednesday in Tallahassee, Florida, accusing Washington of failing to deliver on its promises while fighting culture wars that no longer resonated with voters.

"Republicans ... really were fighting yesterday's battle," DeSantis said at the news conference.

It was a striking critique from a governor who built his national profile on those same fights.

DeSantis can point to Republicans' ability to retain control of Florida even as Democrats flipped longtime GOP strongholds elsewhere. But Republican Byron Donalds won the governor's race by just 1.3 points, while Democrats gained ground in the Legislature.

Still, Rep. Greg Steube, R-Fla., credited DeSantis with building a "massive sea wall against the blue tsunami" in an X post.

The scramble to succeed Trump extends well beyond Vance, Rubio and DeSantis.

Cruz called the midterms a "Watergate-level repudiation" in a post on X Wednesday, blaming Trump's tariffs and the prolonged Iran war. Other potential 2028 contenders include Georgia Gov. Brian Kemp, Arkansas Gov. Sarah Huckabee Sanders and former Virginia Gov. Glenn Youngkin, who face the same challenge of moving beyond Trump without alienating his base.

Some wealthy Republican donors are already looking beyond Vance, weighing DeSantis and other alternatives amid concerns that a candidate from Trump's administration would inherit the political baggage of his second term and low voter approval ratings.

The divisions extend to Trump's own coalition. Among voters who backed Republican House candidates, roughly two-thirds of those under 30 approved of Trump's performance, much less than the nearly nine in 10 voters 65 and older, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Younger Republicans were also more skeptical of the Iran war and U.S. support for Israel, according to the exit poll data.

"If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so," Vance said Wednesday. "Agree or disagree, that's at least honest. But everyone knows why we lost."

#2879. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2878. Kirk Cousins trade gives Justin Jefferson, Vikings' offense a fantasy lifeline

Hayden Winks reacts to the 2026 NFL trade deadline through the lens of fantasy football. Let's examine the Minnesota Vikings reuniting with veteran QB Kirk Cousins in a trade with the Las Vegas Raiders.

First off, great job by the Raiders for signing Cousins this offseason to not just be Fernando Mendoza's mentor but also to help support him with a 2027 third-round pick. That could easily be an interior lineman in the next draft, something they desperately need. The Raiders will also have eight games to see what they have with Mendoza in a totally different offense than what he had in college. Mendoza will make some big mistakes, but this will be exciting to watch, and the Raiders are 4-5, so these games absolutely count on their end. Well done, Vegas!

Cousins has been so much better than the Vikings' QBs this year. Here's a chart to showcase that:

Cousins has passable arm strength, even if it's his clear weakness on tape. He had a couple of amazing post routes to Tre Tucker this season, but his best throws have been "Make a play, Brock Bowers" balls in traffic. Cousins has had to play under pressure behind that porous Raiders offensive line, and the Klint Kubiak offense stresses deep passes off play-action. It's not an easy offense to execute without high-end receiver play, yet Cousins has been the QB12 in EPA per dropback, QB10 in dropback success rate, QB18 in completion percentage over expected and QB13 in PFF passing grade. I'd call him a slightly above-average starter based on his film, too.

Kyler Murray was the QB31, QB29, QB12 and QB20 in those metrics, and him hanging around as an emergency option while learning from a veteran who has played well in this offense can't hurt.

While this trade keeps the Vikings' Super Bowl odds alive with an inarguable top-two defense, the real story for us is saving Justin Jefferson. The career splits are very, very encouraging. In four seasons with Kirk Cousins, Jefferson averaged 16.1 half PPR points per game, which would be WR7 this year. Without Cousins throughout his career, JJ had averaged 12.5 fantasy points (WR16). This year? Oh boy, 11.4 (WR21). It's dangerous to assume an older version of Cousins will get Jefferson back to those mid-range WR1 highs with the Vikings running the ball more now than then, but fringe WR1/2 value is officially back on the menu. I would've treated him as the WR16-22 or so if it was Murray only.

Take Bowers' production as an example. Bowers' success rate on throws sits at 61%, while Jefferson is at 48%. This is partially because of the catchable target rate, which Bowers clears at 73% versus Jefferson's 69%. In terms of catchable targets per game, which is a better metric here because of how many turn-down throws Murray had in this offense, the gap widens. Bowers averaged 7.3 catchable targets per game with Cousins. Jefferson is at 5.1.

I'll be tracking if the Vikings stay so balanced on offense here, too. It's understandable to hide the QB with this defense, but if Kevin O'Connell trusts his QB again, will they throw more often? The Vikings are 25th in neutral pass rate (48%) right now. They were at 57% in 2022-23 with Cousins (fourth).

Does the Cousins upgrade mean Jordan Addison and T.J. Hockenson are fantasy starters? It's possible!

Addison hasn't played well individually on tape this entire year, aside from a couple of downfield shots. His inability to consistently beat man coverage has meant more hardcore coverage on Jefferson. He's the WR65 on WR49 usage this year. He was the WR12 on WR23 usage in seven games with Cousins back in 2023 but that was also years ago!

Hockenson has been better this year after a couple of seasons of forgettable play coming off a significant injury. Back in 2022-23, Hockenson was at the peak of his powers. He was the TE2, only behind Travis Kelce. He averaged 8.4 targets per game on a 24% target share. Those are numbers that he can only dream of now, but Hockenson's starting point right now is the TE11 on TE9 usage. I'm open to him being a top-eight option moving forward.

What I don't anticipate is Jordan Mason and Aaron Jones Sr. separating from each other enough that they are more than desperate RB3/FLEX types. They've split the role since Mason's return, leaving Mason as the RB45 in expected half PPR points and Jones as the RB54. Those numbers will trend up to the 30s, but that's enough to compete in the consolation bracket only.

Exciting times in fantasy football land. Hopefully your Vikings picks can be saved enough to enjoy a fifth- or sixth-seed placement in the playoffs.
