#2877. FDA warns of vision loss risk linked to popular GLP-1 drugs like Wegovy, Zepbound

The U.S. Food and Drug Administration is requiring new safety warnings on all GLP-1 drugs after finding evidence of a potential increased risk of a rare eye condition, the agency said on Monday.

The condition, which can cause sudden vision loss, known as nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION), is rare, and a causal relationship has not been established.

The FDA is requiring the makers of all GLP-1 medications to add a warning about the potential increased risk of NAION associated with use of these medications, it said.

NAION is a condition that can result in sudden decreased vision, including permanent vision loss. It is caused by decreased blood flow to the front part of the ​optic nerve — the underlying cause is not completely understood, however.

NAION cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 products, the FDA said.

Ozempic and Wegovy maker Novo Nordisk said it "continues to believe the data do not establish a cause-and-effect relationship, and we remain confident in the benefit-risk profile of our GLP-1 medicines."

CNBC has reached out to Eli Lilly, the company behind Mounjaro, Zepbound and Foundayo, for comment.

Diabetes itself is a known risk factor for NAION, complicating efforts to determine if cases among GLP-1 users are caused by the medications or by patients' underlying health conditions.

The FDA decision follows a review of clinical trials, real-world patient data, and published studies.

An analysis of 126 trials involving more than 134,000 patients found 6.4 cases of NAION per 100,000 patient-years among GLP-1 users, compared with 3.8 among patients receiving other treatments or placebos.

The FDA said it identified 15 probable NAION events, 10 in patients treated with GLP-1s and five in patients receiving a placebo, and no cases were identified among patients receiving other non-GLP-1 glucose-lowering medications.

Several observational studies also identified an increased risk, supporting the FDA's concerns.

The agency's findings were more mixed when comparing the risk of developing NAION from the use of GLP-1 drugs versus other commonly prescribed diabetes drugs.

Real-world patient data found a statistically significant increase in the risk of NAION among GLP-1 users compared with patients taking DPP-4 inhibitors, another class of diabetes medications, but not compared with those taking SGLT-2 inhibitors, per the FDA. SGLT-2 inhibitors are another class of medicines that includes Jardiance and Farxiga.

However, observational studies consistently reported an increased risk of NAION in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus who started a GLP-1 treatment compared to those who initiated DPP-4 inhibitors or SGLT-2 inhibitors, the FDA added.

The FDA's class-wide labeling action is "likely to attract attention but should have limited impact on GLP-1 uptake," according to Citi analysts.

It may temporarily increase patient and investor concern, but the rarity of NAION and substantial weight, glycemic, cardiovascular, renal, and sleep apnea benefits should preserve a favorable benefit-risk profile and limit changes to prescribing the medicines, they added.

While GLP-1 drugs have been shown to lead to benefits beyond weight loss and controlling blood sugar levels, many also carry the FDA's strongest safety warning, known as a boxed warning, over a potential risk of thyroid tumors.

Studies have shown that semaglutide, the active ingredient in Wegovy and Ozempic, and tirzepatide, the active ingredient in Mounjaro, cause thyroid C-cell tumors in rodents. It remains unknown whether the drugs cause these tumors, including a rare form of thyroid cancer, in humans.

Both semaglutide and tirzepatide also carry warnings about diabetic retinopathy, another eye condition that can lead to blurred vision, difficulty seeing in low light, and, eventually, sight loss. Diabetic retinopathy is when high blood sugar damages the tiny blood vessels in the retina, the light-sensitive tissue at the back of the eye.

In a two-year trial involving patients with type 2 diabetes and high cardiovascular risk, diabetic retinopathy complications occurred in 3% of patients treated with Ozempic, compared with 1.8% of those receiving a placebo, according to the drug's prescribing information.

Mounjaro's FDA label separately warns that rapid improvements in blood sugar control have been associated with temporary worsening of diabetic retinopathy and recommends monitoring patients with a history of the condition.

European regulators flagged a risk of NAION associated with semaglutide in June 2025.

The European Medicines Agency's safety committee concluded that NAION was a very rare side effect of semaglutide that may affect up to 1 in 10,000 people taking it, and recommended updating the drug's product information. The U.K.'s medicines regulator followed with a similar warning in February.

Unlike the FDA's latest action, which covers all GLP-1 medications, the European and British warnings focused specifically on semaglutide.

#2876. Knicks Bulletin: ‘Typically, there’s some ulterior motives and personal agendas’

The Bucks could, but they might not, because they're likely so bad that they would play something close to what we know as basketball, but not entirely the same thing.

Here's a bunch of quotes from Monday's practice.

On how experimenting with different lineups helps the Knicks:

"It builds trust, because it's like, 'Hey, they trust me to go out there and play right now, even though I might not have played in two or three games.' It helps with connectivity because guys feel more part of it, because they're getting an opportunity. It helps with everybody's readiness because the reality of it is, if a guy is sitting on the bench and he's been there game after game after game, which happens sometimes — I'm not saying that will never happen because it's gonna happen sometimes — then he may lose a little interest and relax a little bit because it's like, 'Hey, I'm not going in.' But then, if you call their number every once in a while, throw them in a game, now they know, 'Oh, I gotta be ready.' "

On preparing the Knicks for unexpected lineup changes during the playoffs:

"It builds a lot, when you're talking about building a team. And then at the end of the day, your guys get used to it, so come playoff time, if you try something new or different when it comes to the lineups, it's not like, 'Oh my God, what's going on? We've never done this.' Nah, we've done it."

On what the Knicks learned from their uneven start to the season:

"The group is resilient. [We learned] that it's gonna be hard. This is a new year, new season and new challenges, so we have to embrace all the little details while trying not to have much slippage in our process because nobody is handing anything to us. And if we want to have success, which we're more than capable of, that's what some of these wins show you, that we have to go earn it."

On Karl-Anthony Towns' defensive intelligence, coachability and improvement:

"KAT's got a tremendous feel. He's a smart dude when it comes to a lot of things, but especially when it comes to the game of basketball. His feel is at a high level, and so you couple that with the job that [O'Connor] and Erman are doing, it's easy to see where he is because everything we've asked him to do, he's tried to do.

"So if you're extremely coachable, you got a great feel and you got two guys in Brendan O'Connor and Darren Erman that are teaching you the game defensively at a really high level, you'll see the results we're seeing now."

On the importance of continuing to improve rather than simply winning games:

"Obviously, we want to play well, we want to win, but we also want to continue to grow as a team and try not to plateau. I think the way we do that is to continue to try to see how we can win games differently."

On what the Knicks have learned about themselves through the first three weeks of play:

"That we're still resilient. That we're still open to getting better every single day. And through the highs and lows of the season, how quickly can we bounce back?"

On Karl-Anthony Towns's response to Mike Brown's challenges:

"It's [Towns]. It's his willingness to do what's best for the team to continue to get better. I think we've all been challenged by Mike in some way, shape or form. And he's stepped up to the plate with that. And he's been great."

On Mike Brown's unpredictable rotations and how that helps players:

"It's kind of a freeing thing, playing that way, knowing that you don't have to really be looking over your shoulder or thinking that one thing is gonna ruin whatever. And I think that he knows that we have that makeup as a group, too, that we're all on the same page in wanting the best for each other — the next man up kind of mentality. It's from top to bottom, and it's a good environment to have."

On the Knicks prioritizing team success over individual agendas:

"That's just kind of the makeup of our group, and that's special. Typically, you go to a lot of NBA locker rooms, there's some ulterior motives and personal agendas — which is part of why we're all here, is some ego, of course. Everybody is a good player at this level and is striving to get to something or be somewhere. But very rarely does that take a backseat to just truly buying into one another, wanting the best because, ultimately, good things come from that for everyone. It's special. It's really special."

#2875. Cardinals place QB Gardner Minshew on injured reserve, opening the door for rookie Carson Beck

The Arizona Cardinals have placed quarterback Gardner Minshew on injured reserve on Tuesday because of an elbow injury, opening the door for rookie Carson Beck to move into a starting role.

The Cardinals (3-6) also placed receiver Michael Wilson (neck) on injured reserve. Minshew and Wilson were both hurt during the Cardinals' 17-3 loss to the Seattle Seahawks on Sunday.

Minshew completed 21 of 30 passes for 166 yards on Sunday, one week after taking over for the ineffective Jacoby Brissett and leading the Cardinals to a 14-point comeback win over the Dallas Cowboys. Minshew was selected as the starter in the aftermath of that game and Brissett was traded to the Baltimore Ravens.

Now that Minshew is hurt, the Cardinals are down to Beck, who looked impressive during the preseason but hasn't played in a regular-season game. Arizona also announced that it signed 40-year-old quarterback Josh Johnson from the Cincinnati Bengals' practice squad.

The 24-year-old Beck was a third-round pick out of Miami, where he led the Hurricanes to the title game in his senior season before losing to Indiana.

Arizona will try to snap its 10-game home losing streak on Sunday when it hosts the Los Angeles Rams.

#2874. 'Markets bottom on bad news' maxim rings true as chip stocks rally amid Nvidia scandal

"Markets bottom on bad news," is one of those smart-sounding rules of thumb that proves true just often enough to remain a Wall Street cliché in good standing.

Did the brutal weeks-long punishment of AI stocks culminate last week and reverse higher Monday after a pileup of adverse headlines? In the past week, American voters resoundingly opposed data-center construction across much of the country and Anthropic lowered its target IPO valuation just before a sex-and-espionage scandal struck the one-man embodiment of the entire AI economy, Nvidia's Jensen Huang.

The story of a market taking a cue from a crescendo of bad news after a nasty setback to stage a resounding bounce holds together well enough for now, after Monday's 3.4% jump in the Nasdaq-100 and 7% rip in the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index (SOX), even as Nvidia shed 4.3%.

Based purely on the tactical evidence, the tech-hardware complex came into the week pretty well washed out and primed to bounce. Numerous trading desks reported historically rare intensity of net selling in the group by institutions into last Thursday's risk purge. The Nasdaq-100 was as oversold on several metrics as it had been at the March 30 Iran-selloff low. The CBOE S&P 500 Volatility Index has put in a nice spike of the sort usually associated with a passing storm, shooting up to 28 and then receding below 20.

The recent dump in the Nasdaq and uptick in risk aversion, "had many elements of capitulation," says Laurent Laskowski, options strategist at Baird Strategas: "record options activity, a sharp spike in volatility, skew and correlations, an inverted VIX curve, and forced liquidations…The setup, therefore, is one where the market is seeing the [downside] left tail narrowing while the [upside] right tail is fattening."

Several technical-analysis gurus (including Macro Risk Advisors' John Kolovos, whose work is cited below) argue the market hasn't quite served up a "fat pitch" to buy the pullback aggressively. Maybe so. But FanGraphs calculates that in Major League Baseball only about 7% of all pitches are in the very center of the strike zone. While those fat pitches result in some 23% of all home runs hit, the other three-quarters of balls that fly over the wall come with less-perfect setups around the edges of the zone.

I wouldn't argue too strenuously against this tech-centered rally carrying on for a bit, for these reasons as well as the fact that valuations have become far less demanding. Last week, I noted Big Tech had not to that point responded to moderately stressed conditions but was getting closer, and "the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market." Monday's blip higher could be a start, with plenty still to prove.

But this AI-driven part of the market has lately shown itself less worthy of investor trust, leaving enough hints of structural damage to the bull case that faith can't be restored right away.

In a late-June column, I asked, "Was May 14 a consequential moment for this market, at least tactically?" That date, nearly six months ago, was when the S&P 500 spurted higher to reach the 7500 level for the first time; the index has never reached escape velocity since and in fact crossed above 7500 again just yesterday.

May 14 was when highly touted AI-chip upstart Cerebras came public and its shares more than doubled to a high of $386, as President Trump and China's leader were reported to have made progress on ending the Iran war. Since then, Cerebras has collapsed to $97, the semiconductor group is off 17% and the Iran conflict continues.

A month later the SpaceX IPO was another apparent culmination of New Era tech enthusiasm; it might not have been an important peak in AI excitement, but if it were the market action since would possibly resemble what we've seen since.

Perhaps a half-year reckoning period during which the market has refused to pay up for 30% annual earnings growth for two straight quarters is enough to build a new base constructed on more modest expectations. I've been focused on late-cycle tremors unsettling markets in the form of peak earnings growth, peak capex, peak index concentration, a tighter Federal Reserve and credit flareups. This all explains the market's multi-month stall, and they remain.

But it's important to remain open to the chance that these could end up amounting to mere mid-cycle turbulence, as Morgan Stanley chief investment officer Mike Wilson has been arguing.

First, though, the tape will need to show more progress than a nice, broad single-day comeback. Another rule of thumb holds that a rebound rally needs at least to carry above the 20-day moving average to prove that it's not merely a reflexive dead-cat bounce. This would require a 1.3% gain from Monday's close for the S&P 500, 3.7% for the Nasdaq-100 and 9% for the SOX.

Without pretending any clear sense of what the various Nvidia investigations will mean for its China sales, leadership or long-term earnings power, we can say for sure that the scandal arrived at a fairly opportune time for the market.

Valuations have been pounded to multi-year lows on persistent worry that current profits are unsustainable and the group remains vulnerable to any slowdown in data-center builds. This allowed, for a day, every other semiconductor stock to whistle higher as Nvidia lost some $200 billion in market cap on a blistering $80 billion in dollar-volume traded in the stock.

The S&P 500 as a whole, of course, has grown optically cheaper, too, with semis bolstering the projected earnings and investors declining to give the group credit for their sustainability.

The issue remains that less of those earnings are set to manifest as free cash flow as the hyperscalers spend manically. As this chart comparing the valuation gauges shows, the market looks a good deal less cheap on free cash flow than earnings.

A proper bull case for the market from here might be that the likes of Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Microsoft will resume harvesting free cash flow, on which investors might place a higher multiple than they're willing to bestow on those hot but hard-to-trust chip makers. Not yet a provable argument.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "While sentiment is dire, our concern is that technology is in a 'bad oversold' condition, where seductive bounces fail to produce lasting lows. Instead, the broader equity market may need to follow technology lower, with a more widespread capitulation ultimately needed to wash out weak hands and establish a durable bottom."

-This enjoyable and illuminating review in the New Yorker of James Gleick's "The Telephone: A New History" inevitably summons parallels between the advent of telecommunications and the current AI buildout. Huge privately financed infrastructure buildout, destabilizing new habits of interaction with technology and constant calls for government regulation in exchange for near-monopoly power. The book cites an AT&T ad from 1909 calling telephones "tools of 'civilization . . . one of the most powerful influences for broadening human intelligence.'"

-Moody's Analytics economist Mark Zandi has a helpful thread suggesting AI is so far adding more jobs than it's costing the U.S. economy. The main reasons are sobering though, with most gains coming from data-center construction and wealth creation for those who own AI-dependent stocks. Cold comfort for information-economy job seekers.

-Last week's election triggering a Democratic takeover of Congress brought comparisons to the 1994 "Contract with America" Republican sweep for its magnitude and message. That election, in Bill Clinton's first term, highlighted deep societal fissures and economic instability.

With hindsight, many view the '90s as a placid era of consensus and prosperity, but between the '94 backlash election, constant corporate downsizing, scare-mongering over Federal debt levels, the 1995 Oklahoma City bombing and discomfort with technological change, that decade hit many of today's narrative beats. This Chicago Fed paper, "The decline of job security in the 1990s: Displacement, anxiety, and their effect on wage growth" can fill in some blanks for those not around for it.

Happy fifth anniversary to the peak of a pandemic speculative-tech bull market. Not quite two months before the S&P 500 hit a top in January 2022 as the Fed prepared to choke off surging inflation, perhaps by causing a recession, several key retail-trader-driven subsectors peaked.

The third week of November 2021 was the relative-performance peak in Tesla, as well as the absolute top in Coinbase and the Wisdom Tree Cloud Computing ETF (WCLD).

The five-year lookback period is one of the few time spans shy of 25 years over which the Nasdaq Composite has not outperformed the S&P 500. It's a plus that the broad market can withstand such internal speculative blowoffs, though also a good reminder that the most fun pockets of the market don't stay that way forever.

#2873. Colts trade WR Josh Downs to Chiefs after receiver requested a trade

Josh Downs is the latest player to leave Indianapolis after the Colts dealt the wide receiver to the Kansas City Chiefs.

The Chiefs, who are also acquiring a 2027 seventh-round draft pick, are sending a fourth- and fifth-round pick in 2027 back to Indianapolis.

#2872. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2871. Injured Feyi-Waboso is our Robinson – Sinfield

Immanuel Feyi-Waboso, England's prolific but injury-prone wing, is the 2026 team's equivalent of Rugby World Cup winner Jason Robinson says assistant coach Kevin Sinfield.

Feyi-Waboso has scored nine tries in 17 appearances for England since making his Test debut against Italy in 2024.

However, his progress with England has been checked by a string of injuries.

He missed the 2025 Six Nations with a shoulder injury, while his 2026 tournament was ended by a hamstring injury sustained in training before England's opening-round win over Wales.

He returned this summer, scoring in wins over Fiji and Argentina, but suffered an autumn-ending ankle injury in the first half of Sunday's match against Australia.

Robinson, whose acceleration and side-step brought him 28 tries in 51 appearances for England between 2001 and 2007, tore his hamstring in the Rugby World Cup final defeat by South Africa in his final Test.

"He is our Jason Robinson," said Sinfield, who played against Robinson during the pair's days in rugby league.

"When you have a player who is that explosive and that powerful sometimes a run of injuries just happens.

"He's across everything to do with his body and hopefully we get him back firing as soon as we can.

"That collaboration [between England and Feyi-Waboso's club Exeter] is going be really important because these guys play a lot of rugby.

"Making sure they're fit for those big games is going to be really important."

England have the final say over the fitness and conditioning of centrally-contracted players such as Feyi-Waboso, but there have been disagreements in the past with Exeter over his treatment.

Relations have since improved with England signing off Fey-Waboso's return for June's Prem final, only three weeks after he suffered a facial fracture.

Feyi-Waboso played on after suffering his ankle injury in the 32nd minute of Sunday's 45-22 win over the Wallabies, hobbling his way to half-time before being replaced.

Sinfield said that the desire of Feyi-Waboso, who is studying medicine, to stay on the pitch swayed the decision to keep him on.

"It's getting the balance right, because the player is telling you he wants to try and run it off," said Sinfield.

"You can't scan anyone on the grass, so you're looking at it with your eyes. I think it's only right for us, as coaches, that we listen to players."

England will be without Ollie Chessum, George Furbank and Seb Atkinson for Saturday's match against Japan, while flanker Tom Curry has, like Feyi-Waboso, been ruled out of the entire autumn campaign after he aggravated a long-standing hip issue.

Saracens' 20-year-old Noah Caluori, who made a try-scoring England debut against Fiji in July, has been called up in Feyi-Waboso's place.

SInfield's most recent challenge culminated in his delivering the Super League trophy to Old Trafford for the Grand Final [Getty Images]

Sinfield was knighted last month for his services to both codes of rugby and for raising money and awareness about motor neurone disease.

Sinfield's friend and former Leeds Rhinos team-mate Rob Burrow died from MND in June 2024 at the age of 41.

Sinfield has raised more than £13m for charities involved in attempting to support those with the disease and find a cure, running seven ultra marathons in seven days last month in his most recent challenge.

He now plans to run seven marathons in seven days in New Zealand and Australia in late May to help global collaboration in research. NRL star Jai Arrow and former Wallaby Nathan Charles have recently been diagnosed, raising the profile of MND in Australia.

"It's been a great journey and it's important we continue because there's no cure," said Sinfield. "We've got to carry on."

#2869. Anthropic's trillion-dollar IPO is coming, but retail investors could have owned AI stock much earlier

Anthropic is set to go public next week, with a potential date of Nov. 19 for an initial public offering that could value the AI giant on a fully diluted basis at over $1.4 trillion. For many retail investors, the IPO may be the first chance they have to own Anthropic and even that ownership event will be out of reach with limited shares allocated to retail brokerage firms. But there were ways for retail investors to own Anthropic shares at a much earlier date. Investors may also be able to get in on the next hot IPO well before listing day.

There are several ways to do this, though it can depend on whether you're an accredited investor under SEC guidelines when investing directly in a private company versus accessing a privately held stock through a diversified fund. Generally, this means being able to prove you have $200,000 in individual income, or $300,000 jointly, or $1 million in net worth, not counting an investor's home. The SEC has proposed expanding accredited investor eligibility beyond traditional income and net worth thresholds, through an exam-based route, but that's not currently an option.

Here's what investors looking for some pre-IPO action need to know:

Some mutual funds and ETFs invest in private companies before they are public. "It's not like buying the IPO directly, so you have to be comfortable with the portfolio," said Chester Spatt, professor of finance at Carnegie Mellon University's Tepper School of Business.

That means reviewing all of the portfolio holdings of a fund and its investment approach, not just the fact that it has a stake in the one company that has yet to go public. Even so, mutual funds and ETFs can be a way to gain some exposure to the pre-IPO world, with SpaceX, Anthropic and its rival OpenAI all recent examples.

For example, Fidelity Contrafund, a large-cap growth fund, says in its prospectus that the adviser may invest the fund's assets in securities of private or newly public companies. It was an early investor in SpaceX, for example, buying shares in 2015 when the company was valued at about $10 billion. SpaceX represented about 4.4% of the fund's holdings at the end of September.

Billionaire mutual fund manager Ron Baron's Partners Fund, which seeks long-term capital appreciation, also invested in SpaceX. At the end of October, SpaceX was the fund's No. 1 holding, accounting for 28% of its assets.

Before buying a fund that invests in private companies, be sure to consider redemption and fees, Spatt said. For example, The Private Shares Fund, which seeks capital appreciation by focusing on investments in late-stage, venture-backed private companies, allows quarterly tenders for up to 5% of the fund's outstanding shares at NAV. The management fee is 1.9%, higher than many index funds.

Several ETFs list Anthropic among their holdings, including the Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR), Tema Electrification ETF (VOLT),  Alger Concentrated Equity ETF (CNEQ), Alger AI Enablers & Adopters ETF (ALAI), Alger 35 ETF (ATFV), KraneShares Public-Private AI & Technology ETF (AGIX), and iShares A.I. Innovation and Tech Active ETF (BAI).

Several big mutual fund and ETF companies, similar to the SpaceX shares, have reported ownership in Anthropic in the past, including T. Rowe Price, Capital Group and Fidelity Investments. Fifty Fidelity funds have stakes in Anthropic, according to Morningstar, at roughly $5 billion. Capital Group has an even greater stake across its funds, at $10 billion, according to Morningstar.

But there is a catch: not all the funds own shares outright, with some ETFs gaining exposure through what are called special purpose vehicles.

Fund managers and accredited investors can access late-stage start-ups through special purpose vehicles.

Tema's LAZR ETF, for example, lists Anthropic as its second-biggest holding, but it is through an SPV. Its VOLT fund has a much smaller holding in Anthropic.

Accredited investors can also access SPV-related investment opportunities directly, through an online platform like Caplight, Sydecar and Augment, said Emily Zheng, senior venture capital analyst at private investment research firm Pitchbook, or through their wealth manager. Keep in mind, however, you could be buying an SPV that owns the shares outright, or you could be invested in an SPV that has shares of an SPV that owns the shares, she said. There can also be multiple layers of fees.

"This structure may allow investors to participate at lower minimums than would typically be required for a direct share purchase, while the fund manager handles the complexity of acquisition, ongoing administration, and tax reporting on behalf of investors," according to private wealth and alternative investments manager TSG Invest, which invests in pre-IPO shares. It says SPVs are becoming the dominant structure in pre-IPO investing, now accounting for roughly 40% to 50% of all secondary market transactions.

Accredited investors who want access to shares of private companies that may not be imminently ready to IPO can buy through online private stock trading platforms such as Schwab's Forge Global, Morgan Stanley's EquityZen, Notice, Caplight Technologies and Clarity, previously known as Hiive.

Platforms have different private holdings available, and investment requirements can vary. There can also be multiple fees. For instance, at Forge, opening an account and using the marketplace to trade shares are free. However, buyers pay a one-time fee for both direct shares and fund offerings. Investors should also be aware that some companies may charge their own transfer or legal fees, according to a Forge spokesperson.

Several platforms require investment minimums. The standard minimum for direct secondary transactions at Forge, for example, is $100,000, though minimums may vary by investment type and offering. Certain Forge fund offerings may accept indications of interest starting at $5,000, but availability is limited and subject to the terms of the specific offering, the company says on its website.

Investors need to consider their time horizon and their liquidity needs before investing in private companies. Additionally, minimums at online platforms can be high, even for accredited investors, said Zheng. "But it is a lot lower than traditional venture investment," Zheng said.

Individual investors can get access to private companies through about two dozen publicly available closed-end funds focused on private equity or venture capital, according to Morningstar Direct.

Funds vary in their holdings between early-stage and late-stage companies. OpenAI, for instance, represents about 9.4% of the holdings of Robinhood Ventures Fund I. The fund's holdings also include 4.2% of Ramp, which could IPO at some point in the next year, and its largest holding is enterprise tech company Databricks.

Sofi, meanwhile, offers access to certain funds that may not be available through other brokerages. Private and venture funds on Sofi's platform include the ARK Venture Fund, which owns both OpenAI and Anthropic; and the Destiny Tech100 Closed End Fund, which also owns both AI companies.

Closed-end funds also offer exposure to other closely watched private names, such as the Cashmere Fund, which owns Kalshi. The Fundrise Innovation Fund has reported holdings in Anthropic, OpenAI, Databricks, Anduril and Ramp as of its year-end 2025 statement, but not more recently.

These funds can carry significantly higher fees than index funds or ETFs consisting of public company stocks. Robinhood Ventures Fund I, for example, has an expense ratio of 3.13%.

If a retail investor does want to get in on the IPO, some brokerage firms are able to offer Anthropic IPO access to clients, and you generally don't have to be an accredited investor to be eligible.

Anthropic, for example, said in a Nov. 2 regulatory filing that it was allocating some IPO shares to brokerage customers of Charles Schwab, Fidelity, Robinhood Financial (including its TradePMR division), SoFi, and Morgan Stanley's E-Trade.

SpaceX offered about 20% of its IPO shares to individual investors through these brokerages.

Individual brokerages determine the requirements for participation. SoFi, for example, requires investors to have a particular account type, but there are no account minimums. Fidelity allows access to brokerage customers with at least $25,000 in retail assets at the firm.

To request shares, eligible investors generally enter an indication of interest for the offering they are interested in. However, there's no guarantee investors will receive shares or how many. In many cases, investors may be disappointed with their allocation.

"We only get a limited number of shares for each IPO," Robinhood says on its website. "If customer demand is higher than our supply, we can't fulfill every request in full. How shares are distributed depends on the allocation method used for the offering."

#2868. Chiefs strike at trade deadline for WR as Colts continue fire sale

Kansas City Chiefs general manager Brett Veach has made yet another mid-season trade for a wide receiver.

This time, it's Indianapolis Colts wideout Josh Downs heading to Kansas City, the team announced.

It's the third time in the last four years that Veach and the Chiefs are acquiring a receiver ahead of the NFL trade deadline. Veach previously traded for Kadarius Toney in 2022, Mecole Hardman in 2023 and DeAndre Hopkins in 2024.

Downs had requested a trade out of Indianapolis at the end of October, according to ESPN's Jeremy Fowler, and he's set to get his wish. The wide receiver joins guard Quenton Nelson, defensive tackle DeForest Buckner and fellow receiver Keenan Allen as players the Colts have moved or are moving ahead of the NFL trade deadline.

TRADE DEADLINE TRACKER: Rumors, updates as blockbuster deal gets done

The Chiefs were in dire need of help at receiver as injuries began to stack up in their receiving corps, which was already lacking depth. Tyquan Thornton, who led the Chiefs with 19.9 yards per catch, suffered a dislocated ankle in Week 4. Xavier Worthy, who is tied for the team's lead in receptions (33), is day-to-day with a hamstring injury. Rashee Rice missed two games earlier this year with his own hamstring injury.

Here's what the Chiefs gave up to acquire Downs:

Indianapolis has received a total of five picks in next year's NFL draft plus an additional pick in 2028 in return for four players. The Downs trade was the final move the Colts made ahead of the deadline as they opted to keep running back Jonathan Taylor on the roster.

The Chiefs gave up their first two picks on Day 3 of next year's draft to get receiver help now. Kansas City had lost two straight games and possession of first place in the AFC West before stopping their skid with a Week 9 win over the Jets. Downs projects to slot into the Chiefs' starting trio of receivers immediately upon his arrival.

#2866. 'PASS TO STEPH': Jimmy Butler moonlights as assistant coach during Warriors' rout of Pelicans

When Steve Kerr led the Golden State Warriors out onto the court against the New Orleans Pelicans on Monday night, he did so with a slightly larger coaching staff than usual. The new addition had to be the highest-paid end-of-bench assistant in NBA history.

That's right: Jimmy Butler, still in the midst of his ongoing rehabilitation following surgery to repair the torn right ACL that ended his 2025-26 season, decided to come out for Monday's matchup decked out in something different than designer duds. This time around, the six-time All-Star opted for something a little more muted: an assistant coach's pullover. (That sound you heard might have been Fred Hoiberg, Tom Thibodeau, Brett Brown and Erik Spoelstra all spitting out their Big Face Coffee at the notion of Coach Jimmy Butler.)

"It was funny, because I don't know how this happened, but I told [assistant coach] Khalid [Robinson] about five minutes before the game [...] 'One of these days, we should have Jimmy dress up like us,' because he's always got some great outfit on and something fashionable, and we wear our quarter-zips and sweaters," Kerr later told reporters. "I said, 'We should really get him to dress like a coach one day,' and then he walks out, and he's dressed like us. I turned to Khalid, I said, 'Did you tell him to do that?' and he goes, 'No.'"

"During the first time out, I'm walking by, and I'm going to ask [assistant] coach Frank [Vogel] a question, and then, I see Jimmy," Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg told reporters. "And I'm like, 'What are you wearing?'"

"It was, I guess, mental telepathy or something," Kerr said. "We were on the same page on that. He was enjoying himself."

But not, of course, at the expense of shirking his assistant-ly duties.

"He said all the right things, you know," Kerr said. "Team fouls, you know — how many team fouls we had. Like, he was really acting the part."

Up to and including toting around a clipboard during timeouts …

… and even, with the Warriors holding a huge lead in the third quarter, diagramming a real honey of an Xs-and-Os play and sending it in from the bench:

"PASS TO STEPH." Clearly, Jimmy's learned a thing or two about how to create quality offense over the years.

"He was on the bench the whole time, grabbing [De'Anthony Melton] by his jersey, talking about, 'Give the ball to Steph,'" Lendeborg said.

Asked about Jimmy's clipboard call-out in the Warriors locker room, Stephen Curry simply replied, "That's good coaching."

Even so, Melton didn't feel like he needed that particular bit of guidance.

"He need to go home," a laughing Melton said after the game. "Next time he do something like that, he need to go back home or do something else, because he tripping."

"I didn't like his advice," Lendeborg deadpanned after the game. "I didn't like him out there."

Be that as it may, Yaxel, you simply can't argue with the results.

In Butler's first game as a properly uniformed assistant, the Warriors annihilated a short-handed Pelicans squad playing on the second night of a back-to-back and without starters Zion Williamson and Trey Murphy III. Golden State led for nearly the entirety of what settled as a 138-87 rout — the fourth-largest win in Warriors franchise history, and the second-most-lopsided defeat ever for the Pels.

Stephen Curry scored 25 points in 20 minutes, continuing the remarkable start to the season that has seen him join the scorching Luka Dončić and Victor Wembanyama as the only players averaging more than a point per minute of play. Brandin Podziemski added 25 while rookie phenom Lendeborg chipped in 23 for the Warriors, who have now won eight in a row. That's Golden State's longest winning streak since midway through the 2021-22 season — which, as you might remember, is the last time the Dubs won the NBA title.

As impressively as they performed with Butler on the sideline, though, the Warriors would certainly prefer to have him trading in that pullover for a uniform.

When Butler arrived at the 2025 trade deadline, he gave the club precisely the jolt it needed to return to contention, helping the Warriors finish the 2024-25 campaign on a tear, win their opening-round playoff series against the Houston Rockets, and take a 1-0 lead on the Minnesota Timberwolves in the Western Conference semifinals before Curry suffered a hamstring injury that effectively submarined their chances. He was playing at an All-NBA level last season, too, before suffering the knee injury that ended his season. Golden State went 12-26 after he went down, and entered this season picked by many pundits to remain mired in play-in contention without his services.

The Warriors' stellar start to the season, though — 8-3, with the NBA's No. 6 offense and No. 10 defense, buoyed by Curry's brilliance and the instant impact of first-round pick Lendeborg — has changed that perception, allowing for a more optimistic view of what might be possible in the Bay.

"I'm almost looking at this like this could be the exact opposite of last season," Kerr told ESPN's Anthony Slater during the preseason. "We get Jimmy back around February instead of losing him in February. So, what's our job? Lay the best foundation, best platform for success before that, that can put us in a good position where we've got momentum, and all of a sudden, we're adding him with the playoffs coming a couple months later. I think the plan is there."

All that's left, then, is to execute it. And if you can trust anybody to nail the execution, it's someone, like Butler, who is cerebral enough to call the "PASS TO STEPH" play.

#2865. House Oversight calls on DOJ to interview key Epstein witnesses, says govt. ‘failed’ survivors

The House Oversight and Government Reform Committee on Tuesday released a sharply critical report on the federal government's handling of investigations into Jeffrey Epstein and his accomplice, Ghislaine Maxwell.

"The federal government has failed the survivors of Jeffrey Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell's horrific crimes for decades," Oversight Chair Rep. James Comer, R-Ky., said in a statement.

The report called for the Justice Department to interview or reinterview "key witnesses" with information about Epstein's sex trafficking of young women and girls, "further investigate allegations of criminal activity involving Epstein's associates, and provide survivors with unredacted access to their records."

The report also urged Congress to pass legislation sponsored by Comer that would allow survivors of sexual abuse to speak freely about it, even if they had signed confidentiality agreements.

In addition to criticizing the federal government, the committee blasted various financial institutions for their roles in dealing with Epstein, who killed himself in August 2019 in jail after being arrested on child sex trafficking charges.

"U.S. and foreign government authorities failed to identify indicators of human trafficking and immigration fraud," the committee said. "Institutions such as American Express enabled this activity, with company agents allowing Epstein to use fraudulent decoy flights and refundable or dummy itineraries to transport foreign women across borders directly to his residences."

"Financial institutions possessed substantial information about Epstein's criminal history and repeatedly identified transactions and activities presenting heightened compliance risks," the committee said. "Yet they failed to adequately address those risks, in some cases reporting them years later after Epstein's 2019 arrest, due to Epstein's personal connections, wealth, and influence."

The committee called on the Treasury Department and the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network to review whether banks and other financial institutions are meeting their obligations to report activity that could point to money laundering and human trafficking.

The committee has been investigating Epstein, Maxwell and their connections since last year.

#2863. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2862. Anthropic sets IPO price range of $70 to $78, looks to start trading Nov. 19, sources say

Anthropic said it will offer 825 million shares in its upcoming IPO at a price range between $70 and $78 per share, according to an updated filing on Tuesday, bringing the 5-year-old artificial intelligence lab a step closer to a potentially historic Nasdaq debut.

The company is planning to price on Nov. 18 and trade on Nov. 19, according to people familiar with the details who were granted anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is targeting retail allocation of about 20%, the people said, a much higher percentage than the typical initial public offering, which is 5% to 10% for retail investors.

At the high end of the range, Anthropic would net up to $64.35 billion in proceeds through the IPO. The company could end up being worth up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis, rising from the $965 billion valuation it announced as part of a funding round in May.

Anthropic said it has entered into an agreement with Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. to purchase $26 billion of the company's Class N common stock in a private placement at a price per share equal to the IPO price. The shares sold in the private placement will not be registered in the offering and will not be subject to lock-up agreements with the underwriters, the company said.

As Anthropic executives get set to pitch the offering to investors, they have to contend with a tumultuous political environment, following last week's midterm elections that included a Democratic sweep in Congress and the passage of numerous statewide ballot measures that could slow the AI buildout. They're also facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade on concern that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for companies that need it for costly infrastructure purchases.

CNBC reported last week that some Anthropic investors were bracing for a lower valuation than had previously been expected. While Anthropic had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation until recently, shareholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

Should Anthropic reach the Nasdaq this month as planned, under the ticker symbol "ANT," the debut would come roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. Elon Musk's reusable rocket and AI company has a market cap of just under $2 trillion, around its valuation at the time of the IPO.

Institutional demand for Anthropic is even stronger than it was for SpaceX at this point in the process, the people said. The company's book is already covered at the $70 to $78 per share range on an allocable basis, they said.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the AI lab in 2021 after he and a small group of researchers defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and the company primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

On Monday, Anthropic introduced two new AI models, Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, the first time the company has released so-called frontier models since Amodei called to slow down the pace of AI development in September. Anthropic said that the models went through rigorous safety and alignment testing, and that the company developed new evaluations and post-launch monitoring tools ahead of the release.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic said in its amended filing Tuesday that its run-rate revenue was roughly $81 billion at the end of October, up $10 billion from the previous month.

"Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles," Anthropic's co-founders wrote in the prospectus. "Everyone who owns our stock, whether an institution or an individual, becomes part of that effort. We hope you will share our ambition to see it though."

#2858. Alphabet shares appear to have bottomed. How to play it for a quick rebound

Ten days after Alphabet shares experienced their worst one-day drubbing in its history, the search, media and AI giant has barely retraced a faction of its nearly $500 billion in lost market capitalization, having rallied just a paltry 1.5%. The muted recovery stands out for a company that posted its 13th straight quarter of double-digit revenue growth in the same report, with Google Cloud revenue more than doubling. Alphabet's earnings were the first major crack to appear in the AI trade and foreshadowed a series of volatile events for the complex, including the election and the growing scandal enveloping Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang.

Still, a bullish case for Alphabet rests not in its post-earnings concerns being unfounded, but rather on its growth being strong enough to withstand them. On Monday, Google launched two new systems built on its custom AI chip, the TPU, a sign the company keeps evolving, and the debate over AI and search is far from settled. The sheer size of the drop, a record one-day loss after a quarter of 25% revenue growth, may also leave room for a reversion toward pre-earnings levels. For investors who agree, options offer a variety of ways to express that view.

Based on Monday's close, I'm buying the December 18 285/300 call spread for a debit of roughly $7.07.

Alphabet's 30-day implied volatility closed Monday around 35%, higher than about 44% of days over the past year. That is mid-range, so either buying or selling premium can be viable for this trade. A bull call spread is the higher-conviction route. It needs the stock to rally to pay off, but it offers a larger payout if the view is right. Selling a put spread is the lower-conviction route. It can carry a higher probability of profit, but with a smaller reward relative to the risk.

Due to the sheer size of the drop and the weak recovery thus far, I'm choosing the higher-conviction call spread.

Alphabet has recovered only a small part of its post-earnings loss, still down 11.3% from its pre-earnings close of $324.89. The trade expires Dec. 18 and assumes that gap at least partly closes at some point over the next 39 days, not necessarily right away.

The long 285 call sits just in the money, and the short 300 call sits about halfway back from the post-earnings low to the pre-earnings close. This gives the trade a breakeven of $292.07, 1.4% above Monday's close and still about 10% below pre-earnings levels.

The trade has a theoretical probability of profit of 47% and reaches its maximum profit of $793 per spread if Alphabet closes at or above $300 on Dec. 18. The maximum loss of $707 comes if shares close at or below $285. If Alphabet closes between $285 and $300 on Dec. 18, the long call can be exercised, leaving investors holding 100 shares per spread. Investors can close the spread before expiration to capture profits, cut losses, or avoid taking on shares.

This is a directional bet on a continued rebound, and the main risk is that the recovery stalls, even for a historically strong company. Nvidia's results on Nov. 17 could set the tone for the AI trade, and rates, energy prices, and scrutiny of data center construction remain headwinds to watch.

#2857. Re-grading the Sixers’ trade for Jaylen Brown ahead of first Celtics game

If there's one thing we love to do in Big Media, it's re-grading trades prematurely. It takes years before we have enough perspective to know which teams actually won or lost any transaction, but who's got the time or patience for that these days?

Fortunately, the Boston Celtics are coming to Philadelphia on Tuesday, which gives us a golden opportunity to relitigate their decision to trade Jaylen Brown to the Sixers this past offseason.

First, let's recap the terms of the trade itself:

Boston Celtics receive: Paul George, 2028 1st (likely a swap involving the LAC pick), 2031 PHI 1st, 2028 2nd (best of GSW/MIL/OKC), 2030 2nd (best of PHX/POR/WAS)

Before we go any further, we must emphasize: The final judgment on this deal won't be rendered for years to come. We'll have to see if the Celtics eventually use George's contract in another trade and whether the Sixers extend Brown (and for how much if so).

But again, what's the fun in waiting? Let's examine whether either team should already be having buyer's remorse.

If the Sixers didn't trade for Brown, LeBron James likely would not have signed a veteran-minimum contract with them in free agency. He admitted as much this offseason. That alone makes this deal a clear A for now.

That isn't the only reason why it deserves an A, though.

With Jayson Tatum on the mend from an Achilles tear for most of last season, Brown averaged nearly 29 points per game and finished sixth in the MVP race. Unsurprisingly, his scoring output has dipped in Philadelphia, as he's now sharing touches with LeBron, Tyrese Maxey, VJ Edgecombe and Joel Embiid. Still, he's averaging 22.3 points per game, which ranks second on the team behind only Maxey, and is doing so on an efficient 47.7 percent shooting overall and 39.2 percent shooting from deep.

A bunch of analytics dorks justified the trade for the Celtics by pointing to Brown's mediocre on/off splits, which drew Brown's ire.

Ironically, Brown's on/off splits have been great in Philadelphia thus far. They've outscored opponents by 4.8 points per 100 possessions with him on the floor and have gotten outscored by 1.9 per 100 with him off. That plus-6.7 on/off split is the second-best mark of his career, trailing only his sophomore NBA campaign in 2017-18.

That's largely been fueled by defense, which Brown has repeatedly described as a "non-negotiable" for the star-studded Sixers this year. Brown is taking advantage of his reduced workload on offense by devoting more energy to the other end of the floor, where he's often tasked with guarding the opponent's toughest wing or forward. It was only fitting that he punctuated his 30-point outing against the Detroit Pistons—his highest-scoring game with the Sixers yet—with a game-saving block of Cade Cunningham as the Pistons were trying to mount a furious late rally.

Brown and the Sixers are still trying to get their footing on offense, particularly in half-court settings. But his ability to carry the team at times has been invaluable, especially with Embiid off to a slow start offensively.

Does Brown still take some ill-advised shots at times? Of course! Good luck finding an NBA star who doesn't. But he has otherwise acquitted himself well to the Philly faithful in the early going. And even if he hadn't… again, LeBron James would not be a Sixer if they hadn't traded George for Brown. 'Nuff said.

Down the road, we'll have to see where the two first-round picks involved in this deal land and whether the Sixers eventually sign Brown to an extension. Any of those could impact the long-term perception of this trade. But the Sixers were staring down a third straight season where Embiid and George's availability (or lack thereof) could have submarined their ceiling. Now, they're one of the clear title favorites in the East.

When ESPN's Shams Charania broke news of the trade on July 1, most Celtics fans spent the next 24-72 hours having a nuclear-grade meltdown.

That's all you got for Jaylen Brown? Finals MVP Jaylen Brown?!?

In a league without a salary cap, the Celtics assuredly would have gotten more for him. But teams have gotten increasingly wary about big-money, long-term contracts, and Brown's deal is worth roughly 35 percent of the salary cap through 2028-29. The Sixers could afford taking it on since they already had George signed to a roughly equivalent contract, but other teams didn't have that same luxury.

Celtics president of basketball operations Brad Stevens later admitted that finances were part of the motivation to move Brown. "The path looked a little bit more challenging with 70 percent of our [salary] cap and such a high percentage of our usage tied into two players," he added. What was left unsaid: The Celtics wanted to trade Brown before they had to confront whether to extend his contract.

George will be under no illusion that he's still a max-worthy player once his deal expires after the 2027-28 campaign. The Celtics might look to trade him before that, but if not, they should be able to re-sign him to far below a max deal. Brown, meanwhile, will likely want a max or near-max extension.

George doesn't boast Brown's explosiveness, which limits his ability to drive downhill. But he has long been an elite three-point shooter, which makes him a perfect fit for head coach Joe Mazzulla's system. In the early going, George is shooting a career-high 42.5 percent from deep on 8.1 attempts per game.

George has also been solid on defense thus far. In fact, he's currently averaging a career-best 2.3 steals per game. We'll see how long that lasts or whether he'll be able to sustain that level of production over a full 82-game season, but so far, so good.

Both teams are still in their honeymoon phase with Brown and George, but at this point, neither should have major regrets about the deal. If anything, the Celtics' biggest pang of remorse might be paving the way for the Sixers to land LeBron as well.

Aside from, you know, blowing a 3-1 series lead to the Sixers in last year's playoffs, which set the stage for the Brown trade in the first place.

#2856. We're about to see two Boston Celtics champions go toe-to-toe

From the moment the NBA released its schedule for the 2026 NBA season on August 13, Boston Celtics fans have had Tuesday, November 10, circled on their calendars. Why? Because that was the day that Jayson Tatum and Jaylen Brown would be facing off in a competitive game for the first time in their careers.

A lot has been said about Tatum and Brown's off-court friendship, or lack thereof. It's been a talking point for years. However, discussions surrounding their off-court relationship certainly ramped up following Brown's departure from Boston, which, of course, means there will be even more eyes on their match-up come Tuesday night.

In a video that the Celtics recently uploaded to YouTube, "NBC Sports Boston" host Abby Chin was joined by Sean Grande to preview the upcoming duel between the former teammates and what we can expect once the ball is tipped off and the game is underway. The two discussed Tatum and Brown's skill sets, and the fact that they know each other's game inside and out, along with the perceived disconnect between the 2024 NBA champions following Brad Stevens' decision to sign off on Brown being traded.

#2855. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.
