#2833. How Jayson Tatum and the Celtics haven't skipped a beat without Jaylen Brown

Despite having Jayson Tatum for a grand total of 522 minutes during the 2025-26 regular season, the Boston Celtics won nearly 70% of their games, finished in the top five in the NBA in both offensive and defensive efficiency, and landed the No. 2 seed in the Eastern Conference. They reached that level of success thanks, in large part, to Tatum's longtime running mate, Jaylen Brown, turning in the finest individual season of his career en route to a sixth-place finish in MVP voting and an All-NBA Second Team selection.

After that, though, the Celtics traded Brown to the Philadelphia 76ers this summer — a move that you might have heard just a teensy bit about over the past five months, and that has been driving no small amount of conversation ahead of the first meeting of the season between Boston and Philly on Tuesday night.

Since that tectonic roster shift, as they seek to chart a new path forward with a shuffled-up rotation and without a franchise cornerstone, the Celtics have … won 80% of their games, rank in the top seven in the NBA in both offensive and defensive efficiency, and sit in the No. 2 seed in the Eastern Conference.

The more things change, the more they stay the same?

This, to some degree, was the bet that Brad Stevens made when he made the decision to move Brown for Paul George, two first-round picks and two second-round picks. In the longer term, exchanging Brown — who has three years and $185 million remaining on his contract, and is also in line for an extension — for George — who makes less this season, has a player option for next season and comes off the books one year earlier — tidies up the Celtics' balance sheet moving forward, potentially putting them in better position to remain competitive in the race for the top of the East several years down the line. (Say it with me, Brad: "optionality!")

In the here-and-now, though, Stevens wagered that, as excellent as Brown was last season and as vital as he's been to the Celtics' perennial success for the past decade, the surrounding talent and head coach Joe Mazzulla's system would provide enough insulation to ensure that the team wouldn't skip too many beats without Brown. Through 10 games, at least? Boston looks pretty right about that.

The Celtics enter Tuesday's heavyweight battle with the Sixers looking broadly like the same elite squad they were last season. On offense, they sit in or near the top 10 in points scored and allowed per possession, and both team effective field goal percentage and opponents' eFG%. They're great at both avoiding turnovers and forcing them. They grab a ton of offensive rebounds and defend without fouling. They still jack a ton of 3s and make 'em at a top-10 clip, and still give up a ton of 3s in favor of protecting the rim without giving up the entire store on defense. They still play a methodical and effective brand of half-court basketball on offense; they're still great at preventing transition opportunities, despite sending multiple bodies to the offensive glass.

Even without Brown, the fundamentals of who the Celtics are haven't changed; the specifics of how they get there, though, are a little bit different. It starts with Tatum, now 18 months removed from rupturing his Achilles tendon and once again firmly entrenched as Boston's No. 1 option.

Tatum is still ironing out some of the wrinkles in his scoring game, finishing at substandard rates at the rim, from midrange and from beyond the 3-point arc. As he told reporters after blowing out the Charlotte Hornets last week, "I still got a little ways to to get back to being the best I ever felt."

Even with his bucket-getting lagging a bit, though, the 28-year-old is off to a flying start, averaging 27.1 points and 6 rebounds in 33.8 minutes per game. (He's been nails in crunch time, too, ranking eighth in the league in clutch scoring, with 21 points in 15 minutes on 8-for-13 shooting with 3 assists and 0 turnovers.) Just as Brown stepped into a higher-volume role in his absence last season, Tatum has slid into the monster void left by Brown's departure this season. He's finishing a career-high 33.3% of Boston's offensive possessions with a shot attempt, foul drawn or turnover, and leads the Celtics in usage, touches and time of possession.

As Gary Washburn of the Boston Globe put it, "Within the Celtics organization, there appears to be a sense of relief that the tension is gone" — that the team's hierarchy is clarified and established, with Tatum at the top of the pecking order, George comfortably slotting in behind him, and everybody else falling in line after that. Despite that, though, it hasn't felt like Tatum's got a stranglehold of Boston's offense — far from it, in fact.

Tatum's on-ball percentage — literally the share of his floor time that he has the ball in his hands — is down from where it was the past two seasons. His average time of possession of 5.1 minutes per game, 33rd in the NBA, between Donovan Mitchell and Ja Morant.

His willingness to get off the ball rather than pound it has helped produce a more egalitarian attack. Boston is averaging 27.2 assists per game, seventh-most in the NBA, up from 24.6 per game (27th) last season. Those dimes are leading to 70 points per game via assist, sixth-most in the league, up from 64 per game (22nd) last season.

Plenty of those helpers are coming directly off Tatum's fingertips. In the early going of the season, his playmaking has really leaped off the screen. Whether working in the pick-and-roll, operating out of the post, or surveying from the top of the floor at the nail, he's mapping the floor and delivering on-time and on-target feeds more often than ever. Tatum is averaging a career-high 7.8 assists against 2.8 turnovers per game, creating more baskets at the rim than all but five players in the NBA, according to PBP Stats, and setting up more 3-pointers than all but eight. He's one of just two players in the NBA in the top 10 in both scoring and assists. The other? The scorching-hot Luka Dončić.

The player Tatum has assisted the most thus far this season? George, who's been the recipient of 13 Tatum dimes, and who has looked like a hand-in-glove fit in his new digs.

George has picked up where he left off last season, when he came back from a 25-game suspension for violating the NBA/NBPA joint anti-drug program by taking "improper medication" for mental health issues to shine for the Sixers in their playoff push and their first-round victory over these same Celtics. He's averaging 20.4 points, 5.2 rebounds and 4.1 assists in 30.1 minutes per game, shooting 50.7% on 2-pointers, 42.5% on 3-pointers while taking more than eight per game, and 80.8% from the free-throw line.

The nine-time All-Star isn't quite the full-tilt offensive force that he was earlier in his career, but he's fit in brilliantly as a secondary scorer and creator who can demand attention as an off-ball threat on offense, and a high-level, gap-filling playmaker who can help crank up the heat on the defensive end. He's still capable of assuming a larger role when called upon, too: When Tatum got the night off against the Bucks last Wednesday, George stepped to the forefront, scoring 25 points with 5 assists, 4 steals and 3 rebounds to lead the way in a 109-102 win.

"The way we're asking him to play is maybe a little bit different than what he's been asked to do in the past," Mazzulla told reporters in Milwaukee on Sunday. "He enjoys that, he's open-minded to it and he just works at it. He's very hard on himself. Holds himself to a very high standard, constantly watching film, taking notes in his journal and just processing the game. He takes pride in learning and getting better, and you're seeing the fruits of that."

The C's are also seeing the fruits of swapping Brown for George in the possession game. According to Jared Dubin's tracking at Last Night in Basketball, the Celtics finished 15th in the nightly possession battle last season, barely generating more scoring chances than their opposition on a night-to-night basis despite top-five finishes in both offensive rebounding rate and offensive turnover rate — due in large part to bottom-10 marks in both getting to the free-throw line and forcing turnovers on defense. Seven of the 14 teams that finished ahead of Boston last season won a playoff series — including all four conference finalists, and the Sixers team that eliminated them in the first round of the playoffs.

It is perhaps notable, then, that the rate at which the Celtics forced turnovers last season increased more with Brown off the floor than it did for any other player who logged at least 1,000 minutes — and that their forced turnover rate had been significantly higher with him off the floor in each of the previous two seasons.

So: Out went Brown, and in came George, a superior off-ball defender who has consistently posted higher steal rates, and center Mitchell Robinson, who'd built his reputation in New York as one of the league's premier extra-possession creators on offense and a disruptive presence in the middle on defense.

Robinson hasn't yet begun to make a major impact in tilting the math. After a sluggish start to the season following the Knicks' championship run and a short summer, the new arrival has started to round into shape, though, averaging 7 points, 8 rebounds and 2.7 steals-plus-blocks over the last three games, with the C's outscoring opponents by 30 points in his 53 minutes of floor time. All told, though, the Celtics have ratcheted up their board-crashing another notch, pulling in 34% of their missed shots through 10 games, up from 31.8% last season.

They're taking 6.5 more free-throw attempts per game, too, due in large part to a more concerted effort to pressure the rim. Last season, Boston ranked dead last in the share of its field goal attempts that came within four feet of the basket. This season, that's up to 17th.

Tatum's played a big role there. Replacing Brown's high-volume attacking as the only Celtic to rank in the top 50 in the league in drives to the basket per game, he's been living at the free-throw line. His previous career high in trips to the charity stripe was 8.4 per game, accumulated when averaging 36.9 minutes a night during the 2022-23 season. Through his first nine appearances of this season, he's taking 9.3 freebies in 33.8 minutes a night — a notable uptick on a per-minute and per-possession basis, and on pace for far and away the highest free-throw attempt rate of his career. (In fact, Tatum's .477 FTA rate ranks in the top 15 in the league among all non-centers.)

Most notably, though, the C's have been significantly more disruptive on the ball. Last season, Boston ranked 28th in the NBA in steals, 25th in deflections and 23rd in opponent turnover rate, playing more conservatively on defense in the belief that if they just stayed at home, funneled the ball to the areas and shooters they preferred, and contested, the math would work out in their favor over the long run.

This season, though — owing partly to the arrival of George, who's been a demon in the passing lanes in the early going — the Celtics are tied for 14th in steals, fifth in deflections and sixth in opponent turnover rate. They're cashing in on those miscues, too, scoring 20.8 points per game off turnovers — seventh-most in the league, and a full five points per game more than last season. (It's also on pace to be more than any Celtics team has posted since Rick Pitino was on the sidelines at the Garden, regaling reporters about Larry Bird and doors.)

Add up the gains in creating cough-ups, in getting to the line and in even more aggressively crashing the offensive boards, and the Celtics are now generating 4.1 more possessions per game than their opponents — the third-highest total in the league, according to Dubin's tracking.

Thanks to Tatum's playmaking, George's shotmaking and a collective effort from a deep rotation, the Celtics have made those extra possessions count, producing a top-five offense despite shooting just 63.2% at the rim as a team (a bottom-five mark) and seeing multiple players (Tatum, Derrick White, Payton Pritchard, Baylor Scheierman, Jordan Walsh, Hugo González) struggle with frigid jumpers in the early going. If those perimeter players start to thaw out a little bit, this offense could hit a new level, and the Celtics could once again find themselves in the thick of the championship chase, even with Brown now plying his trade elsewhere in the division. The more things change, the more they stay the same.

#2832. U.S. gas prices fall below $4 for the first time since July

Americans may finally find some relief at the pump.

The national average price of regular gasoline fell below $4 a gallon for the first time since July, reaching $3.9788 on Tuesday, according to AAA. Prices are down about 17 cents from a week ago and nearly 39 cents from a month ago.

The decline comes after energy inflation slowed significantly in October. Energy prices increased 0.7% during the month, a far cry from the 5.2% surge seen in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The BLS' gasoline index increased 1% over the month, after advancing 9.1% in September.

Bespoke Investment Group pointed out that gasoline prices also tend to decline at this time of the year, making part of the recent relief seasonal.

"While lower oil prices have been a tailwind for the relief at the pump, it's also perfectly normal for gas prices to be falling at this time of year, so if they weren't, there would really be something wrong," the firm said.

The drop at the pump followed a sharp retreat in crude oil. International oil benchmark Brent crude on Tuesday briefly fell below $90 a barrel for the first time since August as comments from U.S. President Donald Trump fueled hopes for an end to Washington's nearly nine-month conflict with Iran.

Recovering oil flows from the Persian Gulf have also eased supply concerns. Regional crude exports have returned to an estimated 80% to 85% of prewar levels, including pipeline shipments, according to ING.

"Energy markets remain under pressure as hopes for a de-escalation in the Middle East reduce geopolitical risk premiums. Oil extended its losses as Gulf supply flows continued to recover, while European gas also moved lower," analysts at ING wrote.

However, the Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller warned Tuesday that energy shocks may have more persistent effects on inflation than they did before the pandemic.

"While I am pleased to see energy prices down from their high rates over the past week or so, they are still quite elevated relative to the start of the year, and the ultimate effect on inflation will depend on what levels are sustained," he said.

#2831. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2830. Jets stay quiet at the NFL trade deadline

A year after making a pair of franchise-altering deals at the NFL trade deadline, the Jets stayed quiet this year.

The only trade they made was a minor one, sending backup safety Andre Cisco to the Detroit Lions in exchange for a future sixth round pick.

At the same time, a coaching staff and possibly front office fighting for its respective jobs might not be willing to give up quality players in exchange for future Draft picks they might not be around to make.

So we ended up with a trade deadline that lacked much activity for this team.

#2829. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2828. The best revenge for Jaylen Brown is to keep doing what he’s doing

Sunday wasn't the finest outing of the 2026-27 season for the Sixers.

They struggled defensively against the one-win Bulls, but came away with a 127-121 win in Chicago, moving their record to a league-best 9-2. An afternoon day game to close out a three-game road trip against a team collecting ping-pong balls is ripe for a letdown, but the Sixers took care of business.

Jaylen Brown was strong throughout, dropping a game-high 29 points to go along with seven rebounds and four assists. The five-time All-Star's numbers have been solid through 10 games with averages of 22.3 points, 6.7 rebounds and 4.0 assists. He's shooting 47.7% from the field (the exact same percentage he shot last season) with a decent uptick from three at 39.2%.

As the Sixers get set to welcome Jayson Tatum and the Boston Celtics to South Philly on Tuesday, things remain unsettled between Brown and his former organization. When it comes to Tatum, who Brown spent nine seasons with in Boston, there is no beef, but maybe some lamb chops and gravy, as the 2024 Finals MVP put it.

With all the attention and narratives, it's hard to know what we'll see Tuesday night. Will Brown be out for revenge or does he just want to get a win for his team?

There's a way to do both — keep doing exactly what he's doing.

While the numbers have been solid, closely resembling the numbers he put up in 2023-24 when he helped the Celtics win a championship, it's been the little things with Brown.

Sunday's win was a prime example. As the plucky young Bulls kept coming, Brown provided an offensive boost, nailing a pair of threes in the fourth to thwart multiple Chicago runs. With under a minute left, he made all four of his free throws and also came away with a steal on Josh Giddey while the game remained in doubt.

Earlier last week, he made similar plays against the Pistons. While the Sixers were in control throughout the game — with Brown scoring a game-high 30 — a late comeback attempt by Cade Cunningham in Detroit was enough to make any Sixers fan nervous.

A Cunningham layup cut the lead to 106-102. After a rough offensive possession that ended in a Joel Embiid missed three, the Pistons' All-Star guard came charging up the floor. He pulled up for three in transition, but was blocked by Brown. If that shot goes in, it's suddenly a one-point game with 38 seconds left. Instead, Brown's rejection allowed the Sixers to close the game at the free throw line.

While we heard so much talk about Brown's advanced analytics and on/off splits this offseason, it's hard to quantify a guy who simply makes winning plays.

"He's a really good player," Nick Nurse said last week. "He just wins. He defends. He'll rebound. Obviously, he can score from a bunch of different levels, and I'll take all that any day. It's been phenomenal to coach him.

"What does he bring? He brings an intangible of confidence to your team. Even maybe he's not pouring in points or doing anything that you're [seeing in the box score], but there's a level of fight and competitive drive and winning spirit."

Brown has flaws like any other player. Last season, he turned the ball over more than any player in the NBA. This season, he's averaging 2.1 giveaways a game, on pace for his best season in that category since 2018-19.

There were concerns Brown wanted his own team, which supposedly led to the rift with Tatum. Thus far, there have been no issues there. In fact, Brown even openly said last week this was Tyrese Maxey's team and they're all following the second-longest-tenured Sixer's lead.

When Brown is on the floor, the Sixers' net rating is +5.4, per DataBallr. It's -3.1 when he's off. So, it's cut and dry, the Sixers are a better team when Jaylen Brown is on the court.

And just like his time with the Celtics, his current team has an excellent record.

His best path to revenge against his former team is to do what he does — win.

"Listen, the dude is tough and he wins. I'll take that all day long," Nurse said. "Makes a lot of winning plays, man. He's a winner."

#2827. 10-year Treasury yield races back to 5% as inflation data, Waller comments drive rate hike expectations

Treasury yields jumped on Tuesday after the U.S. consumer price index data for October came in hotter than expected. A mediocre auction of $42 billion in Treasury notes compounded the decline in bonds.

The yield on the 10-year Treasury note — the benchmark for mortgages, car loans and credit card rates — traded above 5% before paring gains. It was last up 13 basis points at 4.992%. The 30-year Treasury bond yield, more sensitive to geopolitical developments, rose more than 9 basis points to 5.352%. The 2-year Treasury note yield, which closely tracks short-term Fed rate expectations, surged more than 18 basis points to 4.736%.

The 10-year yield last traded above the 5% level earlier this month. In October, 10-year and 30-year yields hit 24-year highs.

One basis point equals 0.01% or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

The consumer price index for October grew 0.4%, more than the 0.3% expected from economists polled by Dow Jones and down from the 0.6% growth in September.

Core CPI, which excludes volatile food and energy prices, gained 0.3%. Economists had expected a 0.2% gain on the month. The reading was in line with September's 0.3% rise.

The report came after Fed Governor Christopher Waller said he would support "more robust action" if inflation readings are higher than expected.

"In that case, I believe a 50 basis point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting," Waller said in London at a central bank conference hosted by the Bank of England. "Hiking only 25 basis points would put our credibility at risk, a risk I am not eager to take."

Lindsay Rosner, head of multi-sector fixed income investing at Goldman Sachs Asset Management, said the hot CPI numbers signal that Fed hikes could spill into 2027.

"The Fed could push for a more-aggressive tightening cycle with CPI continuing to print strong," she said. "A December hike is our firm base case despite wider AI jitters, and stubborn inflation means we cannot rule out further hikes in 2027."

A lackluster auction of 10-year Treasurys added further downward pressure on bond prices Tuesday. "Today's 10-year auction was a bit soft," BMO Capital economists wrote. "Before the auction, Treasuries were selling off on strong volumes, and 10-year notes went into the bidding deadline 12 bp cheaper on the day. Since the results, yields have ticked slightly higher in the follow-through."

"[D]ealers were left with 10% of the auction, the most since May," said Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners. "Inflation breakevens today are focused on the drop in oil prices while regular Treasuries today are reacting to a Fed that is definitely going to hike next month, as of now, with one another in January," he said.

Oil prices dipped further Tuesday, with Brent crude falling below $90 per barrel for the first time since August. Brent futures settled above that level, however, falling 1.17% to at $91.49 a barrel. This comes on the back of President Donald Trump's comments that the war with Iran could end "fairly soon" and downplaying the prospect of further strikes in the region.

Tuesday's CPI data, together with wholesale inflation numbers due Friday, may shape the Federal Reserve's future decisions on interest rates. Markets have largely priced in a quarter percentage point hike in December, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool. Friday's stronger-than-expected U.S. employment data, more than double what economists had forecast, further strengthened those hawkish expectations.

#2826. Here's the inflation breakdown for October 2026 — in one chart

Inflation throttled back in October from its blistering pace in September amid a more moderate rise in gasoline prices, but remained high as categories like food and housing put upward pressure on consumer prices.

The consumer price index, an inflation barometer, rose 0.4% on a monthly basis in October, after adjusting for seasonal factors, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. That's down from 0.6% in September but higher than the 0.3% that economists expected.

"It's uncomfortably high," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's. "The Fed's not going to be happy about it. It certainly makes it more likely we get another rate increase in December."

The Federal Reserve uses inflation data to guide its interest rate policy. The U.S. central bank raised rates in September amid a recent pickup in inflation, which economists largely attribute to the war in Iran. It held rates steady at its last meeting in October.

Economists generally assess a measure known as "core" CPI — which strips out volatile food and energy prices — to examine underlying inflation dynamics in the U.S. economy.

That core metric rose 0.3% in October, as it also did in September and August. A consistent monthly reading around 0.2% would be more in line with the Fed's 2% annual inflation target, Zandi said.

Inflation has been above the Fed's target for more than five years.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics didn't report an annual inflation figure in the latest CPI report because of the lengthy federal government shutdown that happened a year ago. That shutdown, which ran from Oct. 1 to Nov. 12, disrupted the agency's typical data collection, and it didn't release an inflation report for October 2025.

"Investors do not need year-over-year figures to see that this was a hot inflation report and that prices are not on a sustainable path back toward the Fed's 2% target," Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth management firm, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

"It would have taken exceptional evidence of disinflation to dissuade the Fed from raising rates in December, and this report delivered the opposite," he wrote.

Economists pointed to four big reasons for the hotter-than-expected CPI reading.

Perhaps the largest contributor to the outsized inflation reading in Tuesday's CPI report was housing, Zandi said.

Housing is the largest component of CPI, and movements in the category strongly influence the index's month-to-month reading, he said.

The "shelter" CPI index rose 0.3% in October, accounting for over 30% of the CPI increase, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Zandi said he doesn't expect that dynamic to persist, however, because it's not consistent with the trajectory of market rents in the U.S.

"It does feel like we have a high level of monthly volatility" in the CPI data, he said. "It's all over the map month to month."

But economists said broader factors are putting upward pressure on prices, too.

"Core inflation today reflects three outsized shocks: tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related demand," Josh Hirt, senior U.S. economist at Vanguard Group, an asset manager, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

The Iran war has raised oil prices, pushing up prices for fuels like gasoline, diesel and jet fuel, which are refined from crude oil, economists said.

Gasoline prices rose 1% in October, a significant pullback after a 9.1% monthly increase in September, CPI data showed.

While diesel prices have retreated from recent record highs, they remain elevated and could spill more broadly into the U.S. economy in the coming months, economists said.

"Diesel prices have a really slow pass-through to all the areas of CPI, given it affects transportation costs," said Thomas Ryan, a senior North America economist at Capital Economics.

"These things take time," Ryan said. "It'll be an inflationary impetus for at least the next three to six months, I suspect."

There was some evidence of that broader percolation of diesel prices in October, such as in food prices, Zandi said. Higher diesel prices add to the cost of transporting food to the grocery store shelves, he said.

Food prices rose 0.5% in October after an unchanged reading in September, according to CPI data.

The run-up in jet fuel costs has also led airlines to raise their ticket prices, economists said. Airfares climbed 0.4% in October, after monthly increases of 1.2%, 2.7% and 3.8% in the preceding months, respectively, according to the CPI report.

Meanwhile, the inflationary impact of tariffs imposed by the Trump administration should fade by the end of the year, assuming the White House doesn't renew its push for more import taxes, Zandi said.

Certain categories of physical goods, like toys, furniture, and appliances, saw elevated inflation rates in October, likely because businesses have passed on tariff costs to consumers, he said.

The buildout of data centers that power artificial intelligence has driven up prices for electricity and consumer electronics, economists said.

Those data centers have led to a shortage of technology equipment, such as memory chips used in household electronic products like iPhones, Ryan said.

#2825. Ravens trade G Andrew Vorhees to Falcons for C Ryan Neuzil

The Baltimore Ravens are acquiring center Ryan Neuzil from the Atlanta Falcons in exchange for guard Andrew Vorhees and a 2028 sixth-round pick, per Jonathan Jones of The Athletic.

The draft pick heading to Atlanta is conditional, according to the NFL's daily personnel notice. The attached conditions remain unreported.

In Baltimore, Neuzil will reunite with Dwayne Ledford, his former offensive line coach in Atlanta who developed him from an undrafted free agent in 2022 into the Falcons' starting center by the end of the 2024 season.

#2823. Traders bet on a pop for Anthropic's historic IPO

News of Anthropic's historic initial public offering provided a wave of relief for tech investors. The response in the perpetual futures market might provide even more relief.

As Anthropic prepares to raise money in its initial public offering this month, traders in offshore markets are making large bets that there will be a big pop on the AI giant's debut.

Trying to guess an initial first day IPO reaction is nearly impossible, but a new and fast-growing corner of the markets might offer some clues.

Pre-IPO perpetuals, contracts that aim to track the price of private companies, trade on decentralized crypto exchanges like Hyperliquid, Coinbase, Binance and OKX to name a few. The current Hyperliquid Anthropic contract implies a stock price of $98 and its market cap at roughly $1.7 trillion, more than 30% higher than the $70-$78 range announced in the company's updated S1.

While trading on the exchange is not currently available to American investors, about $250 million of volume traded across Hyperliquid's two Anthropic trackers in the past 24 hours, according to the exchange.Hyperliquid's pre-IPO Anthropic perp began trading two weeks ago at $138 before settling into a roughly ten-dollar range between $110 and $120 prior to last week's selloff in tech stocks, as the contract slipped below $94 at one point.To proponents of this new asset class, the pricing of the perps is evidence that bankers intentionally undersell equity ahead of IPOs to ensure a post-market "pop." Looking at SpaceX, the argument rings true: pre-IPO perps on SpaceX traded at a premium and converged with equity prices at the IPO, all before shares went on a three-day rally of as much as 40% in the immediate aftermath of the listing.News of Anthropic's upcoming roadshow may be weighing on competitor OpenAI. Perps tracking OpenAI's market cap dropped 7% to $1.27 trillion, according to Hyperliquid trading on a contract trading $2.8 billion in volume over the past 24 hours.Shares of Hyperliquid Strategies (PURR), the publicly-traded Treasury company investing in the HYPE tokens that power the exchange fell 4% Tuesday. The stock is up 150% on the year.
